# MIN281 — Conflict Management

## Course objectives

- 1. Explain the significant place managing conflict has in the work of ministry.
- 2. Identify varying theories concerning the best approaches to managing and resolving conflict.
- 3. List and explain the various levels of conflict and how they differ from one another.
- 4. Explain how a minister can position himself or herself in a conflict for greatest congregational health.
- 5. Identify key biblical passages that give guidance to attitude and behavior in times of conflict and can aid the resolution of conflict.
- 6. Explain key factors that exist in communities and churches that give additional cause for conflict.
- 7. Identify key principles that aid leaders as they help others manage conflict.
- 8. Explain how a church’s size, community, and culture become catalysts for conflict.
- 9. List effective ground rules for conflict management.
- 10. Explain the difference between destructive and constructive conflict and how to respond when conflict reaches a destructive level.

## 1 - The Conflict Issue

### Ministry Has Changed

#### 1.1.1 — Define cultural changes and their impact on the ministry of the local church.

The twenty-first century has unique opportunities and challenges. The technology that is available today is different from what pastors had even ten years ago. Computer literacy is extremely helpful, as much of our community and the people in our churches use e-mail, websites, and other types of cyber communication. The types of songs and choruses churches sing are often foreign to a visitor, or an adjustment to those who are accustomed to songs from a hymnal. Often a pastor adjusts his or her ministry style because he or she feels another type will be more effective. We can identify many reasons why ministry is changing. Leaders need to evaluate change carefully because it always brings concern from some—whether the change is for obvious reasons or for reasons of personal preference.

One of the basic assumptions motivating the Pulpit and Pew Committee with the Duke Divinity School was that social change in the last four decades has put new pressures on the ministry, requiring an evaluation of today’s structures to see if they were still serving well. We wanted to know:

• Exactly what has changed?

• What are the pressures on today’s structures?

• How do we know the structures are not performing well?

We have seen numerous recent changes in Protestant ministry. The fifties decade was unique in that church involvement rose above levels prior to World War II and remained high until the middle of the 1960s (Wuthnow 1989, chapter 3). During those years, more people were coming to church, shopping for churches, and committing themselves to church life than had been the case for a long time. A similar growth occurred in church attendance immediately following the tragedy of September 11, 2001. However, that growth lasted only a few weeks or months. The story of the fifties and sixties was of growth, especially in the new suburbs.

The research on Protestant ministry in the 1960s was dominated by the secularization theory, which asserted that long-term secularization trends in the United States were pushing steadily onward and in the process changing the role and identity of the Protestant minister. A second assumption was that the ideal of professionalism was growing among Protestant clergy, moving ministry from an earlier self-understanding as a religious vocation based on lifelong calling, self-sacrifice, and personal holiness to a new professional ideal stressing learning, certification, and identity. (Hoge and Wenger 2005, 4)

Looking back from 2006 we can come to some conclusions. America did not become secularized as some predicted it would in the last decades of the twentieth century, and the putative advance in professionalism has not been apparent. American society is as religious and nonsecular today as it was in the 1960s, and talk of the growing professionalism of the Protestant ministry has subsided (Hoge and Wenger 2005, 4).

We can observe four main trends between today and the 1960s.

1. We have a more educated laity .

Ministers serve a more educated laity than they did a half-century ago. In the entire adult American population in the 1970s, 10.7 percent had attended four or more years of college; in 2000 it was 24.2 percent (Caplow, Hicks, and Wattenberg 2001). International travel greatly increased after the 1960s; in 1960 about 2 million Americans went abroad (not counting trips to Canada or Mexico); in 1997 it was 22 million (Caplow, Hicks, and Wattenberg 2001, 30). A more educated laity with a broader worldview have higher expectations for the performance of ministers, in other words, the quality of preaching, teaching, and understanding of leadership.

2. People have less trust in centralized authority.

Since the 1960s, numerous studies in the United States (from George Barna to the federal government) have demonstrated a decrease of trust in centralized institutions. This is true of the federal government, mass media, large corporations, national labor leaders, and professional associations. A question in numerous American surveys asked, “How much of the time do you trust the government in Washington to do the right thing?” The percentage saying “just about always” or “most of the time” dropped from 76 in 1964 to 25 in 1995. Another often-used question asked the respondent to choose between two options: “Most people can be trusted,” and “You can’t be too careful in dealing with people.” In 1964, 54 percent said that most people can be trusted, and in 1995, 35 percent did ( Washington Post, January 28, 1996). Americans today keep their trust on a more personal level and are more relational. For example, national philanthropic organizations have been forced to decentralize their actions to more local levels, and Protestant mission programs have had to build networks of local relationships rather than depend on national appeals from national offices. The downturn in trust is most significant among younger people (Putnam 2000, chapter 7).

3. There is a decreased denominational commitment.

Identification with specific Protestant denominations is lower than it was. When, for example, an Assemblies of God family moves to a different town, they will start their church shopping by visiting the Assemblies of God churches nearby; then they will branch out to other Pentecostal denominations, Charismatic churches or denominations, and ultimately make their choice based on what the church offers in programs, inspiration, like-minded people, and convenience. Typically they will say, “Denominational differences do not matter, and we felt more comfortable in that this church had more programs for our children, and the times of services fit our schedule better.”

Robert Wuthnow (1989) points out that mainline denominationalism went into decline after the 1960s because of several factors: the ecumenical movement ; attitudes of greater tolerance fostered by a growing number of Americans with higher education; the displacement of denominational seminaries by university-based religious studies departments as the arena for teaching theology; and increasing intermarriage between adherents of different faiths (15).

Central denominational structures have been gradually losing their authority as church members pay more attention to their local churches and less to the denomination they have been a part of. Also, local churches are less willing to go along with denominational directives or to automatically become involved in denominational programs in youth and children’s ministry, Sunday school programs, missions, or scouting programs. The fastest growth in Protestant churches in the last two decades has been in nondenominational churches and in those that de-emphasize their denominational ties (Miller 1997).

4. There is a lower clerical authority.

The authority and esteem that was once enjoyed by clergy in Protestant churches in America has decreased. The two common explanations for the decline in the esteem of ministers in recent decades are the loss of social functions that the clergy once held (when social work, counseling, and welfare were church functions) and the ambiguities of theological authority within the American culture. The amount of public confidence in religious leaders is measurable and has been monitored by public opinion polls. The trends of confidence in American institutions since 1973 consistently indicate that confidence in most institutions, with the exception of the military, has declined. Organized religion had enjoyed a high level of public confidence; however, its rating dropped modestly after the early 1980s. Though these polls do not show confidence specifically in clergy—as opposed to the institutional church—the trends would likely be similar. The esteem of most leaders of institutions has declined since the 1970s. Relative to the average decline, the decline for religious leaders has been smaller. That is, everybody went down, but the clergy went down less.

The styles of ministry leadership have changed. The authoritarian leadership by church leaders is less acceptable in 2006 than it was in 1960. The leveling between clergy and laity in education and training results in church members today demanding a more collaborative and less arbitrary leadership style (Carroll 2000; Carroll 1991). The level of respect given to a minister just because he or she is a minister is lower today. Pastors who are new to a community need to demonstrate their wisdom and leadership through their actions over a period of time. They no longer can expect to be accepted as community leaders automatically, just because of their profession.

The conclusion concerning the question “Is pastoring more difficult than it was four decades ago?” largely depends on the history of a particular church, a minister’s style, and the ability to handle transition effectively. However, the research shows no solid evidence that ministry is more difficult; it is just different.

Whether or not it is difficult is not a clearly evident answer, but anyone going into the ministry today should understand that ministry is different in the twenty-first century. Today’s pastors serve a more educated and cosmopolitan congregation whose denominational loyalty has diminished along with its overall trust in institutions. Understanding the times in which we live will give the twenty-first century pastor an advantage in his or her ability to manage conflict.

**Q:** How have individual levels of commitment changed over the past few decades?

**A:** People tend to be less committed to institutions and more in pursuit of value before making a commitment. People shop around more and will more easily transition to another church. People will often avoid conflict by moving to other congregations rather than remaining and resolving conflicts.

**Q:** Has secularization taken over the American church as expected?

**A:** While professionalism grows in importance, it has not overtaken the modern church as anticipated and feared. The desire for spiritual experience continues to be strong and even seems to be on the increase.

**Q:** What are the four main trends that have occurred in the past four decades?

**A:** More educated laity, Less trust in centralized authority, Decreased denominational commitment, Lower clerical authority

**Q:** What are some contributing factors in the decline in trust for centralized authority?

**A:** Government scandal and abuses, Economic downturns and the loss of growth potential, National-level scandals among religious leadership, Local church conflicts

**Q:** What are some of the factors of denominational decline over the past four decades?

**A:** The ecumenical movement, Attitudes of greater tolerance, Displacement of denominational seminaries by university-based religion departments, Intermarriage between adherents of different religious groups

**Q:** How has the style of ministry changed and affected the authority of the clergy?

**A:** Ministry has moved away from an authoritarian model to a more collaborative approach. While more reflective of a biblical model, it has altered the role once held by the local pastor. The variety and number of ministries in a community has lessened an individual minister’s influence within his or her community.

### Pastors Who Leave Local Church Ministry

#### 1.2.1 — Identify the five dominant reasons for pastors leaving local church ministry.

Hoge and Wenger’s (2005) recently released study has uncovered the major reasons why a pastor chooses to leave the pastorate, or a congregation chooses to discontinue their pastor’s tenure.

The study was not initiated due to any new crisis in numbers leaving parish ministry, and the numbers have not changed much in recent years. The best data found were with the Evangelical Lutheran Church in America, where five out of fifteen of the newly ordained in 1988 left the roster of clergy in the subsequent thirteen years. The percentage of ministers leaving local church ministry was probably higher.

Beginning in 2001, five denominations were requested to participate in this study: The Assemblies of God, Evangelical Lutheran Church in America, Lutheran Church–Missouri Synod, Presbyterian (USA), and United Methodist. The target group was defined as follows: We are interested in everyone who was ordained in the past, who served in parish ministry full-time or part-time, and within the last eight years has left parish ministry in either of two ways: (1) left parish ministry for non-parish ministries recognized by their ordinations, especially hospital chaplaincies, military chaplaincies, campus ministers, teachers, and professors; or (2) left church ministry entirely. It does not matter whether the persons, when serving in parish ministry in the past, were senior pastors or not, whether or not they define their current non-parish jobs as their “ministry,” or whether or not they have given up their ordination. It does not matter if the departure was voluntary or involuntary. It does not matter if the persons were seminary graduates. We will not study (1) persons temporarily without a job who are now actively seeking a parish ministry job, (2) persons who have retired or who have moved from full-time to part-time parish ministry, (3) persons who have switched denominations but have remained in local church ministry, and (4) persons who left the parish to take denominational jobs such as presbytery staff or district superintendent. (Hoge and Wenger 2005)

The study targeted a random sample of about two hundred from the total population of ministers who fit the target description in each denomination. Each denomination agreed to construct its own sample from available records and to mail out the questionnaires. All questionnaires were identical (with two minor differences), and all were returned to the writers of this document, not to denominational offices. No names were asked for, but on the last page respondents were asked if they would be open to a phone call later, if needed, and 79 percent answered affirmatively.

Questionnaires went out in the spring and summer of 2002. Response rates varied: Assemblies of God, 19 percent; ELCA Lutheran, 54 percent; Lutheran Church-Missouri Synod, 43 percent; Presbyterian, 38 percent, and United Methodist, 20 percent. The reason for the variation was unclear. It is believed that the low response rates introduced some bias into the study. Other respondents, perhaps the angriest and most alienated ministers, tended not to return the questionnaires. They are therefore under-represented. Another possible explanation is that those currently in specialized ministry did not think they fit the study criteria. Many of the respondents (some were quite adamant) made it clear that they had not changed careers but were still involved in pastoral ministry. The number of completed cases were these: Assemblies of God, 174; ELCA Lutheran, 291; Lutheran Church-Missouri Synod, 106; Presbyterian, 173; and United Methodist, 219.

Two other sources of data were evaluated as well. Jackson Carrol with Duke University’s Seminary did a nationwide survey of active ministers, where about fifty were asked the same questions that this study asked in its survey. Also, ninety former pastors were interviewed by phone. Of the total of ministers who returned the questionnaires the following findings were revealed.

1. Characteristics of the Former Pastors

Many of the former pastors are women. In the Methodist sample it was 29 percent; in the Presbyterian sample, 41 percent; in the ELCA sample, 17 percent and in the Assemblies of God sample, 5 percent. These figures are higher than in the active clergy of two denominations—18 percent of the active Methodist clergy are women and 18 percent of the Presbyterians—but not in the others. Twenty-two percent of the active ministers in the ELCA and 17 percent in the Assembles of God are women.

In the age and years of service, the former pastors were not much different than pastors in active service in these denominations—they are not disproportionately young persons or disproportionately second-career ministers. Also, the levels of education of the former pastors are similar to those of current pastors. One main pattern is that the Assemblies of God ministers began earlier and left earlier than the others.

The Methodists had the highest percentage of ministers serving two or more congregations, and the Assemblies of God were highest in the percentage of ministers working at other jobs in addition to ministry.

In marital status, the Methodists had the lowest percentage in their first marriage and the highest percentage remarried after divorce. The Assemblies of God had the highest percentage currently in their first marriage.

2. Why They Left Local Church Ministry

This was the heart of the inquiry, and it was approached in several ways. It was understood that any decision as life changing as a pastor’s decision to leave local church ministry was usually pushed and pulled by more than one influence. The task was to discern and convey the most important factors. One approach was to ask the respondents to tell their main feelings and motivations concerning when they decided to, or were required to, leave local church ministry. Ninety-two percent wrote in comments, and from them, it was determined statistically that there was an average of 1.9 motivations.

The most commonly mentioned motivation was “an opportunity came for new ministry.” This factor was not always the only one in pushing the decision, since a highly satisfied local church pastor may not be likely to leave even if an opportunity came for new ministry. We need to see it as often acting in combination with other factors; thus, the task of discerning its importance was in play. This top reason for leaving was a lower motivation for the Methodists and Assemblies of God, and the highest for the Missouri Synod pastors.

The second most common comment was that the denomination was not supportive, or that there was conflict with denominational officials. This was similar across denominational lines. The third most common was that the minister was burned out, discouraged, stressed, or overworked (a feeling that was expressed by ministers in all the denominations). The fourth was the needs of children and family, where this was slightly higher for the Presbyterians than for the others. The fifth most common comment concerned conflicts with church members and the sixth was doctrinal conflicts over specific issues (occurred in all denominations).

Thomas E. Trask, a former general superintendent of the General Council of the Assemblies of God, gives several suggestions on how to manage the common challenge of conflict.

People are often not willing to address conflict, but Matthew 5:23–24 says, “Therefore, if you are offering your gift at the altar and there remember that your brother has something against you, leave your gift there in front of the altar. First go and be reconciled to your brother; then come and offer your gift.” If we follow the biblical pattern, God will help us resolve conflict and restore relationships. When we do not follow the biblical pattern, we cannot expect the Lord’s help.

Second, many ministers have no conflict-management training. Conflict-management skills can be learned. That is why Bible college training is valuable. Students learn from godly instructors who have experience. Students can benefit from others and avoid ministry pitfalls.

Some people do not understand the need for conflict management training. They believe if Christians would pray more, they would have less conflict.

Prayer does aid in conflict resolution. We need to pray for the people with whom we struggle. Also, when we pray, God gives insight and solutions to conflict. James 1:5 says, “If any of you lack wisdom, he should ask God, who gives generously to all without finding fault, and it will be given to him.” We have all been in many situations where we needed an answer and prayed for God’s wisdom and help. With His help we have avoided some mistakes.

We need to depend on the Lord, but He also expects us to use common sense. Proverbs 11:14 states, “But many advisers make victory sure.” Pastors can ask for advice from experienced ministers and gain from their wisdom and insight. A new ministry group structure that is being launched in our Fellowship promotes mentoring and coaching among ministers. Many pastors have testified how this has encouraged and strengthened them.

God has also filled our churches with wise, godly people who love the ministry and their pastors. They have learned conflict-management principles in their businesses that are applicable to the church. Pastors can learn from them. These learning opportunities can serve a pastor well (Trask 2005, 18).

Developing a Model for Conflict Resolution

Richard L. Dresselhaus suggests several steps to follow for successful resolution in church conflicts:

Describe the predicament: What is the story that has been pieced together about the conflict? What are the strands and patterns of events that have converged to create the crisis?

Define the plight: Who are the persons in conflict? That is, whose self-esteem has been significantly threatened to move him or her from stress to distress?

Identify the coulds : What are the could haves and the if maybes that may have prevented the escalation of events to the point of conflict?

List the cans : What needs to be done to move those in conflict to a point of resolution? Make an exhaustive list of the options.

Evaluate the shoulds : What are the pros and cons of each option? What is the anticipated conclusion of each option?

Commit to the will: What is the option all parties are prepared to accept?

Intentionality in each step will yield the most positive results. It is the road map and strategy that will lead to positive conflict resolution (Dresselhaus 2005, 34).

**Q:** What are the five dominant reasons pastors leave local church ministry?

**A:** (1) Opportunity for new ministry; (2) Lack of denominational support; (3) Discouragement, stress or burnout; (4) Needs of family; (5) Conflict

**Q:** What have been some of the responses to the challenges of conflict?

**A:** Prioritizing prayer; Conflict avoidance; Training for managing conflict; Coaching relationships with experienced ministers

**Q:** What are the six steps in Richard Dresselhaus’s model for conflict resolution?

**A:** Describe the predicament; Define the plight; Identify the coulds ; List the cans ; Evaluate the shoulds ; Commit to the “will”

**Q:** What are the coulds in Dresselhaus’s approach?

**A:** The coulds are those things that could have or might have been done to avoid the conflict. The coulds may have avoided the escalation of the conflict to its current level.

**Q:** How does one evaluate the shoulds ?

**A:** Define the pros and cons of each potential solution. Define the anticipated conclusion of each possible solution.

**Q:** What assumptions did the Duke study make in their research? Do you agree with those assumptions? Explain.

**A:** The conditions of ministry have changed dramatically in the last thirty to forty years. Too many local church ministers have left the ministry. The second question will be a personal response.

#### 1.2.2 — Describe Dresselhaus’s model for conflict resolution.

Each denomination agreed to construct its own sample from available records and to mail out the questionnaires. All questionnaires were identical (with two minor differences), and all were returned to the writers of this document, not to denominational offices. No names were asked for, but on the last page respondents were asked if they would be open to a phone call later, if needed, and 79 percent answered affirmatively.

Questionnaires went out in the spring and summer of 2002. Response rates varied: Assemblies of God, 19 percent; ELCA Lutheran, 54 percent; Lutheran Church-Missouri Synod, 43 percent; Presbyterian, 38 percent, and United Methodist, 20 percent. The reason for the variation was unclear. It is believed that the low response rates introduced some bias into the study. Other respondents, perhaps the angriest and most alienated ministers, tended not to return the questionnaires. They are therefore under-represented. Another possible explanation is that those currently in specialized ministry did not think they fit the study criteria. Many of the respondents (some were quite adamant) made it clear that they had not changed careers but were still involved in pastoral ministry. The number of completed cases were these: Assemblies of God, 174; ELCA Lutheran, 291; Lutheran Church-Missouri Synod, 106; Presbyterian, 173; and United Methodist, 219.

Two other sources of data were evaluated as well. Jackson Carrol with Duke University’s Seminary did a nationwide survey of active ministers, where about fifty were asked the same questions that this study asked in its survey. Also, ninety former pastors were interviewed by phone. Of the total of ministers who returned the questionnaires the following findings were revealed.

1. Characteristics of the Former Pastors

Many of the former pastors are women. In the Methodist sample it was 29 percent; in the Presbyterian sample, 41 percent; in the ELCA sample, 17 percent and in the Assemblies of God sample, 5 percent. These figures are higher than in the active clergy of two denominations—18 percent of the active Methodist clergy are women and 18 percent of the Presbyterians—but not in the others. Twenty-two percent of the active ministers in the ELCA and 17 percent in the Assembles of God are women.

In the age and years of service, the former pastors were not much different than pastors in active service in these denominations—they are not disproportionately young persons or disproportionately second-career ministers. Also, the levels of education of the former pastors are similar to those of current pastors. One main pattern is that the Assemblies of God ministers began earlier and left earlier than the others.

The Methodists had the highest percentage of ministers serving two or more congregations, and the Assemblies of God were highest in the percentage of ministers working at other jobs in addition to ministry.

In marital status, the Methodists had the lowest percentage in their first marriage and the highest percentage remarried after divorce. The Assemblies of God had the highest percentage currently in their first marriage.

2. Why They Left Local Church Ministry

This was the heart of the inquiry, and it was approached in several ways. It was understood that any decision as life changing as a pastor’s decision to leave local church ministry was usually pushed and pulled by more than one influence. The task was to discern and convey the most important factors. One approach was to ask the respondents to tell their main feelings and motivations concerning when they decided to, or were required to, leave local church ministry. Ninety-two percent wrote in comments, and from them, it was determined statistically that there was an average of 1.9 motivations.

The most commonly mentioned motivation was “an opportunity came for new ministry.” This factor was not always the only one in pushing the decision, since a highly satisfied local church pastor may not be likely to leave even if an opportunity came for new ministry. We need to see it as often acting in combination with other factors; thus, the task of discerning its importance was in play. This top reason for leaving was a lower motivation for the Methodists and Assemblies of God, and the highest for the Missouri Synod pastors.

The second most common comment was that the denomination was not supportive, or that there was conflict with denominational officials. This was similar across denominational lines. The third most common was that the minister was burned out, discouraged, stressed, or overworked (a feeling that was expressed by ministers in all the denominations). The fourth was the needs of children and family, where this was slightly higher for the Presbyterians than for the others. The fifth most common comment concerned conflicts with church members and the sixth was doctrinal conflicts over specific issues (occurred in all denominations).

Thomas E. Trask, a former general superintendent of the General Council of the Assemblies of God, gives several suggestions on how to manage the common challenge of conflict.

People are often not willing to address conflict, but Matthew 5:23–24 says, “Therefore, if you are offering your gift at the altar and there remember that your brother has something against you, leave your gift there in front of the altar. First go and be reconciled to your brother; then come and offer your gift.” If we follow the biblical pattern, God will help us resolve conflict and restore relationships. When we do not follow the biblical pattern, we cannot expect the Lord’s help.

Second, many ministers have no conflict-management training. Conflict-management skills can be learned. That is why Bible college training is valuable. Students learn from godly instructors who have experience. Students can benefit from others and avoid ministry pitfalls.

Some people do not understand the need for conflict management training. They believe if Christians would pray more, they would have less conflict.

Prayer does aid in conflict resolution. We need to pray for the people with whom we struggle. Also, when we pray, God gives insight and solutions to conflict. James 1:5 says, “If any of you lack wisdom, he should ask God, who gives generously to all without finding fault, and it will be given to him.” We have all been in many situations where we needed an answer and prayed for God’s wisdom and help. With His help we have avoided some mistakes.

We need to depend on the Lord, but He also expects us to use common sense. Proverbs 11:14 states, “But many advisers make victory sure.” Pastors can ask for advice from experienced ministers and gain from their wisdom and insight. A new ministry group structure that is being launched in our Fellowship promotes mentoring and coaching among ministers. Many pastors have testified how this has encouraged and strengthened them.

God has also filled our churches with wise, godly people who love the ministry and their pastors. They have learned conflict-management principles in their businesses that are applicable to the church. Pastors can learn from them. These learning opportunities can serve a pastor well (Trask 2005, 18).

Developing a Model for Conflict Resolution

Richard L. Dresselhaus suggests several steps to follow for successful resolution in church conflicts:

Describe the predicament: What is the story that has been pieced together about the conflict? What are the strands and patterns of events that have converged to create the crisis?

Define the plight: Who are the persons in conflict? That is, whose self-esteem has been significantly threatened to move him or her from stress to distress?

Identify the coulds : What are the could haves and the if maybes that may have prevented the escalation of events to the point of conflict?

List the cans : What needs to be done to move those in conflict to a point of resolution? Make an exhaustive list of the options.

Evaluate the shoulds : What are the pros and cons of each option? What is the anticipated conclusion of each option?

Commit to the will: What is the option all parties are prepared to accept?

Intentionality in each step will yield the most positive results. It is the road map and strategy that will lead to positive conflict resolution (Dresselhaus 2005, 34).

**Q:** What are the five dominant reasons pastors leave local church ministry?

**A:** (1) Opportunity for new ministry; (2) Lack of denominational support; (3) Discouragement, stress or burnout; (4) Needs of family; (5) Conflict

**Q:** What have been some of the responses to the challenges of conflict?

**A:** Prioritizing prayer; Conflict avoidance; Training for managing conflict; Coaching relationships with experienced ministers

**Q:** What are the six steps in Richard Dresselhaus’s model for conflict resolution?

**A:** Describe the predicament; Define the plight; Identify the coulds ; List the cans ; Evaluate the shoulds ; Commit to the “will”

**Q:** What are the coulds in Dresselhaus’s approach?

**A:** The coulds are those things that could have or might have been done to avoid the conflict. The coulds may have avoided the escalation of the conflict to its current level.

**Q:** How does one evaluate the shoulds ?

**A:** Define the pros and cons of each potential solution. Define the anticipated conclusion of each possible solution.

**Q:** What assumptions did the Duke study make in their research? Do you agree with those assumptions? Explain.

**A:** The conditions of ministry have changed dramatically in the last thirty to forty years. Too many local church ministers have left the ministry. The second question will be a personal response.

### Conflict—The Number One Reason Pastors Quit

#### 1.3.1 — Explain the essential nature and definition of conflict.

Undoubtedly, pastors feel many pressures with their occupation. Colleges, universities, and seminaries are now offering courses in practical areas such as conflict management, as well as training in Bible and theology. With many new books and seminars being offered, it is evident that “survival skills in ministry” is a necessary part of the training.

The Pulpit and Pew Project (in which I participated) at Duke University Divinity School analyzed the reasons why men and women left local church ministry. Our Duke research team did not assume that life for local church ministers is harder today than it was in the past, and it did not assume that conditions are inherently unfair to ministers. But it did make two assumptions: that conditions of ministry have changed in the last three or four decades, and that too many local church ministers leave (Hoge and Wenger 2005, xi). Among the denominations studied was the Assemblies of God.

Why did ministers in the various denominations leave? Three summary statements are supported by the research:

1. Conflict was the main reason ministers left—conflict with parishioners, with other staff members, or with denominational officials. Many ministers felt blocked or frustrated in their efforts to bring new life to their congregations, and this led to disillusionment with their members and with their denominations.

2. Women left local church ministry more often than men. Many times, this was due to family responsibilities, especially the needs of their children. Women also left because of disillusionment with denominational systems and continuing resistance to women clergy in some local churches and among some district superintendents.

3. More clergy left due to institutional or interpersonal problems than due to loss of faith or financial need. The greatest interpersonal problems were feelings of loneliness, isolation, and inadequate boundaries between ministry and family life (Hoge and Wenger 2005, 29).

Below is a report on the main findings of the Pulpit and Pew study:

1. Pastors who left local church ministry did so for many reasons. The seven most common were

Preference for another kind of ministry

Need to care for children or family

Conflict in the congregation

Conflict with denominational leaders

Feelings of burnout or frustration

Sexual misconduct

Divorce or marital problems

2. About 15 to 25 percent left voluntarily, 30 to 40 percent were forced out, and the remaining cases are the result of a combination of motives.

3. Preference for specialized ministry and frustrations from dealing with conflict were the most common reasons for leaving parish ministry.

4. Institutional and interpersonal problems were more significant factors than loss of faith, health problems, or financial difficulties (Hoge and Wenger 2005, 49).

Though all these concerns are important, the purpose of this book is to help ministers understand the main reasons many of them choose to leave their occupation / calling. In doing so, it is our hope that many will learn skills and have a better understanding of:

• The nature of conflict: Where does conflict come from and what increases or diminishes it?

• How to handle conflict: protocols, sensitivities, and timing are all involved

• How to prevent conflict from overwhelming us: All ministers (and their spouses) face it and the pain it causes can be devastating

Webster’s dictionary gives these definitions of conflict : “to strike, to be antagonistic, incompatible, and so forth; 1. a fight or war; 2. sharp disagreement, as of interests or ideas; 3. emotional disturbance” ( Webster’s New World Dictionary, 1989, under the word “conflict”). Other definitions of conflict include “a problem that interferes with the accomplishment of purposes” (Bolman and Deal 1997, 172) and “the active striving for one’s own preferred outcome which, if attained, precludes the attainment by others of their own preferred outcome, thereby producing hostility” (Likert and Likert 1976).

The Bible questions where conflicts come from and gives us the answer: “What causes fights and quarrels among you? . . . your desires that battle within you” (James 4:1).

There is always something within that needs to be understood by all the people involved in the conflict. When we understand where the conflict comes from, it will give us wisdom to manage and bring solutions to the conflict.

The desires , styles, personalities, and history that different people have can give us information on how to deal with the issue at hand and can help us sense the timing for involvement. Conflict basically comes because of:

• Controversies

• Various interests

• Difference of style

Not all conflict is bad, and, in fact, it can sometimes bring about good if handled appropriately. The apostles Paul and Barnabas had a disagreement, but they did not become bitter or remain angry at one another, and in fact God blessed both of their plans. “Barnabas wanted to take John, also called Mark, with them, but Paul did not think it wise to take him, because he had deserted them in Pamphylia and had not continued with them in the work. They had such a sharp disagreement that they parted company” (Acts 15:37–39).

We must recognize that Paul and Barnabas disagreed, separated, and continued the work of God. They did not become bitter or hostile with one another.

In so many cases of conflict, people become angry, then bitter, and then begin to strike out with words, accusations, slander, and even physical means in order to bring injury. This is never God’s will. Conflict is normal, and there are appropriate and inappropriate ways to handle it.

Understanding that there are different phases of conflict and knowing which phase you are in has great value.

Phase one conflicts are the normal conflicts that happen every day. Someone steps in front of you in line at the supermarket. A driver pulls into your lane, and you need to slam on the brakes in order not to hit him or her. Your assistant does not follow through on something that you needed to have finished that day.

These kinds of conflict require little action. We normally deal with these types of issues by choosing not to become angry because it is not worth the energy needed to deal with it. Many phase one conflicts come because people are unpredictable. We simply cannot control the people and situations around us.

We can deal with phase one conflict by either avoiding the person or conflict or by obliging .

Avoidance passes things off rather than deals with them. The feeling may be that in some cases it is better to be silent than to discuss. We simply do not have the motivation (not worth the discussion) to deal with the idiosyncrasies at hand.

Obliging is a deeper level of avoidance that tends to give in to the other person’s opinion in order to keep the process moving along. It is difficult to anticipate or predict when another person will stop being willing to oblige. When they or we do, there will be a conflict situation.

The ways we can choose to handle phase one conflicts are:

• We can evaluate both sides of the argument and get a good understanding of why there is a misunderstanding or disagreement.

• We should ask ourselves, or the people involved, if the reaction to the issue is proportionate to the situation.

• We should identify the points of agreement first and then look at the points of disagreement.

Phase one conflict happens daily. Every church leader—from those working in the nursery to those working with senior citizens—must deal quickly with this level of conflict whenever people are involved in any kind of confrontation or program.

Phase two conflict goes to a deeper level and becomes a greater challenge. At phase two people become competitive and develop a win-lose attitude. Both sides believe they are right, even though one is probably wrong. The people begin to tell their side to their friends and alliances, and cliques begin to form in support of each. Just deciding to get over the conflict (like someone cutting in front of you at the grocery store) is no longer the way it can be handled.

The emotional level of phase two is often called the problem, and people will begin to verbalize by using generalizations like, “they always . . .: and “he never…” Gossip, sarcasm, and misinformation become typical ways to characterize the other person and his or her opinion.

Phase two requires a new way of management and, hopefully, solution. As leaders, we need to separate the person from the problem and the person from the opinion. Try to remove your emotions and feelings from the conflict. It is necessary to create a safe atmosphere where the issues can be discussed. A place that is informal (a restaurant, a walk in the park, a casual conversation in the parking lot) is often less threatening and / or challenging. A neutral setting is needed (not in one person’s home, office, or classroom).

We need to decide on the agenda for the discussion before we talk to the parties, and we must stay on the agenda. Both sides will likely try to get us involved in side issues or try to get us to understand their emotions by saying, “I just feel . . .” or “In my spirit I just cannot . . .”

As a leader, stay in control and set the tone of the discussion. Not raising your voice, not using labels, and not being biased to one side are all part of being in control. Stay with the facts and be gentle with the people involved.

Trying to find a win-win is the goal. Your challenge is finding middle ground and getting to a resolution. Forcing the issues will often backfire and cause further problems. The only time we should force an issue is when it is a solid biblical fact or biblical conviction that has no other solution.

Phase three takes on a new life of its own. This can be defined as the battle stage. There probably is very little that will trouble us as leaders as much as phase three conflict. When this type of conflict occurs, we do not forget the experience because it is often mean, unpredictable, and emotionally damaging.

This type of conflict shifts from just wanting something to trying to hurt the other person, emotionally, professionally, psychologically, or possibly physically. Most of our newspapers mention this type of conflict on the first page. Police tell me that domestic violence is the most dangerous kind of call for assistance, because people are often hurt emotionally or physically and want to hurt anyone who intervenes.

In the church, people will hurt each other with words, false accusations, slander, and lies. They want the other person to lose in a big way.

The emphasis of the argument is “I am right; they are wrong.” There is no middle ground: anger, possibly hatred, has become part of the emotional baggage. Each of the parties has people organized and has identified who is on each side. When Christians are involved they can say, “they have all compromised . . .” or “we need to split this church and form . . .” These people just want to be right, and they do not care who gets hurt (even their children) in the process.

A leader or small group of spokespeople will surface, and there will be a loss of perspective about the nature of the real disagreement. Common sense, willingness to negotiate, or openness to any kind of compromise is long gone.

Phase three often takes a third, outside, person to intervene, bring arbitration and / or mediate the situation. As a leader, you could be the third person; however, you must use caution as one or both of the sides could label you.

The way to intervene in this phase of conflict is to be diligent to the details of the real issues. Do not let the emotions of the participants sidetrack you. Understand that this phase often takes a great deal of time and will involve many meetings. You will need to understand fully what individuals are part of each side and will need to determine which people are in phase one, two and three. It can be complicated to separate the emotions and opinions from the truth; however, the goal is to redirect the combatants’ energies to possible solutions.

You might think, “Phase three should not happen in church or among Christians.” You are right; however, it does. Just a quick reading of the books of Corinthians and most of the writings of Paul will point out that phase three is relatively common in churches. Learning how to identify which phase the conflict is in and changing our strategy as we deal with each different level is valuable for those of us who are called to be leaders.

**Q:** What are some of the key areas of conflict that have led to pastors leaving the ministry?

**A:** Conflict with parishioners; Conflict with staff members; Conflict with denominational leadership; Conflict concerning gender acceptance; Other interpersonal conflict

**Q:** How does James explain the source of conflict (4:1)?

**A:** He indicates that conflicts are the result of “your desires that battle within you.”

**Q:** Explain phase one conflicts and how to respond.

**A:** Phase one conflicts are normal, daily conflicts that require little response. The choice to avoid anger and get over such conflicts is usually the best approach.

**Q:** Explain phase two conflicts and how to respond.

**A:** Phase two conflicts reach a deeper level that leads individuals to seek a win-lose solution. To resolve we must separate the person from the problem. Seek a private place to work toward resolution. Establish a clear sense of agenda or direction for the meeting. The leader should take the initiative and set the tone for discussion. Seeking a win-win solution will bring the best result.

**Q:** Explain phase three conflicts and how to respond to them.

**A:** Phase three conflicts move beyond the issue to take on a life of their own. To resolve one must typically seek the involvement of a third party as arbitrator or mediator. One must avoid generalizing statements that hinder progress. The greater good becomes the primary motive for resolution rather than personal benefit.

**Q:** Explain phase two conflicts and how to respond.

**A:** Phase two conflicts reach a deeper level that leads individuals to seek a win-lose solution. To resolve we must separate the person from the problem. Seek a private place to work toward resolution. Establish a clear sense of agenda or direction for the meeting. The leader should take the initiative and set the tone for discussion. Seeking a win-win solution will bring the best result.

**Q:** Explain phase three conflicts and how to respond to them.

**A:** Phase three conflicts move beyond the issue to take on a life of their own. To resolve one must typically seek the involvement of a third party as arbitrator or mediator. One must avoid generalizing statements that hinder progress. The greater good becomes the primary motive for resolution rather than personal benefit.

### The Basics of Conflict Management

#### 1.4.1 — Identify Paul’s three steps for conflict management given to Titus (3:10–11).

“Warn a divisive person once, and then warn him a second time. After that, have nothing to do with him. You may be sure that such a man is warped and sinful; he is self-condemned” (Titus 3:10–11).

Understanding tactful ways to deal with church conflict and having a sense of timing (when and how to get involved) is as basic as Preaching 101 for those entering the ministry. Healthy conflict happens in all relationships; however, divisive people are to be evaluated and dealt with quickly, diplomatically, and effectively.

Paul’s admonition to Titus concerned those who were teaching false doctrine, giving misinformation, and were causing confusion in the church body. He was telling his mentee: “Titus, the first thing you need to do is talk to them. Tell them that what they are saying is inaccurate and is causing division and confusion. Ask them to discontinue this kind of behavior.”

These were Paul’s instructions if they did not listen: “Titus, the second thing you need to do is go back to them, quickly. Do not let it go on, but as a leader you will need to deal with the inaccuracies and inform the persons that if they will not stop gossiping, slandering, or teaching unbiblical doctrines, they will be removed from membership. Explain that you do not want to do this; however, for the sake of the body of Christ it will be necessary.”

Paul was saying this regarding their unrepentance: “Titus, you will need to follow through and remove them from membership.”

This might seem harsh to some; however, the Bible is full of types of conflict examples. Anyone entering ministry today will need to understand that dealing with conflict is part and parcel of what we need to do. It might not happen often or it could happen frequently, depending on the church, the people, our personalities and / or outside influences.

Antagonists in the church are what I believe Paul is talking about. Understand the difference between healthy church conflict and antagonists who could be part of your congregation. Antagonism should not be confused with mere criticism or healthy conflict in the church. Antagonism is unhealthy conflict (Haugk 1988, 21).

The person or people who are involved in antagonism can be defined as “individuals who on the basis of non-substantive evidence, go out of their way to make insatiable demands, usually attacking the person or performance of others. These attacks are selfish in nature, tearing down rather than building up, and are frequently directed against those in a leadership capacity” (Haugk 1988, back cover).

Whom does the antagonist affect? Antagonists affect the whole church body to some degree, depending on how much influence they have, their history, the way in which they are communicating, and how vocal they are with their antagonistic opinions. The church is a body (1 Corinthians 12), and all of its members affect one another. Some members of the body have influence or some type of obvious importance. Other members have importance, but not as much influence. However, all the members are important and necessary.

The antagonist, or divisive person, is someone who has decided to bring unjust criticism on church leadership or on a particular ministry program that is being developed to assist the body in its growth. He or she does not want to help or understand the rationale; antagonists just want their way. If they do not get their way, they will take it to another level by approaching others in the body. They will bear misinformation and seek out people who will take their side. The person without much influence has now gained more influence, and these kinds of people will endeavor to get more until they have destroyed everyone around them and / or have won their battle.

This antagonism is dangerous. Ken Haugk (1988) writes in Antagonists in the Church :

Antagonism is like a virulent disease in that body. A body cannot regard attack on a single part as an inconsequential threat requiring no response by other parts. Antagonism poses a threat not only to an isolated organ, but also to the entire organism, which suffers until the disease is overcome. The whole body must work to overcome it. (23–24)

Thousands of pastors around the world have utilized an effective discipleship tool called The Purpose Driven Church . Rick Warren developed this program for his congregation, and this discipleship program has been a valuable tool for assisting Christians in discovering their unique gifts and the value and purpose God has for their lives. The Assemblies of God Fellowship has been able to work with this tool and adapt it to our Pentecostal doctrine and style of worship. We recommended this tool as a way for our churches to be intentional about discipleship.

I use this tool and have seen hundreds of people become greatly encouraged in their faith, become leaders, become small group facilitators, and become more confident in their God-given gifts.

However, when I brought this program to the church I was pastoring, I experienced a degree of conflict that I did not anticipate. Following is my story, and how I chose to deal with it:

Pastor Wayde Goodall hoped God would do great things in the First Assembly of God church of Winston-Salem, N.C., during 40 Days of Purpose. He wasn’t disappointed. The church started 80 new small groups during the six-week campaign, while care groups grew from 15 to 100. Attendance swelled with over 500 new people becoming a part of the church with many making first-time commitments to Christ. Most important, the transition to the disciple-making, purpose driven model the church’s leadership started 18 months earlier took hold, permanently changing the church at its most fundamental level.

Yet the joy over all God had done was soon tempered by another growing movement within the church: conflict. 40 Days of Purpose was the final straw for a small but vocal group within the church who didn’t care for the new vision and direction the church had taken. When Pastor Goodall came three years earlier, he planned on implementing the purpose-driven model for ministry within the church. “As I look back, I didn’t see any tension or crisis brewing,” Wayde says, “I probably should have looked at this, because any time you create change, you have conflict.”

The gossip and divisiveness stayed under the radar until 40 Days of Purpose caused it to burst to the surface. A small but vocal group of people began stirring up dissension by saying things like, “Why are we doing this 40-Day thing? It comes from a Baptist!” They also criticized Wayde for preaching Rick Warren’s sermons that come with the 40-Day campaign. “Why doesn’t the Holy Spirit lead him to write his own sermons?” they would ask. The attacks also repeated material found on numerous websites critical of The Purpose Driven Life and the Purpose Driven movement. With time the criticism moved from general disagreement with the church’s overall direction to personal attacks against Wayde himself. For nearly a year conflict ensnared the church, ultimately resulting in 150 people leaving. When they left they took nearly $100,000 with them.

Wayde survived the attacks without abandoning the vision he believed God gave him for the church. Along the way he discovered some basic principles that can help any pastor walk down the difficult path of conflict.

1. Do not get defensive

When attacks begin, most of us feel the need to fight back. Wayde refused to give in to this inclination and instead focused upon doing the right things. “As I went to the Lord with all this, I just felt like he was saying, ‘Don’t defend yourself. I will vindicate you,’” Wayde says. “He told me to remain loving, preach great messages, and keep my focus. I wasn’t to focus on the small group, but on the crowd of hungry people.”

2. Make sure the leadership is on board with you

No pastor should ever try to face conflict alone. Wayde immediately went to his board and told them, “I need you to get shoulder to shoulder with me. This small group of people needs to understand there are no weak links on the board of elders. We’re in this thing together, we’ve studied this, and we believe this is right for the church.”

3. Be completely transparent with your board

When attacks come against a pastor, they always strike at his integrity. Charges do not have to be true to be effective. Even the appearance of impropriety can cause damage. To combat this, Wayde made sure the members of the church’s board knew all the details of the conflict along the way. He did not hide anything. In fact, he made sure one or two members of the board were present when a disgruntled church member came to him with complaints.

4. Involve the leadership team in confronting divisive people and restoring them to fellowship with Christ and the church

Titus 3:10 says, “Warn a divisive person once, and then warn him a second time. After that, have nothing to do with him.” Rather than do this on his own, Wayde made this a function of the leadership team. Together, they developed a process for both confronting divisive people and for restoring them into the fellowship.

5. Focus on the Kingdom, not yourself

Although attacks against you feel very personal, they cannot be taken that way. Your focus must stay fixed on God’s kingdom. Wayde puts it this way, “My focus cannot be my future, my security, whether or not I’m going to be able to save money for my kid’s education, although those things are all important to me. My focus has to be that I have a divine call to serve God’s kingdom and I have to do the right thing for the sake of this church.”

6. Stay the course

Conflict always lasts longer than you thought possible. “As we were going through this, I wondered where the bottom was,” Wayde says. He soon discovered it was much deeper than he imagined. Conflict enveloped the church for 8 to 10 months. Even today, many months later, the people who left continue to nip at the church. To prevail, you must persevere. Too many pastors give up too soon, and a pattern of conflict imprints itself on the church for years to come.

Wayde is thankful he did not give up in the middle of those days when he felt his strength was being tested. Today the church is stronger and healthier than ever in its history. In the end, the pain of enduring unjust attacks and constant conflict was worth it.

My experience certainly is not uncommon. This example demonstrates a way conflict can come to a church. As a pastor or church leader, it is our responsibility to deal with conflict with the help of God’s wisdom. But deal with it we must. The body of believers that you serve needs leaders who will do the right thing with tender hearts, but be unafraid of those who would bully and bring harm.

**Q:** What are the three steps Paul gives to Titus when dealing with people in conflict?

**A:** Talk to them, explaining the unacceptable nature of their behavior. Go to them quickly when you discover they have rejected previous counsel. Remove them from membership.

**Q:** What is an antagonist, and how does he or she differ from those who experience healthy conflict?

**A:** An antagonist is one who pursues conflict, usually attacking others for selfish reasons. He or she consistently tears down rather than builds up. An antagonist is one who has decided to attack others, often church leaders or ministry efforts, with little genuine interest in benefiting the body.

**Q:** How does an antagonist’s level of influence affect a pastor’s approach to dealing with the individual?

**A:** An antagonist with a high level of influence must be dealt with more quickly and completely. Often a pastor will avoid such efforts of resolution because of the individual’s level of influence, but this can be catastrophic. An antagonist with a low level of influence can often be dealt with by those with greater influence.

**Q:** What are the six principles for addressing conflict situations?

**A:** Do not get defensive. Make sure the leadership is on board with you. Be completely transparent with your board. Involve the leadership team in confronting and seeking to restore divisive people. Focus on the Kingdom and not on yourself. Stay the course.

**Q:** Why should the pastor involve the church board or other leadership entities in such situations?

**A:** The additional involvement may provide greater perspective. The additional involvement can protect the pastor from being attacked individually. The additional involvement can steer the pastor from self-preserving motives. The additional involvement carries the larger voice of the congregation.

**Q:** What are the advantages of establishing a consistent approach to conflict management?

**A:** Other leaders will know what to expect in such situations. Consistent approaches provide training for other leaders in facing their own conflict situations. Consistent approaches strengthen individual responses. The offending individual then has no claim to having been treated differently or more harshly than others.

**Q:** List at least three Scripture passages that offer guidance for behavior in the midst of conflict.

**A:** Proverbs 15:1; Proverbs 16:7; and Proverbs 25:15
