### Conflict, the Bible, and You

#### 1.5.1 — Identify and explain scriptural guidelines for behavior in the midst of conflict.

The Bible is filled with examples of conflict and directions for us as we bring resolution to these challenging occurrences. We need to look at the Bible as a manual for handling conflict in the church. The New Testament letters were largely written to groups of Christians facing problems within their church family. The instructions by the various writers are timeless. Conflict among God’s people is as old as the book of Genesis and the other books that Moses wrote. He dealt with conflict—seemingly endlessly―and understood it to be a part of what God called him to do. Moses wrote: “Do not hate your brother in your heart. Rebuke your neighbor frankly so you will not share in his guilt” (Leviticus 19:17).

The Scripture speaks to us about:

• Disputes with another believer

If your brother sins against you, go and show him his fault, just between the two of you. If he listens to you, you have won your brother over. But if he will not listen, take one or two others along, so that every matter may be established by the testimony of two or three witnesses. If he refuses to listen to them, tell it to the church; and if he refuses to listen even to the church, treat him as you would a pagan or a tax collector. (Matthew 18:15–17)

Jesus sets forth the method of restoring or disciplining a professing Christian who sins against another member of the church in a private manner. To neglect Christ’s instruction will bring spiritual compromise and ultimate destruction to the church as a holy people of God (1 Peter 2:9) (Stamps 1992, 1443).

• When a believer is living in sin

“Brothers, if someone is caught in a sin, you who are spiritual should restore him gently. But watch yourself, or you also may be tempted” (Galatians 6:1).

Paul’s reason for giving us this instruction is to encourage believers to take responsibility for each other and to help the person who is sinning or causing a problem in the church. The goal is to help the person understand the seriousness of their conversations or behavior and hopefully motivate them to get right with the Lord.

“You who are spiritual” refers to those who have enough spiritual discernment to understand the problem and enough spiritual insight to realize that they could fall into sin. They know themselves and have enough spiritual maturity to confront the person with godly compassion.

Human nature causes some people to act or lead in controlling ways because previous leadership (or you) have been afraid to speak to them or confront them. In the Lord’s church, it is totally acceptable and responsible for pastors to expose people’s political games and agendas as they seek to win a church battle. As pastors, we are to go to them or ask another leader to go to them and say, “I see you are lining up a coalition and I understand why. But could we work on the problem together, rather than taking sides?”

When we need to confront, it is helpful to keep the focus on the future of the church with comments like this one:

“I do not think you desire to injure this church; I want to help you keep this from bringing future harm or distracting us from reaching people for Christ.”

Ask for permission to speak to the issue:

“May I share with you something that I feel is very important and crucial to your well being (spiritual growth, personal health, family health)?”

Include their strengths in your conversation:

“Your discipline (attitude, involvement, ability to understand, and so forth) will give you a great foundation so you can make some changes that will help you and others.” (See Philippians 1:6.)

Give them hope with words like these:

“Please understand that the Holy Spirit will help you. He is your constant companion and will give you wisdom. The body of Christ will help you and I will do everything I can to assist you. For everyone, the Christian life is all about growing through difficult times.” (See Romans 5:1–5.)

Give them words of mercy:

“I understand what it is like to grow, to change, and to learn obedience. I have walked through those times too. The Bible gives us great encouragement in examples from Peter, David, Moses, and other leaders who needed to do this.” (See 1 Timothy 1:2–3; Ephesians 4:31–5:2.)

Not All Conflict Is Sin

When we are involved in a church conflict:

1. Understand the nature of the conflict situation and the objectives, motives, and feelings of those involved.

2. Review what the Scripture teaches (or in principle demonstrates) concerning the causes of and solutions for conflict.

3. Make specific suggestions that are practical and will restore peace and healthy, constructive solutions.

4. Encourage healthy relationships, through forgiveness, correct information (versus misinformation or not enough information), and putting disputes behind us.

Guidelines for interpersonal behavior:

• To be in control of what we say

“A man who lacks judgment derides his neighbor, but a man of understanding holds his tongue” (Proverbs 11:12; see James 3:1–10).

• To respond to anger with a gentle answer

“A gentle answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger” (Proverbs 15:1).

• To live in ways that please God

“When a man’s ways are pleasing to the Lord, he makes even his enemies live at peace with him” (Proverbs 16:7).

• To develop patience

“Through patience a ruler can be persuaded, and a gentle tongue can break a bone” (Proverbs 25:15).

• To fight inner conflict

This is an example of inner conflict with Paul. He is torn between doing good and giving in to temptation. These struggles can be met and overcome through the power of Jesus Christ.

“I do not understand what I do. For what I want to do I do not do, but what I hate I do. . . What a wretched man I am! Who will rescue me from this body of death? Thanks be to God—through Jesus Christ our Lord!” (Romans 7:15–24).

Often used passages that address church conflict:

• When another Christian is in error, sin, or is guilty of ethical misbehavior (Matthew 18:15–17)

• The church is to enjoy an atmosphere of peace so that God can freely move (Romans 12:9–21).

• Believers are to walk in love (Ephesians 4:25–5:2).

• Christians are to restore one another in love (Galatians 6:1).

• Christians are to love—with a godly love (1 Corinthians 13).

• Believers are to be patient with one another (Colossians 3:12–17).

• Leaders are to lead with a gentle attitude (2 Timothy 2:24–25).

• Believers are to confront, or correct each other with humility (1 Thessalonians 2:5–7).

• We are not to repay evil for evil (1 Peter 3:8–12).

• Pastors are to shepherd the flock of God (1 Peter 5:1–7).

• We are to pray for one another (James 5:16).

The Most Effective Tool for Church Conflict

“The prayer of a righteous man is powerful and effective” (James 5:16).

The under-shepherd is the person whom God has chosen to lead in the office of a pastor. Though we make mistakes, we understand that through Christ we are righteous. The righteous person’s prayer is very effective and God will move circumstances, hearts, and emotional mountains through the faithful prayers of the righteous leader. God watches the humble heart of the prayerful leader, and He will mediate in any conflict.

However, James also instructs us that when we pray we need to have pure motives. When involved in a stressful conflict that is full of tension, Christians sometimes act in a very ungodly manner. People fear change, being embarrassed, humbling themselves, and feeling exposed or humiliated; many overreact when they start losing control. People avoid emotional pain, and they do not want to be alone. Many times one will choose to yell, lie, slander, gossip, or worse in order to avoid these outcomes.

Pastors and church leaders are not exempt from all of these concerns. Pastors can be so concerned about how they will appear, and they want to look like they are in control. As a result, they find it difficult to admit to themselves or to others that their motives could be wrong.

When you pray about those involved in the conflict you are dealing with, ask God to help you see your motives and insure that you want to hear and understand His divine will. Ask the Lord to point out Scriptures that will direct you, remembering that “When you ask, you do not receive because you ask with wrong motives, that you may spend what you get on your pleasures” (James 4:3–4).

Paul encourages us to “consider others better than yourselves. Each of you should look not only to your own interests but also to the interests of others” (Philippians 2:3–4).

When we model pure motives, our people will see and often behave like we do. Here is a listing of sins that will always lead to a conflict in the church:

• Gossip, dissension, divisiveness, rebellion

• Complacency, passivity, and apathy

• A critical spirit

• Self-pride (not willing to compromise or feeling one’s way is the only way)

• Allowing sins to continue, unwillingness to confront

• Unwillingness to forgive

• Poor stewardship

• Power struggles

• Personal agendas

• Unwillingness to encourage each other toward righteousness

• Unwillingness to evangelize (only concerned about what is within our church walls)

Conflict can be destructive, but it can also help us grow. It is wonderful when a conflict ends in peaceful resolution.

**Q:** What does Paul mean by “you who are spiritual” when assigning the task of restoration?

**A:** He is calling for the more spiritually mature to set aside their desire for retaliation and choose restoration as a more spiritual goal. He is placing the responsibility not on the more guilty party, but on the more spiritually mature party. He reveals the importance of spiritual maturity in pursuing the proper priorities.

**Q:** Why is prayer essential in dealing with conflict?

**A:** Through prayer one gains the greater perspective God possesses. 2 CHAPTER

#### 1.5.2 — Identify the most effective tool for church conflict and explain how it is to be used.

The Bible is filled with examples of conflict and directions for us as we bring resolution to these challenging occurrences. We need to look at the Bible as a manual for handling conflict in the church. The New Testament letters were largely written to groups of Christians facing problems within their church family. The instructions by the various writers are timeless. Conflict among God’s people is as old as the book of Genesis and the other books that Moses wrote. He dealt with conflict—seemingly endlessly―and understood it to be a part of what God called him to do. Moses wrote: “Do not hate your brother in your heart. Rebuke your neighbor frankly so you will not share in his guilt” (Leviticus 19:17).

The Scripture speaks to us about:

• Disputes with another believer

If your brother sins against you, go and show him his fault, just between the two of you. If he listens to you, you have won your brother over. But if he will not listen, take one or two others along, so that every matter may be established by the testimony of two or three witnesses. If he refuses to listen to them, tell it to the church; and if he refuses to listen even to the church, treat him as you would a pagan or a tax collector. (Matthew 18:15–17)

Jesus sets forth the method of restoring or disciplining a professing Christian who sins against another member of the church in a private manner. To neglect Christ’s instruction will bring spiritual compromise and ultimate destruction to the church as a holy people of God (1 Peter 2:9) (Stamps 1992, 1443).

• When a believer is living in sin

“Brothers, if someone is caught in a sin, you who are spiritual should restore him gently. But watch yourself, or you also may be tempted” (Galatians 6:1).

Paul’s reason for giving us this instruction is to encourage believers to take responsibility for each other and to help the person who is sinning or causing a problem in the church. The goal is to help the person understand the seriousness of their conversations or behavior and hopefully motivate them to get right with the Lord.

“You who are spiritual” refers to those who have enough spiritual discernment to understand the problem and enough spiritual insight to realize that they could fall into sin. They know themselves and have enough spiritual maturity to confront the person with godly compassion.

Human nature causes some people to act or lead in controlling ways because previous leadership (or you) have been afraid to speak to them or confront them. In the Lord’s church, it is totally acceptable and responsible for pastors to expose people’s political games and agendas as they seek to win a church battle. As pastors, we are to go to them or ask another leader to go to them and say, “I see you are lining up a coalition and I understand why. But could we work on the problem together, rather than taking sides?”

When we need to confront, it is helpful to keep the focus on the future of the church with comments like this one:

“I do not think you desire to injure this church; I want to help you keep this from bringing future harm or distracting us from reaching people for Christ.”

Ask for permission to speak to the issue:

“May I share with you something that I feel is very important and crucial to your well being (spiritual growth, personal health, family health)?”

Include their strengths in your conversation:

“Your discipline (attitude, involvement, ability to understand, and so forth) will give you a great foundation so you can make some changes that will help you and others.” (See Philippians 1:6.)

Give them hope with words like these:

“Please understand that the Holy Spirit will help you. He is your constant companion and will give you wisdom. The body of Christ will help you and I will do everything I can to assist you. For everyone, the Christian life is all about growing through difficult times.” (See Romans 5:1–5.)

Give them words of mercy:

“I understand what it is like to grow, to change, and to learn obedience. I have walked through those times too. The Bible gives us great encouragement in examples from Peter, David, Moses, and other leaders who needed to do this.” (See 1 Timothy 1:2–3; Ephesians 4:31–5:2.)

Not All Conflict Is Sin

When we are involved in a church conflict:

1. Understand the nature of the conflict situation and the objectives, motives, and feelings of those involved.

2. Review what the Scripture teaches (or in principle demonstrates) concerning the causes of and solutions for conflict.

3. Make specific suggestions that are practical and will restore peace and healthy, constructive solutions.

4. Encourage healthy relationships, through forgiveness, correct information (versus misinformation or not enough information), and putting disputes behind us.

Guidelines for interpersonal behavior:

• To be in control of what we say

“A man who lacks judgment derides his neighbor, but a man of understanding holds his tongue” (Proverbs 11:12; see James 3:1–10).

• To respond to anger with a gentle answer

“A gentle answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger” (Proverbs 15:1).

• To live in ways that please God

“When a man’s ways are pleasing to the Lord, he makes even his enemies live at peace with him” (Proverbs 16:7).

• To develop patience

“Through patience a ruler can be persuaded, and a gentle tongue can break a bone” (Proverbs 25:15).

• To fight inner conflict

This is an example of inner conflict with Paul. He is torn between doing good and giving in to temptation. These struggles can be met and overcome through the power of Jesus Christ.

“I do not understand what I do. For what I want to do I do not do, but what I hate I do. . . What a wretched man I am! Who will rescue me from this body of death? Thanks be to God—through Jesus Christ our Lord!” (Romans 7:15–24).

Often used passages that address church conflict:

• When another Christian is in error, sin, or is guilty of ethical misbehavior (Matthew 18:15–17)

• The church is to enjoy an atmosphere of peace so that God can freely move (Romans 12:9–21).

• Believers are to walk in love (Ephesians 4:25–5:2).

• Christians are to restore one another in love (Galatians 6:1).

• Christians are to love—with a godly love (1 Corinthians 13).

• Believers are to be patient with one another (Colossians 3:12–17).

• Leaders are to lead with a gentle attitude (2 Timothy 2:24–25).

• Believers are to confront, or correct each other with humility (1 Thessalonians 2:5–7).

• We are not to repay evil for evil (1 Peter 3:8–12).

• Pastors are to shepherd the flock of God (1 Peter 5:1–7).

• We are to pray for one another (James 5:16).

The Most Effective Tool for Church Conflict

“The prayer of a righteous man is powerful and effective” (James 5:16).

The under-shepherd is the person whom God has chosen to lead in the office of a pastor. Though we make mistakes, we understand that through Christ we are righteous. The righteous person’s prayer is very effective and God will move circumstances, hearts, and emotional mountains through the faithful prayers of the righteous leader. God watches the humble heart of the prayerful leader, and He will mediate in any conflict.

However, James also instructs us that when we pray we need to have pure motives. When involved in a stressful conflict that is full of tension, Christians sometimes act in a very ungodly manner. People fear change, being embarrassed, humbling themselves, and feeling exposed or humiliated; many overreact when they start losing control. People avoid emotional pain, and they do not want to be alone. Many times one will choose to yell, lie, slander, gossip, or worse in order to avoid these outcomes.

Pastors and church leaders are not exempt from all of these concerns. Pastors can be so concerned about how they will appear, and they want to look like they are in control. As a result, they find it difficult to admit to themselves or to others that their motives could be wrong.

When you pray about those involved in the conflict you are dealing with, ask God to help you see your motives and insure that you want to hear and understand His divine will. Ask the Lord to point out Scriptures that will direct you, remembering that “When you ask, you do not receive because you ask with wrong motives, that you may spend what you get on your pleasures” (James 4:3–4).

Paul encourages us to “consider others better than yourselves. Each of you should look not only to your own interests but also to the interests of others” (Philippians 2:3–4).

When we model pure motives, our people will see and often behave like we do. Here is a listing of sins that will always lead to a conflict in the church:

• Gossip, dissension, divisiveness, rebellion

• Complacency, passivity, and apathy

• A critical spirit

• Self-pride (not willing to compromise or feeling one’s way is the only way)

• Allowing sins to continue, unwillingness to confront

• Unwillingness to forgive

• Poor stewardship

• Power struggles

• Personal agendas

• Unwillingness to encourage each other toward righteousness

• Unwillingness to evangelize (only concerned about what is within our church walls)

Conflict can be destructive, but it can also help us grow. It is wonderful when a conflict ends in peaceful resolution.

**Q:** What does Paul mean by “you who are spiritual” when assigning the task of restoration?

**A:** He is calling for the more spiritually mature to set aside their desire for retaliation and choose restoration as a more spiritual goal. He is placing the responsibility not on the more guilty party, but on the more spiritually mature party. He reveals the importance of spiritual maturity in pursuing the proper priorities.

**Q:** Why is prayer essential in dealing with conflict?

**A:** Through prayer one gains the greater perspective God possesses. 2 CHAPTER

## 2 - The Evaluation of Conflict

### Early Prognosis of Conflict

#### 2.1.1 — Identify the eight warning signs of disruptive church conflict and be able to characterize each accurately.

In North America, church officials have had much interest in determining what might be the early warning signs of destructive conflict. The concern, however, is that church leaders are overly attentive to these factors and thus become nonproductive in developing the health and vision for the church. We must maintain a juggling act to keep our eye on the goal of a healthy church in God’s kingdom while we remain aware that the enemy or unusual conflict could arise and disrupt the will of God.

Some churches need outside intervention and the steady hand of a recognized leader or possibly the help of the denominational district leadership. However, this needs to be carefully evaluated, as the congregation must desire this level of assistance. If district leadership steps in too soon, more problems, rather than good can come. Here are some reasons for being careful about the timing of bringing outside intervention into the church:

They do not know the roots of the conflict, only what has preceded specific conflicts.

Outside intervention before it is asked for is not universally appreciated and may generate a conflict that did not exist before nor would exist without such intervention.

Denominational offices do not have enough adequately trained personnel or staff with enough time to deal with what they are likely to discover.

Because local churches are voluntary religious organizations, are sometimes led by marginally skilled leaders, have heterogeneous belief systems, and are less and less admired and bolstered by society at large, they are rife with the potential for conflict.

There is little value for the denominational official to step in when he or she cannot know for sure that there is a preventable conflict ahead. He or she is not likely to be appreciated for uninvited meddling (Leas 1998, 12).

Sensing Possible Problems

Speed Leas defines eight possible warning signs of disruptive church conflict. In evaluating these signs, a church can see what is happening in their setting, and develop a conflict management process for potential difficulties. Following is the list of factors:

Environmental Factors

If the community’s job market gradually or suddenly depreciates, some people will leave the area to look for work. Thus, people may leave churches because they need to find a way to make a living. Or economic decline in a community can cause people to be very concerned about personal finances, and they can become stressed because of personal issues. People who have tension in their lives may bring the tension with them to church. When a person is having marital difficulty, financial problems, or stress because of the local economy, there will be more potential for conflict in that church. On the other hand, if the community is relatively stable, progressive, and the people feel optimistic about living there, then the congregation will likely feel more at peace with other areas of their lives. Pastors often make the mistake of thinking that if people do not have jobs, or they have lost some of their retirement they should continue giving at the level they once did. People respond to life issues. The environment has an effect, and pastors are wise to consider the environmental issues of their community.

Special Congregational Factors

Some congregations have more potential for destructive conflict because of events in their history. One congregation I knew of had more antagonistic, aggressive behavior from some of the lay leaders. Some would yell at the pastor, feel they had a right to tell him off, or accuse him of not being sensitive to their needs. One man proudly said, “I shook the pastor’s desk and raised my voice when I confronted him.” When evaluating the possible reasons for this excess of anger and improper aggression, it was noted that a former, long-term pastor was known for his bad temper and tendency to fly off the handle. Most of the people, who felt they had a right to tell the pastor off, came into the church during that pastor’s tenure. Although this man might have done much good, he also trained the people to respond or act in this improper way.

We also know that when a congregation has had one pastor for a long time (more than twelve years) the church is likely to experience difficulty with the next pastor. This person is typically labeled the sacrificial lamb pastor and is someone who only stays one or two years. There does not have to be a sacrificial lamb if significant instruction, training, and sensitivity are part of the congregation’s preparation. Also, the search committee needs to be aware of this real possibility and secure a pastor who is mature, understands this potential, and has experience in transitioning a church.

Unresolved grief over the moral failure of a pastor can cause deep hurt within a church body, or an untimely death of the pastor can shake people’s faith. The pastor who follows these kinds of situations needs to focus on healing and trust-building during his or her first months or years.

At times there are lay leaders with power and influence but very little spiritual maturity. When the new pastor recognizes this, these people can lose power and begin to cause great difficulty as they also lose their influence.

Understanding the history of the congregation takes time; however, gaining this knowledge is necessary as the pastor leads a church toward health.

Some congregational factors cannot be managed through rational, problem-solving techniques. Some emotional problems that people or groups bring are beyond the ability of a church to change by dialogue and good communication. When these kinds of factors are present, the deacons and pastor will need to band together to deal with the unresolved conflict issues of these unreasonable or controlling people.

Rumors

Member complaints, whisperings, and the e-mail concerns or gossip in disguise of prayer requests are often warning signs of potential destructive conflict. These complaints could be about anything: a new program, decorations, denominational concerns, the pastor’s message, or a new leader in a department. The rumors are frequently vague and general, and it might take considerable time to determine the real concern a person or group has. By the time the solid concern is understood and the root person responsible is discovered, the rumors have developed a life of their own.

Determining these types of complaints as early as possible is always the optimum way of managing them. Quick identification and a sooner-rather-than-later response will often prevent long-term damage and eliminate the discomfort a congregation could experience. The ability to determine when and where the rumors are coming from is a gift that pastors should seek as they deal with conflict issues quickly and effectively.

Normal, surface-level complaints always happen, and mature congregational members can help you identify the frequency, type, and people involved in those complaints.

Reduced Participation

Conflict can cause people to withdraw from personal contact with other members or with the pastor. Sometimes this is catalogued by people saying, “The new pastor does not visit like the last one did.” Or, “This pastor does not come to as many events.” They say these things when the comments are not accurate. To save face, these people find a way to excuse their lack of participation by using a leader or the pastor as a scapegoat. This is a common human characteristic and one of the only ways to manage this is to try to meet with the offended and assure them of your love and care as a shepherd. We must also remember that some will choose not to like us and may not agree with the healthy direction of the church. We must do all we can to communicate effectively and demonstrate a servant’s attitude as we minister to these people as well.

Decline in Attendance and Giving

A drop in attendance and a decrease in giving is often a sign of destructive conflict. On several occasions, I have heard a pastor say, “These people told me that if I do not do this, or unless I stop that, they will leave and take their money with them.” While the pastor should be sensitive to their real concerns, he or she cannot tolerate being threatened by members of the congregation.

Research on congregations has shown that membership decline seems to be the least sensitive indicator of conflict in a church. People tend not to remove their names from membership as frequently as they tend to reduce the frequency of Sunday attendance and their financial giving. Attendance is the most sensitive of these factors, tending to decline at a faster rate than giving, which is affected more often than membership (Leas 1998, 14).

Changes in Lay Leadership

This concern often comes to the surface when a new pastor selects new leaders without knowing who has been in leadership and who has served well or poorly. It can also occur when a congregation grows and there is a need for new leaders. Long-term leaders can feel threatened and begin to complain.

The new leaders could be younger, or new programs could be introduced that the pastor feels will bring health to the church. Sometimes the new leaders have a different theological perspective on a particular doctrine of the church. They can be labeled as too contemporary or as not being as spiritual because of their ideas. A new leader can come from a cultural group that is different from the majority of the congregation, and the new leader may desire to reach out more to other cultures.

When a congregation’s demographics shift from the blue collar classification to a more professional category, one group may feel threatened and tension between the different groups can occur, even among Christians.

While new leadership is not a guarantee of future conflict, it will often test the congregation’s ability to understand and manage change. For a congregation to grow and develop healthy maturity it needs to be open to additional or different leaders as the need arises. A healthy church equips the members to do ministry.

The Emergence of Hardliners

If people are beginning to take stands and are not communicating in healthy ways, not seeking win / win solutions, then hardness has set in. This congregation has gone beyond the precursor stage and someone typically loses. There is the possibility that intervention can restore health and God can change hearts. However, when people do not want to communicate in an understanding and gracious way, it could be very difficult. People in healthy relationships listen to one another and give and take as they are willing to be flexible and seek solutions.

Changes in the Pastor’s Behavior

Pastors can respond to the conflict and tension in ways that are damaging to their health, family, and relationship with God. We understand that stress can cause physical problems, emotional pain, and injury to a marriage. Sometimes a pastor will begin to drink alcoholic beverages, ingest drugs, constantly argue with his or her spouse, entertain adulterous ideas, become involved in an adulterous relationship, or view pornography. These choices will cause the pastor’s downfall and most certainly are not the ways God has given us to manage tension in our lives.

Pastors should strive to lead a balanced life and grow in their devotion and passion to Christ. A balanced life includes exercise, diet control, adequate sleep, taking a regular day off, and taking a vacation. The spiritual discipline of scheduling fasting and prayer, spiritual retreats, and devotions are part of the balanced pastor’s schedule as he or she endeavors to serve the congregation and live a satisfying life.

**Q:** How do environmental factors play a part in the atmosphere that leads to disruptive conflict?

**A:** Uncertainty in the work force can bring instability to a congregation. The worries people carry outside the church can affect their behavior inside the church. Economic decline in a community can cause people to relocate, thus depleting the congregation and often taking its most progressive leaders.

**Q:** How can the departure of a former pastor contribute to later conflict?

**A:** If a former pastor left as a result of a moral failure, the congregation may unwittingly express their hurt and frustration in conflict with one another or with their new pastor. A congregation whose former pastor served a lengthy term may be unfamiliar with the process of establishing a new pastoral relationship. Sadly this new pastor often must face significant rejection as people seek to honor their former pastor with what they feel is loyalty. A new pastor may be forced to face the unwise decisions a former pastor made when placing immature people in leadership.

**Q:** How does reduced participation indicate possible conflict?

**A:** People may not bring conflict to the surface but choose instead to be less involved. People tend to reduce their giving and involvement in ministry prior to their decision to leave the church.

**Q:** How might a new pastor’s effort to involve new leaders bring evidence of underlying conflict?

**A:** People may be accepting of new leaders until an area they are involved with is affected. Because new leaders have not had the time to demonstrate their full set of beliefs or past experiences, they may evidence differing theologies or ways of operating because of their previous ministry background. If the new leader is a part of a minority group or is distinct culturally from other members, there can be a reaction to the choice of a new leader.

**Q:** How can a pastor’s behavior be affected by disruptive conflict?

**A:** Pastors may choose habits they had previously spurned as an act of rebelling against their responsibility. Often an adulterous relationship or some other significant change in behavior can indicate a pastor’s level of hurt or anxiety. Such choices can never be excused or blamed in conflict. The pastor is responsible for his or her choices just as any other individual would be.

A balanced life includes exercise, diet control, adequate sleep, taking a regular day off, and taking a vacation. The spiritual discipline of scheduling fasting and prayer, spiritual retreats, and devotions are part of the balanced pastor’s schedule as he or she endeavors to serve the congregation and live a satisfying life.

**Q:** How do environmental factors play a part in the atmosphere that leads to disruptive conflict?

**A:** Uncertainty in the work force can bring instability to a congregation. The worries people carry outside the church can affect their behavior inside the church. Economic decline in a community can cause people to relocate, thus depleting the congregation and often taking its most progressive leaders.

**Q:** How can the departure of a former pastor contribute to later conflict?

**A:** If a former pastor left as a result of a moral failure, the congregation may unwittingly express their hurt and frustration in conflict with one another or with their new pastor. A congregation whose former pastor served a lengthy term may be unfamiliar with the process of establishing a new pastoral relationship. Sadly this new pastor often must face significant rejection as people seek to honor their former pastor with what they feel is loyalty. A new pastor may be forced to face the unwise decisions a former pastor made when placing immature people in leadership.

**Q:** How does reduced participation indicate possible conflict?

**A:** People may not bring conflict to the surface but choose instead to be less involved. People tend to reduce their giving and involvement in ministry prior to their decision to leave the church.

**Q:** How might a new pastor’s effort to involve new leaders bring evidence of underlying conflict?

**A:** People may be accepting of new leaders until an area they are involved with is affected. Because new leaders have not had the time to demonstrate their full set of beliefs or past experiences, they may evidence differing theologies or ways of operating because of their previous ministry background. If the new leader is a part of a minority group or is distinct culturally from other members, there can be a reaction to the choice of a new leader.

**Q:** How can a pastor’s behavior be affected by disruptive conflict?

**A:** Pastors may choose habits they had previously spurned as an act of rebelling against their responsibility. Often an adulterous relationship or some other significant change in behavior can indicate a pastor’s level of hurt or anxiety. Such choices can never be excused or blamed in conflict. The pastor is responsible for his or her choices just as any other individual would be.

### Evaluating the Depth of Conflict

#### 2.2.1 — Describe the essential elements of a constructive win / win approach to addressing conflict and a destructive win / lose approach.

When church leaders discuss church conflict, the leaders themselves may disagree on how to handle it. Some may feel the conflict should be ignored because it will go away on its own. Others feel it must be addressed gently because it could develop a life of its own; some leaders feel it should be aggressively dealt with because the conflict could cause grave damage to the church.

A leader may make this statement about an agitator: “That is just his personality; he has whined for years, and he is harmless.” Or, “She has no influence in the church, so no one will listen to her.” However, when these types of people have been negative for years, causing people to be uncomfortable, causing them to question leadership, and causing them to consistently challenge new and healthy ideas—the negative people have created problems that must be addressed.

We should access the depth of the conflict in order to better deal with it and to determine whom to involve in the resolution. We should understand these basic assumptions:

• All conflict is different.

• Our emotional impression or discernment is often an accurate barometer for how bad the conflict actually is.

• An accurate understanding is needed in order to respond to conflict properly.

David Johnson and Frank Johnson discovered that there are basically two types of conflict: one where people have a win / win goal and one where there is a win / lose goal. We will consider here the characteristics of the two types of conflict (Johnson and Johnson 1975, 157):

Constructive (Win / Win)

• Defining the controversy as a mutual problem

• Participation by all group members

• Open and honest expression of ideas and feelings

• Everyone’s contributions listened to, given attention, taken seriously, valued, and respected

• Quiet members encouraged to participate

• Effective sending and receiving communication skills used

• Differences in opinions and ideas sought and clarified

• Underlying assumptions and frames of reference brought out into the open and discussed

• Disagreement not taken as personal rejection by some or all group members

• Adequate differentiation of positions; differences clearly understood

• Adequate integration of positions; similarities clearly understood and positions combined in creative syntheses

• Emotions responded to with involvement and other emotions

• Equal situational power among all members

• Moderate level of tensions

Destructive (win / lose)

• Defining the controversy as a win / lose situation

• Participation by only a few group members; self-censorship and withdrawal

• Closed or deceitful expression of ideas and feelings

• The contribution of many members ignored, devalued, not respected, and treated lightly

• Quiet members not encouraged to participate

• Effective sending and receiving communication skills not used

• Differences in opinions and ideas ignored or suppressed

• Underlying assumptions and frames of reference not brought out into the open and discussed

• Disagreement taken as personal rejection by some or all group members

• Inadequate differentiation of positions; differences not clearly understood

• Inadequate integration of positions; similarities not clearly understood and positions not combined in creative syntheses

• Emotions responded to by uninvolved understanding, or ignored

• Unequal power among group members

• Tension level too low or too high for productive problem-solving

As we determine which type of attitude each person or group has, we can also decide on the plan we will use to deal with the conflict. One person might be reasonable and understanding as we begin the process of finding a solution, and the other might be hardened and not willing to budge. Pride, ego, and control can get in the way of healing between two people. There must be a willingness to be flexible and to compromise if it will be for the good of the body.

Speed Leas identifies five levels of conflict that must be addressed in different ways.

1. Problems to Solve

2. Disagreements

3. Contests

4. Fight / Flight

5. Intractable Situations

1. Problems to Solve

At this level, the people feel discomfort in one another’s presence. Differences exist; the people understand one another, but have conflicting goals, values, needs, action plans, or information. There may be short-lived anger or denial of hostile feelings. Anger may flare at any level; however, at this level the anger will be short-lived and quickly controlled; sustained anger is an indicator of higher levels of conflict (especially 3 and 4).

At level 1 the language is clear, specific, oriented to the here and now, and not blaming or filled with innuendo . People at level 1 are open to others’ opinions and to discuss why they feel the way they do. They can rationally describe what is happening to create the conflict.

2. Disagreements

This level has not yet gone to the win / lose destructive level; however, it is more difficult and emotionally charged than level 1. At level 1, the people are focused on solving the problem. At level 2, they are less concerned with problem-solving and more concerned about self-protection. Parties in the conflict are concerned about coming out looking good.

At level 2, a new level of shrewdness and calculation enters in. The two parties involve friends in their discussion and begin to justify their case and plan a strategy. The language shifts from specific to general. The people are cautious about sharing all they know about the difficulties and tend to withhold information that might enhance the other or hurt themselves. Hostile humor, labeling, and distancing begin to elevate the tension, and people begin choosing sides.

3. Contests

At level three the conflict has begun the win / lose stage. Though this level is not at the fight / flight stage, it is nevertheless on the downside of real conflict resolution. It has shifted from self-protection to winning. Competition enters in, as one party wants to win and tries to do what he or she can to make the other party lose. At this level there is frequently more than one problem to fix. A list of concerns begins to add up, and it gets more complicated. Perceptions of the real problem are very different, and people begin to depend on their gut feelings rather than on the facts. Research on conflict and intervention (especially Transactional Analysis , Rational-Emotive Therapy , and Neuro-Linguistic Programming ) shows that perceptual distortions can cause serious problems. Some of the distortions that begin to be present at level 3 and increase at levels 4 and 5 are magnification , dichotomization , over - generalization , and assumption .

Magnification is the tendency to see oneself as more benevolent than one actually is and the other as more evil than he or she actually is.

Dichotomization is the tendency to divide everything and everybody into neat dual, but separate packages: us or them, right or wrong, stay or leave, fight or flee, and so forth. In dichotomization, it is almost impossible to see shades between extremes or to find more than two alternative choices.

Over-generalization takes on an out-of-control and / or pernicious quality, with comments like “You always . . . ,” “He never. . . ,” and “Everybody . . . .” In the assumption distortion, the people delude themselves into believing that they are excellent mind readers. They often believe they know the other person’s mind better than he or she does (Berne 1972; Wessler and Wessler 1980; Bandler and Grinder 1975).

4. Fight / flight

At level 4 the goal of the parties changes from wanting to win to wanting to hurt or get rid of the others. The parties no longer believe the other will change; or if they could possibly change, it is still not enough. Damage to the other’s reputation, integrity, or personhood is the goal. Examples include trying to get the pastor fired, trying to get the opposition to leave the church, or trying to get people to join their group in leaving the church. At this level the objectives have shifted from desiring any good for the church or for the group to being right and punishing the other side in the process.

At this level, factions are identified, and it is not difficult to determine who is in and who is out in each of the two groups. A strong leader (or leaders) emerges and the members of the groups conform to the desires and goals of the leader. The language becomes defined, and the people talk about principles more than issues; they refer to eternal verities such as truth, freedom, doctrine, and justice as they endeavor to solidify their case. They are less concerned with ethics and justify their behavior by thinking, The end justifies the means.

This fight / flight level brings out the primitive survival responses of both defendants and proponents. No middle ground exists between running and attacking. Other behaviors that are common to level 4 include a detachment of the parties from one another, which prevents awareness of (or concern for) the pain they cause the others and the unforgiving, cold, self-righteousness of themselves. People at this level attempt to enlist outsiders in their cause and they try to muster their forces to bring down the opponent. At this level the people will not even speak to each other if they walk down the same hallway or see each other in the grocery store.

5. Intractable Situations

This level is unmanageable. At level 5 the situation is out-of-control and the conflict is not solvable unless there is divine intervention and the parties are open to that.

At level 4, the conflict has gone to “How can I punish the other side?” and “How can I get rid of them?” But now with level 5, the conflict has gone to “How can I destroy the other side?” Annihilation is the goal. The opposition is seen not only as anti-functional to the church, but as harmful to society at large and therefore needs to be removed. An example of this in the church is expressed when members have dismissed a pastor, but they also feel that he or she should never ever get another church position. They may seek ways to hinder his or her search for another ministry position.

At this level the people typically perceive themselves as part of an eternal cause, and are fighting for universal principles or truths. They feel they are compelled to fight, and they care more about their cause than the good of the church, truth, their families, or even God. They are going to defeat the opposition, no matter how much or how long it takes (Leas 1998, 19–22).

If you have been in church ministry for very long, you have likely experienced, seen, or at least heard of all the levels of conflict that we identified. Looking at these levels might cause you to fear the pastorate, or determine that you should not enter the pastoral ministry. However, when God chooses us to be pastors, He calls us to serve the church and reach the community. It is not the length of tenure that God evaluates; it is our faithfulness in understanding our unique church, evaluating what we should do, and doing the right thing. The seven churches of Asia, which are mentioned in the book of Revelation, had unique challenges. The Lord informed each pastor regarding what he needed to do to lead that particular church. The word of the Lord for each pastor was not only to identify the good that was present, but also the people or situations that needed to be corrected. The pastor was expected to do what the Lord instructed him to do. When doing this, we should not think or dream of being there ten or twenty years; we may have been sent there just to restore (or bring for the first time) health to a church so that it could become a positive influence in that community.

**Q:** Name five of the characteristics of a win / win mind-set.

**A:** Defining the conflict as a mutual problem. Open and honest expression of ideas and feelings. Encouraging all members involved in the conflict to participate. The use of effective communication skills. Differences of opinion sought out and clarified. Open discussion of underlying assumptions and frames of reference.

**Q:** Name five of the characteristics of a win / lose mindset.

**A:** Participation by only a few with others intentionally excluded. Closed or deceitful expression of ideas and feelings. Defining the controversy in win / lose attitudes. Disagreement taken as personal rejection. Differences of opinion ignored or rejected.

**Q:** What are the five levels of conflict?

**A:** Problems to solve—differences exist but they can be addressed and individual feelings understood by all involved. Disagreements—Here, people can be concerned about self-protection more than the issue to be solved. Contests—Perceptions as to the real problem differ sharply, and there is little willingness to embrace other points of view. Fight / Flight—At this level, the parties now desire more than victory; they want the opposing party to lose. Intractable Situations—At this level the situation is out of control. This stage brings thoughts of destroying the other side of the argument.

**Q:** At what point does a conflict typically move to a win / lose mentality?

**A:** Usually when a conflict has reached the third stage, Contests, it is now in the “win / lose” approach. Given the practice of people, conflicts even at the first stage can take on a win / lose character.

**Q:** Name and define the three distortions that can begin at level three and escalate in the levels that follow.

**A:** Magnification—to see oneself as more benevolent and the other individual as more evil that the facts might warrant. Dichotomization—The tendency to divide every issue and / or individual into two polar parts, as though there is no third or additional perspective possible. Over-generalization—Statements such as “you never” or “you always” which cloud the discussion with inaccurate and unfair characterizations.

**Q:** What are the three common types of conflict people experience in a church?

**A:** Intrapersonal Conflict—this is an internal battle between the person I desire to be and perhaps the role I am expected to fill and the person I feel forced to be. Interpersonal Conflict—Conflicts and personality differences between people. These conflicts are often not related to specific issues. Substantive Conflict—disputes over facts, values, goals, belief, etc.

### Your Style in Handling Conflict

#### 2.3.1 — Explain the three common types of conflict people will experience in a church and how they can be given aid in resolving such conflicts.

As you pastor in the twenty-first century, it is critical to understand yourself: your personality type, how you think, and how you plan. Pastoring is not an easy occupation; however, it is tremendously fulfilling as you see positive growth in people, marriages healed, and people on their way to heaven. The conflict is just a small part (though it may seem large) of what we do.

Remind yourself that people desire to experience God, understand the Bible, and have healthy relationships. All people have been created in the image of God (Genesis 1:27), and Jesus Christ was involved in this act. Colossians 1:16 says, “For by him all things were created.” The Scripture informs us that God has “set eternity in the heart of men” (Ecclesiastes 3:11). Deep inside the most degraded sinner or rebellious, bitter Christian there is a hunger to know the One who has created him or her.

Also, the eternal Word of God is part of our DNA . Those who understand the truth of Jesus Christ will respond to God’s written word as it convicts, convinces, and continues to speak to their conscience. The Scripture is “God breathed and is useful for teaching, rebuking, correcting and training in righteousness, so that the man of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work” (2 Timothy 3:16). God gave us the Scripture to instruct us in how to know Him, how we can spend eternity with Him, and how to enjoy the abundant life He has for us.

People also want healthy relationships. This world is dysfunctional and complicated. Dysfunctional families began when Adam and Eve disobeyed God. This sin nature was passed on from them, so unhealthy relationships have been part of humanity from that time. It is not God’s will for people to be abused, hurt, manipulated, lied to, or injured by others. God desires us to discover healthy relationships that are pure, righteous, and trustful. However, the world simply is not that way. The enemy of our souls has brought disorder, confusion, and hatred to every culture, race, and people. These are the people we are endeavoring to reach for Christ. But we must remember that dysfunction and complication come into our churches when people arrive. Conflict, anger, power plays, and aggressive behavior are nothing new for this world or for the church. However, it still remains that people want to be healthy and have meaningful relationships.

Best selling author Rick Warren writes,

A friend once told me of a survey he took in a church. When he asked his members why they joined the church, 93 percent of them said, “I joined because of the pastor.”

Next he asked, “What if the pastor leaves? Will you leave? ‘No’ was the response from 93 percent of them. When he asked why they would not leave, the response was ‘because I have friends here!’” (Warren 2005, 1).

This allegiance is normal and healthy because people are hungry for good relationships that lead to friendships.

Lyle Schaller has done extensive research that shows that the more friendships a person has in a congregation, the less likely he or she is to become inactive or to leave. In contrast I once read about a survey of four hundred church dropouts who were asked why they left their churches. Over 75 percent of the respondents said, “I did not feel anyone cared whether I was there or not” (Warren 2005, 1 ).

Rick Warren says that it is a myth that you must know everyone in the church in order to feel like a part of a church. The average church member knows sixty-seven people in the congregation, whether the church has two hundred or two thousand attending. A member does not have to know everyone in the church in order to feel a loyalty to “my church,” but he or she does have to know some people (Warren 2005, 1).

Relationships are important to the health of individuals. Understanding how we operate when the pressure is on or when we are under the fire of conflict is also important. Knowing our typical way of doing things and understanding whether we are outgoing or a little timid helps us to deal with people and relationships.

In their book, Church Fights , Speed Leas and Paul Kittlaus write of three common kinds of conflict that churches often experience (Leas and Kittlaus, 1973):

Intrapersonal Conflict:

This refers to the struggle a person has when different parts of the self compete with each another. (I want to be a beloved pastor, but I also want to be a preacher who speaks the truth.)

Interpersonal Conflict:

These conflicts are caused by personality differences between people and are not related primarily to issues. (I think I am a strong, capable person, but the board chairperson treats me like an incompetent.)

Substantive Conflict:

These are disputes over facts, values, goals, and beliefs . (I think we should put a new roof on the church, but the social concerns committee wants to open a clothes closet for the poor.)

Knowing what kind of conflict you are dealing with and how you typically respond to that type is extremely valuable. Examining all three, whether that is intrapersonal, interpersonal, or substantive will help you determine which direction to take in addressing the person or evaluating yourself.

In evaluating the different types of conflict, learn more about whom you are dealing with and how to format your plan of mediation, or topics of discussion. To be prepared for any kind of confrontation—gentle rebuke or just to better understand their point—some questions are helpful:

• What is the spiritual condition of this person?

• Is he or she a believer, a mature Christian?

• Has he or she been involved in ministry in any way?

What is his or her marriage like? Is the family a dysfunctional family, blended family, single parent family, or is there a great deal of tension present within the family? (Family difficulties can often affect a person’s work and church involvement.)

Does this person really want to resolve the conflict, or does he or she just want the situation or person to go away? Will he or she commit to finding a middle ground?

Is the conflict in this person being fed by outside influences, for example, family ties within the congregation or a friend who wants influence because he or she is a major financial contributor?

Is the person willing to be totally transparent and not hide anything about his or her concern, so as to honestly evaluate the situation?

Is the person willing to own or accept his or her part of the problem?

People do things, say things, and feel certain ways for various reasons. Trying to determine a person’s bottom line, personality, history, and desires is the first step.

To understand the conflict more accurately, you must ask yourself some questions. Pastors are teachers, administrators, counselors, and mediators, and they sometimes need to gently rebuke people. Understanding our motives and the motives of others is critical to this office that God has called us to.

The writer of Proverbs gives us this instruction: “Turning your ear to wisdom and applying your heart to understanding . . . call out for insight” (Proverbs 2:2–3).

In acquiring understanding , ask yourself

• What do I have to gain or lose in this situation?

• Am I willing to be totally transparent and without hidden motives?

• Am I willing to consider other options and the advice others may have about my decision . . . or about me?

• Do I have any prejudices or biases that will influence my ability to come to a “pure” decision?

Pastors have motives too. They make mistakes, have prejudices, and struggle with different kinds of personalities. Our goal is to minister to people of every culture, age, color, and personality for the glory of God. Personal evaluation is part of what we do throughout our ministry.

Concerning the conflict:

• What level of trust exists between the person and me?

• Is there anyone going to be present to represent the other person(s) and speak on their behalf? What is their motivation for wanting this person present?

• Is there a history of this particular conflict, person, or a similar situation that should be researched?

• Have the real issues been verbalized and written down, or is the conflict mostly emotional?

• What are the facts, and have they been confirmed?

• Are there any prejudices, perspectives, or personal biases that need to be corrected before the discussion can get to a deeper level where solutions can be found?

• What assumptions have the different people made, and are they accurate, partly accurate, or wrong?

If we will do our homework when faced with conflict, we could prevent many of the difficulties we face in ministry. At times our reaction can be worse than the action. Asking the kinds of questions (if only mentally) that I have listed will enable you to feel more confident and not impulsive as you respond to the issue.

Knowing yourself is important when trying to understand your style of handling conflict. Ask yourself:

• Am I energized or do I feel drained when changes need to happen?

• Can I live without all of the answers, or do I need to have everything under control every day?

• When there is a conflict, do I address it openly or do I ignore it, hoping it will disappear?

• When is the last time I faced a fellow Christian and talked to him or her about his or her sin and encouraged them to change their behavior?

• Am I able to hear unfair personal attacks, criticism, or gossip about me and still keep my focus on the goal of being a healthy pastor?

• Can I make a Bible-directed (Spirit-led) decision when there are other vocal opinions?

• Am I able to stand for what I know is biblically right and for God’s direction without any human certainty that I am right?

• Do I willingly and frequently say, “I do not know” or “I was wrong” when these statements were true?

All of our personalities, histories, cultures, experiences, and physical and emotional challenges are unique to us. All play a part in the way we make decisions and the way we feel. Knowing people and knowing how you act, lead, and react to people is critical.

In his book, How to Manage Conflict in the Church , Norman Shawchuck teaches about the different styles we choose to become involved in or use to respond to conflict (Shawchuck 1983). Though we might respond in more than one way, we generally choose a particular way of dealing with conflict.

Types

Goal

Outcome

1. Avoiding

Let others solve the problem

Feel frustrated; little commitment

2. Accommodating

Preserve relationships; outward cooperation at all costs

Temporary fix

3. Collaborating

Get cooperation from all parties

Stronger trust; easier to implement a solution

4. Compromising

Finds the middle of the road; looks for the common good

Half-hearted relationships; do not get to the bottom

5. Competing

To win and have control

Conformity; win / lose

As pastors and leaders we must promote healthy relationships that allow the church community to take advantage of growth opportunities in conflict situations. Conflict is part of every life. We experience it, every person in our church experiences it, and all churches experience it. We can use this natural challenge to enable us and our parishioners to be all that Christ wants them to be.

**Q:** How can you aid someone in solving substantive conflict?

**A:** Understand their spiritual condition. Understand their environment (home, work, etc.). Understand their intent (do they want resolution, etc.). Identify other influences or those adding to the conflict. Determine the individual’s desire to be transparent and open. Determine the willingness to take his share of the responsibility. Determine the “bottom-line” the person is seeking.

**Q:** What are three of the questions the pastor can ask himself or herself in determining his or her own approach to resolving conflict?

**A:** Am I energized or drained when change needs to take place? Can I live without being in complete control? Do I address conflict openly, or do I hope it will just go away? When is the last time I addressed another individual to resolve conflict? Can I make a Bible-based decision when there are other opinions being voiced? Am I able to stand for what I know is right without making the issue personal? Am I able to admit when I am wrong or when I do not know the answer to a question?

**Q:** What are the five primary ways of dealing with conflict?

**A:** Avoiding, Accommodating, Collaborating, Compromising, and Competing

**Q:** What are the goals of each approach?

**A:** Avoiding—Let someone else solve the problem; Accommodating—Preserve relationship at all costs; Collaborating—Get cooperation from other parties; Compromising—Find the middle or common ground; and Competing—Win and gain greater control

**Q:** What are the outcomes of each approach?

**A:** Avoiding—feelings of frustration for all involved; Accommodating—Outward cooperation but only a temporary fix; Collaborating—Stronger trust and easier solutions; Compromising—Half-hearted relationships that fail to solve deeper issues; Competing—Conformity and short-term victories UNIT 2

#### 2.3.2 — Identify and define the five primary ways of dealing with conflict.

As you pastor in the twenty-first century, it is critical to understand yourself: your personality type, how you think, and how you plan. Pastoring is not an easy occupation; however, it is tremendously fulfilling as you see positive growth in people, marriages healed, and people on their way to heaven. The conflict is just a small part (though it may seem large) of what we do.

Remind yourself that people desire to experience God, understand the Bible, and have healthy relationships. All people have been created in the image of God (Genesis 1:27), and Jesus Christ was involved in this act. Colossians 1:16 says, “For by him all things were created.” The Scripture informs us that God has “set eternity in the heart of men” (Ecclesiastes 3:11). Deep inside the most degraded sinner or rebellious, bitter Christian there is a hunger to know the One who has created him or her.

Also, the eternal Word of God is part of our DNA . Those who understand the truth of Jesus Christ will respond to God’s written word as it convicts, convinces, and continues to speak to their conscience. The Scripture is “God breathed and is useful for teaching, rebuking, correcting and training in righteousness, so that the man of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work” (2 Timothy 3:16). God gave us the Scripture to instruct us in how to know Him, how we can spend eternity with Him, and how to enjoy the abundant life He has for us.

People also want healthy relationships. This world is dysfunctional and complicated. Dysfunctional families began when Adam and Eve disobeyed God. This sin nature was passed on from them, so unhealthy relationships have been part of humanity from that time. It is not God’s will for people to be abused, hurt, manipulated, lied to, or injured by others. God desires us to discover healthy relationships that are pure, righteous, and trustful. However, the world simply is not that way. The enemy of our souls has brought disorder, confusion, and hatred to every culture, race, and people. These are the people we are endeavoring to reach for Christ. But we must remember that dysfunction and complication come into our churches when people arrive. Conflict, anger, power plays, and aggressive behavior are nothing new for this world or for the church. However, it still remains that people want to be healthy and have meaningful relationships.

Best selling author Rick Warren writes,

A friend once told me of a survey he took in a church. When he asked his members why they joined the church, 93 percent of them said, “I joined because of the pastor.”

Next he asked, “What if the pastor leaves? Will you leave? ‘No’ was the response from 93 percent of them. When he asked why they would not leave, the response was ‘because I have friends here!’” (Warren 2005, 1).

This allegiance is normal and healthy because people are hungry for good relationships that lead to friendships.

Lyle Schaller has done extensive research that shows that the more friendships a person has in a congregation, the less likely he or she is to become inactive or to leave. In contrast I once read about a survey of four hundred church dropouts who were asked why they left their churches. Over 75 percent of the respondents said, “I did not feel anyone cared whether I was there or not” (Warren 2005, 1 ).

Rick Warren says that it is a myth that you must know everyone in the church in order to feel like a part of a church. The average church member knows sixty-seven people in the congregation, whether the church has two hundred or two thousand attending. A member does not have to know everyone in the church in order to feel a loyalty to “my church,” but he or she does have to know some people (Warren 2005, 1).

Relationships are important to the health of individuals. Understanding how we operate when the pressure is on or when we are under the fire of conflict is also important. Knowing our typical way of doing things and understanding whether we are outgoing or a little timid helps us to deal with people and relationships.

In their book, Church Fights , Speed Leas and Paul Kittlaus write of three common kinds of conflict that churches often experience (Leas and Kittlaus, 1973):

Intrapersonal Conflict:

This refers to the struggle a person has when different parts of the self compete with each another. (I want to be a beloved pastor, but I also want to be a preacher who speaks the truth.)

Interpersonal Conflict:

These conflicts are caused by personality differences between people and are not related primarily to issues. (I think I am a strong, capable person, but the board chairperson treats me like an incompetent.)

Substantive Conflict:

These are disputes over facts, values, goals, and beliefs . (I think we should put a new roof on the church, but the social concerns committee wants to open a clothes closet for the poor.)

Knowing what kind of conflict you are dealing with and how you typically respond to that type is extremely valuable. Examining all three, whether that is intrapersonal, interpersonal, or substantive will help you determine which direction to take in addressing the person or evaluating yourself.

In evaluating the different types of conflict, learn more about whom you are dealing with and how to format your plan of mediation, or topics of discussion. To be prepared for any kind of confrontation—gentle rebuke or just to better understand their point—some questions are helpful:

Is the person willing to be totally transparent and not hide anything about his or her concern, so as to honestly evaluate the situation?

Is the person willing to own or accept his or her part of the problem?
