## 3 - The Study of Conflict

### Different Levels of Conflict Require Different Types of Responses

#### 3.1.1 — Distinguish between Leas’s five levels of conflict, and demonstrate understanding of how to address each level.

Here is a summary of what we have been saying:

• Conflict is normal.

• Conflict is the companion of change.

• Conflict can look different each time.

• The kind of conflict the church is experiencing should be determined so it can be dealt with most effectively.

Obviously the Bible often speaks of conflict. However, the day we live in seems to encourage conflict in the church or tension against spiritual leadership.

Not all questioning of spiritual leadership is wrong. In my book, Why Great Men Fall , I discuss fourteen tragic decisions leaders make that lead to their downfall (Goodall 2005). The excesses and failures of some television preachers and pastors have caused many to wonder if all ministers live this way. With the high incidence of extra biblical and unscriptural teaching that shows up on Christian television, radio, and in some pulpits, people should question what a teacher or preacher is saying. There is nothing wrong with examining the Scripture to verify if what a minister says is true. The Bereans “received the word with all readiness, and searched the Scriptures daily to find out whether these things were so” (Acts 17:11, NKJV). Also, the tragic situation in the Catholic Church where well over a thousand Catholic priests have molested children over the years has fostered mistrust of clergy.

As pastors and Christian leaders, we must be able to demonstrate that we can be trusted and are obedient to God’s Word concerning spiritual leadership in His kingdom.

We see a growing amount of good literature about different types of conflict and how to deal with it within a church, organization, or ourselves. However, some people simply do not want to resolve conflict. In fact, some thrive on unhealthy conflict and are happier when they are involved in a squabble.

Speed Leas (1985) defines five levels of conflict that a church can experience. Leas explains that with each level we respond differently, use different language, and take different action steps. The leader must determine the most helpful and effective way to respond, depending on the level of conflict.

Level 1: Where We Solve Normal Types of Problems

I recently needed to replace the carpet in a sanctuary. The fifteen-year old carpet was worn to the surface in many places, and I was unable to get it cleaned effectively because of worn spots and permanent stains. Knowing that there would be numerous opinions on the color, type, and price of carpet, I decided that the board and I would not make the decision. I knew that no-matter what kind of carpet we chose, someone would disagree. So I selected three groups from the church who were knowledgeable about color and styles for our part of the country and our type of building. I supplied them with all the information I could find on carpet dealerships and gave them the measurements, locations, and color of the paint and wallpaper in those areas. I also gave them a date when the church board would need to see their decision so they could either approve or table it for further study.

All three committees did outstanding work. Their members cared about the church and its appearance and were sensitive to budget parameters. It was interesting that all three committees picked the same color scheme and type of carpet. They were happy, and so was I.

With this information, the church board unanimously approved their decision.

My original thoughts were right. We still had some conflict, “Who made this decision about the color of the carpet?” Or, “Where did we decide to purchase the carpet? I could have gotten us a better deal” My response was—and is—“Thanks for the question. The board selected three different groups of people to serve on committees to study color, style, and price. The committees determined what was best.” The decision not to make all the decisions myself was good. We need to permit others to take the criticism on these kinds of decisions. I frequently hear about a pastor who is struggling because he or she made some small decision. I often think, “Choose to take the heat for issues that really make a difference in people’s lives.” Carpet is not one of those.

Three steps to help a committee, board, or staff come to a decision: Start by giving the decision makers all the information you can: prices, size, and date you need it. Is it in the budget? Why does the decision need to be made? This questioning is part of the information-gathering and recognition stage.

Second, discuss the information that has been provided until you feel the leaders understand and are ready to make a decision. This phase could last minutes, hours, or weeks (for further study, outside opinions, and so forth).

Third, the decision can be made. The board or committee has studied the information, discussed it thoroughly, and come to a conclusion. When that committee or board walks out of the room, they should be in agreement.

Anger can surface at the early stage (or any stage), but our focus as leaders is to negotiate a win-win decision. At this level, it is likely that it is not person-oriented, but opinion-oriented . We can usually and adequately manage differences at this level if we are sensitive, cautious, and accurate in our decisions.

Level 2: Escalates to a Disagreement

Many people want to protect their opinion and some might become guarded and attempt to protect their real feelings. On the surface they look relatively calm; however, on the inside their emotions are on edge. At this level, they may not be hostile but perhaps a little caustic or sarcastic. Normally, this is not a dangerous level and is commonly found in most churches and homes.

The wise leader understands that there will be other opinions about how we are to do things. Walking through the decision or change with caution is important and being aware of other opinions is always good. I once heard the story of a minister who was fired by his church because he moved the organ from the left side of the platform to the right side. He was shocked with their decision; however, he moved on with his life of pastoring another church. Years later, this minister visited the pastor who followed him in the church he was fired from. When he looked at the platform he saw that the organ was moved . . . to the same spot he had gotten fired for moving it years before.

The minister asked this pastor, “How did you get away with moving the organ? They fired me for doing this!”

His pastor friend said, “Very carefully, about an inch a month.”

Showing a little caution and sensitivity, as well as asking a few questions and hearing the opinions of others, is always helpful before decisions are made.

People might not be specific about their opinions, but they might use loaded generality in their comments like this: “Changing the carpet and the wallpaper? It seems like he is changing a lot of things.”

The carpet and wallpaper are specifics; however, “changing a lot of things” is a generality. At this level we can still try to find a win-win solution and often an appropriate compromise. Sometimes the decision to do something is not worth the price we would need to pay to do it. Better to walk away if you see a deeper level of conflict arising, unless it is a biblical command or conviction that would be violated.

Level 3: Where Conflict Becomes Competitive or a Contest

At this level someone wins and someone loses. One side does not particularly desire to solve the problem or come to an agreement. The objective is that one of the parties or groups wants to win and put the other person or group in its so-called proper place. Emotions are involved; harsh words are used; labels are expressed; however, the fight-and-win-no-matter-what-the-cost level has not yet arrived. Negotiation, apologies, recognition, healthy resolution, and peacemaking are still possible.

This is an awkward stage and can keep you up at night. Finding a solution where all the parties feel like they have some kind of a win is difficult. Reasoning with both sides or with people separately can take time and always needs a great deal of wisdom and temperance. As a leader, although you sense the tension, you must feel that you can calmly communicate with both sides.

Level 4: The Fight or Flight Level

This dangerous phase is where one person wants to hurt the other person or one group wants to hurt the other group. They want to get rid of, or neutralize the other person and want to punish them in the process. The opinion moves from generalizations to personal attack.

The Bible is often used ( out of context) to prove that the other’s opinion is against “God’s will.” On many occasions it is not specific biblical instruction that is the real problem; it is that a person, or group, simply wants to win and will use portions of the Bible (out of context) to prove the point(s).

At this dangerous level, churches split; pastors are released without due cause; people are deeply hurt; and the kingdom of God is injured. People who are part of this type of conflict are either going to fight or leave (and often they do not leave without a fight).

On one occasion, I experienced this with a larger man who liked to yell, threaten, and intimidate people. He did this to me, and after he was finished I calmly said, “Thank you for your opinion; however, understand that you will never be given permission to speak to me like that again.”

He was shocked by my response and grabbed his wife and said, “Come on, we are getting out of here.” When one of my board members (who had been present) talked to him a few days later and informed him that his way of communicating with the pastor was inappropriate, he said; “Well, I have done it with other pastors, and in fact, with one I shook his desk when I was talking to him.” The board member said, “That was wrong, and you will not be able to do that again.”

This man’s reply indicated that the pastor could remove him from leadership but not from church membership. He had decided he was going to win at some level and put the pastor in his place. In his own mind, he did just that. Sadly, the board had to make a decision to remove him from membership because he would not understand appropriate ways to communicate while voicing his opinion. He chose to be divisive and demonstrate his power. He was going to win and hurt the other person in the process.

Level 5: Intractable Situations Often Defined as “Conflict run Amok”

At level 4 conflict, the person or group wants to punish or get the other side removed from the organization. However, when level 5 conflict occurs, one side sets out to destroy the other individual’s reputation, or worse. At this level, they are willing to damage, destroy, or dismantle the other person or group, regardless of the cost (even to themselves) or the effort involved.

The gossip becomes fabricated lies; false accusations, extreme examples, and mean-spirited discussions are also common. The ways of communicating can be by telephone, secret meetings, and / or electronic mail sent to a hundred people in seconds.

Speed Leas says, “The first two levels are easy to work with; the third is tough; the fourth and fifth are very difficult and impossible” (Leas 1985, 16).

Conflict at levels one and two are normal in any church or organization. These levels are to be anticipated, and it is helpful to become knowledgeable of “people skills” from a variety of sources. Level three can also be handled by normal conflict resolution techniques, and the leader can be optimistic about a healthy outcome.

However, levels 4 and 5 are very different. The opposing groups are not playing by the rules of respect and love, and they are not operating at the same level. One group might be at level 1, and just want to solve the problem, but the other party is at level 3 or 4, and is very personally and emotionally involved. The second group may have made up their minds that they will win, even if they have to destroy the other in the process. The level 1 party is basically innocent, while the other is unreasonable.

This is a no-one-wins situation. Everyone loses; people are hurt; reputations are damaged; and sometimes, people do not ever get over it. The leader must recognize whom he or she is dealing with and the ways this level 4 or 5 person or group will possibly respond.

I am grateful that the majority of church conflict takes place at healthy, normal levels and the techniques used to bring solutions are easy to understand. Any pastor can apply them. The pain that can be inflicted beginning at level 3 can be anticipated, and level 4 or 5 is a difficult cross to bear. At these last two levels, people are always emotionally wounded; there are power-players involved, and church discipline has to be initiated by leadership. Most of us have either experienced this level, or we have known others who have. It is never pretty.

We should do these things toward the goal of bringing a healthy resolution:

• Respect one another as individuals or groups

• Understand the other person or group’s opinion

• Agree that the best decision is the one that will help the congregation the most and will bring the greatest good

However, if one party or group only wants to win, disregarding healthy resolution, then we have a level 4 or 5 situation.

Sadly, this is part of the world that we live in because we deal with people. We must be aware that this is a possibility when we choose the ministry or choose to be a leader. M. Scott Peck said, “I have learned nothing in twenty years that would suggest that evil people can be rapidly influenced by any means other than raw power. They do not respond, at least in the short run, to either gentle kindness or any form of spiritual persuasion with which I am familiar” (Peck 1983, 68).

Understanding what level of conflict you are experiencing will bring you wisdom in how you can choose to address it.

**Q:** What are the five levels of conflict identified by Leas?

**A:** Normal types of problems, Disagreements, Contests, Fight or Flight, and Intractable Situations

**Q:** Explain the change that comes between the second and third levels.

**A:** When conflict moves to the contest level, personalities have now taken over and the conflict ceases to revolve around the issue. Now more is at stake. Compromise is less likely because the individuals feel they must give up a part of themselves to achieve it.

**Q:** How do levels four and five differ?

**A:** While both are extremely difficult to solve, level four is characterized by a desire to bring harm to the other individual. Level five seeks an even greater level of destruction for the opponent, actually trying to ruin him or her. At level five deceptive tactics are used in a “win at all costs” mentality, while level four typically limits itself to using the facts to seek damage to one’s opponent.

**Q:** Name the three principles that must govern conflict resolution (the leader’s approach to conflicts at all levels).

**A:** Of course, no one can truly win. The leader must recognize not only his or her own level of conflict, but also the level at which the other individual is operating. Failure to ascertain this information will lead to a major breakdown. Typically, the conflict will move to the highest level either of the parties has chosen. Respect one another as individuals or groups. Understand the other person or group’s opinion. Agree that the best decision is the one that benefits the congregation and not merely either of the individual parties.

**Q:** How does the quote from M. Scott Peck affect your thoughts on conflict?

**A:** This will be a personal response. The leader must understand that there are situations where his or her best efforts will be futile. There are some who simply will not allow effective resolution, but will seek to maintain the conflict until they get what they seek.

#### 3.1.2 — Explain the three principles that must govern conflict resolution at all levels and how to proceed when these principles are neglected.

Here is a summary of what we have been saying:

• Conflict is normal.

• Conflict is the companion of change.

• Conflict can look different each time.

• The kind of conflict the church is experiencing should be determined so it can be dealt with most effectively.

Obviously the Bible often speaks of conflict. However, the day we live in seems to encourage conflict in the church or tension against spiritual leadership.

Not all questioning of spiritual leadership is wrong. In my book, Why Great Men Fall , I discuss fourteen tragic decisions leaders make that lead to their downfall (Goodall 2005). The excesses and failures of some television preachers and pastors have caused many to wonder if all ministers live this way. With the high incidence of extra biblical and unscriptural teaching that shows up on Christian television, radio, and in some pulpits, people should question what a teacher or preacher is saying. There is nothing wrong with examining the Scripture to verify if what a minister says is true. The Bereans “received the word with all readiness, and searched the Scriptures daily to find out whether these things were so” (Acts 17:11, NKJV). Also, the tragic situation in the Catholic Church where well over a thousand Catholic priests have molested children over the years has fostered mistrust of clergy.

As pastors and Christian leaders, we must be able to demonstrate that we can be trusted and are obedient to God’s Word concerning spiritual leadership in His kingdom.

We see a growing amount of good literature about different types of conflict and how to deal with it within a church, organization, or ourselves. However, some people simply do not want to resolve conflict. In fact, some thrive on unhealthy conflict and are happier when they are involved in a squabble.

Speed Leas (1985) defines five levels of conflict that a church can experience. Leas explains that with each level we respond differently, use different language, and take different action steps. The leader must determine the most helpful and effective way to respond, depending on the level of conflict.

Level 1: Where We Solve Normal Types of Problems

I recently needed to replace the carpet in a sanctuary. The fifteen-year old carpet was worn to the surface in many places, and I was unable to get it cleaned effectively because of worn spots and permanent stains. Knowing that there would be numerous opinions on the color, type, and price of carpet, I decided that the board and I would not make the decision. I knew that no-matter what kind of carpet we chose, someone would disagree. So I selected three groups from the church who were knowledgeable about color and styles for our part of the country and our type of building. I supplied them with all the information I could find on carpet dealerships and gave them the measurements, locations, and color of the paint and wallpaper in those areas. I also gave them a date when the church board would need to see their decision so they could either approve or table it for further study.

All three committees did outstanding work. Their members cared about the church and its appearance and were sensitive to budget parameters. It was interesting that all three committees picked the same color scheme and type of carpet. They were happy, and so was I.

With this information, the church board unanimously approved their decision.

My original thoughts were right. We still had some conflict, “Who made this decision about the color of the carpet?” Or, “Where did we decide to purchase the carpet? I could have gotten us a better deal” My response was—and is—“Thanks for the question. The board selected three different groups of people to serve on committees to study color, style, and price. The committees determined what was best.” The decision not to make all the decisions myself was good. We need to permit others to take the criticism on these kinds of decisions. I frequently hear about a pastor who is struggling because he or she made some small decision. I often think, “Choose to take the heat for issues that really make a difference in people’s lives.” Carpet is not one of those.

Three steps to help a committee, board, or staff come to a decision: Start by giving the decision makers all the information you can: prices, size, and date you need it. Is it in the budget? Why does the decision need to be made? This questioning is part of the information-gathering and recognition stage.

Second, discuss the information that has been provided until you feel the leaders understand and are ready to make a decision. This phase could last minutes, hours, or weeks (for further study, outside opinions, and so forth).

Third, the decision can be made. The board or committee has studied the information, discussed it thoroughly, and come to a conclusion. When that committee or board walks out of the room, they should be in agreement.

Anger can surface at the early stage (or any stage), but our focus as leaders is to negotiate a win-win decision. At this level, it is likely that it is not person-oriented, but opinion-oriented . We can usually and adequately manage differences at this level if we are sensitive, cautious, and accurate in our decisions.

Level 2: Escalates to a Disagreement

Many people want to protect their opinion and some might become guarded and attempt to protect their real feelings. On the surface they look relatively calm; however, on the inside their emotions are on edge. At this level, they may not be hostile but perhaps a little caustic or sarcastic. Normally, this is not a dangerous level and is commonly found in most churches and homes.

The wise leader understands that there will be other opinions about how we are to do things. Walking through the decision or change with caution is important and being aware of other opinions is always good. I once heard the story of a minister who was fired by his church because he moved the organ from the left side of the platform to the right side. He was shocked with their decision; however, he moved on with his life of pastoring another church. Years later, this minister visited the pastor who followed him in the church he was fired from. When he looked at the platform he saw that the organ was moved . . . to the same spot he had gotten fired for moving it years before.

The minister asked this pastor, “How did you get away with moving the organ? They fired me for doing this!”

His pastor friend said, “Very carefully, about an inch a month.”

Showing a little caution and sensitivity, as well as asking a few questions and hearing the opinions of others, is always helpful before decisions are made.

People might not be specific about their opinions, but they might use loaded generality in their comments like this: “Changing the carpet and the wallpaper? It seems like he is changing a lot of things.”

The carpet and wallpaper are specifics; however, “changing a lot of things” is a generality. At this level we can still try to find a win-win solution and often an appropriate compromise. Sometimes the decision to do something is not worth the price we would need to pay to do it. Better to walk away if you see a deeper level of conflict arising, unless it is a biblical command or conviction that would be violated.

Level 3: Where Conflict Becomes Competitive or a Contest

At this level someone wins and someone loses. One side does not particularly desire to solve the problem or come to an agreement. The objective is that one of the parties or groups wants to win and put the other person or group in its so-called proper place. Emotions are involved; harsh words are used; labels are expressed; however, the fight-and-win-no-matter-what-the-cost level has not yet arrived. Negotiation, apologies, recognition, healthy resolution, and peacemaking are still possible.

This is an awkward stage and can keep you up at night. Finding a solution where all the parties feel like they have some kind of a win is difficult. Reasoning with both sides or with people separately can take time and always needs a great deal of wisdom and temperance. As a leader, although you sense the tension, you must feel that you can calmly communicate with both sides.

Level 4: The Fight or Flight Level

This dangerous phase is where one person wants to hurt the other person or one group wants to hurt the other group. They want to get rid of, or neutralize the other person and want to punish them in the process. The opinion moves from generalizations to personal attack.

The Bible is often used ( out of context) to prove that the other’s opinion is against “God’s will.” On many occasions it is not specific biblical instruction that is the real problem; it is that a person, or group, simply wants to win and will use portions of the Bible (out of context) to prove the point(s).

At this dangerous level, churches split; pastors are released without due cause; people are deeply hurt; and the kingdom of God is injured. People who are part of this type of conflict are either going to fight or leave (and often they do not leave without a fight).

On one occasion, I experienced this with a larger man who liked to yell, threaten, and intimidate people. He did this to me, and after he was finished I calmly said, “Thank you for your opinion; however, understand that you will never be given permission to speak to me like that again.”

He was shocked by my response and grabbed his wife and said, “Come on, we are getting out of here.” When one of my board members (who had been present) talked to him a few days later and informed him that his way of communicating with the pastor was inappropriate, he said; “Well, I have done it with other pastors, and in fact, with one I shook his desk when I was talking to him.” The board member said, “That was wrong, and you will not be able to do that again.”

This man’s reply indicated that the pastor could remove him from leadership but not from church membership. He had decided he was going to win at some level and put the pastor in his place. In his own mind, he did just that. Sadly, the board had to make a decision to remove him from membership because he would not understand appropriate ways to communicate while voicing his opinion. He chose to be divisive and demonstrate his power. He was going to win and hurt the other person in the process.

Level 5: Intractable Situations Often Defined as “Conflict run Amok”

At level 4 conflict, the person or group wants to punish or get the other side removed from the organization. However, when level 5 conflict occurs, one side sets out to destroy the other individual’s reputation, or worse. At this level, they are willing to damage, destroy, or dismantle the other person or group, regardless of the cost (even to themselves) or the effort involved.

The gossip becomes fabricated lies; false accusations, extreme examples, and mean-spirited discussions are also common. The ways of communicating can be by telephone, secret meetings, and / or electronic mail sent to a hundred people in seconds.

Speed Leas says, “The first two levels are easy to work with; the third is tough; the fourth and fifth are very difficult and impossible” (Leas 1985, 16).

Conflict at levels one and two are normal in any church or organization. These levels are to be anticipated, and it is helpful to become knowledgeable of “people skills” from a variety of sources. Level three can also be handled by normal conflict resolution techniques, and the leader can be optimistic about a healthy outcome.

However, levels 4 and 5 are very different. The opposing groups are not playing by the rules of respect and love, and they are not operating at the same level. One group might be at level 1, and just want to solve the problem, but the other party is at level 3 or 4, and is very personally and emotionally involved. The second group may have made up their minds that they will win, even if they have to destroy the other in the process. The level 1 party is basically innocent, while the other is unreasonable.

This is a no-one-wins situation. Everyone loses; people are hurt; reputations are damaged; and sometimes, people do not ever get over it. The leader must recognize whom he or she is dealing with and the ways this level 4 or 5 person or group will possibly respond.

I am grateful that the majority of church conflict takes place at healthy, normal levels and the techniques used to bring solutions are easy to understand. Any pastor can apply them. The pain that can be inflicted beginning at level 3 can be anticipated, and level 4 or 5 is a difficult cross to bear. At these last two levels, people are always emotionally wounded; there are power-players involved, and church discipline has to be initiated by leadership. Most of us have either experienced this level, or we have known others who have. It is never pretty.

We should do these things toward the goal of bringing a healthy resolution:

• Respect one another as individuals or groups

• Understand the other person or group’s opinion

• Agree that the best decision is the one that will help the congregation the most and will bring the greatest good

However, if one party or group only wants to win, disregarding healthy resolution, then we have a level 4 or 5 situation.

Sadly, this is part of the world that we live in because we deal with people. We must be aware that this is a possibility when we choose the ministry or choose to be a leader. M. Scott Peck said, “I have learned nothing in twenty years that would suggest that evil people can be rapidly influenced by any means other than raw power. They do not respond, at least in the short run, to either gentle kindness or any form of spiritual persuasion with which I am familiar” (Peck 1983, 68).

Understanding what level of conflict you are experiencing will bring you wisdom in how you can choose to address it.

**Q:** What are the five levels of conflict identified by Leas?

**A:** Normal types of problems, Disagreements, Contests, Fight or Flight, and Intractable Situations

**Q:** Explain the change that comes between the second and third levels.

**A:** When conflict moves to the contest level, personalities have now taken over and the conflict ceases to revolve around the issue. Now more is at stake. Compromise is less likely because the individuals feel they must give up a part of themselves to achieve it.

**Q:** How do levels four and five differ?

**A:** While both are extremely difficult to solve, level four is characterized by a desire to bring harm to the other individual. Level five seeks an even greater level of destruction for the opponent, actually trying to ruin him or her. At level five deceptive tactics are used in a “win at all costs” mentality, while level four typically limits itself to using the facts to seek damage to one’s opponent.

**Q:** Name the three principles that must govern conflict resolution (the leader’s approach to conflicts at all levels).

**A:** Of course, no one can truly win. The leader must recognize not only his or her own level of conflict, but also the level at which the other individual is operating. Failure to ascertain this information will lead to a major breakdown. Typically, the conflict will move to the highest level either of the parties has chosen. Respect one another as individuals or groups. Understand the other person or group’s opinion. Agree that the best decision is the one that benefits the congregation and not merely either of the individual parties.

**Q:** How does the quote from M. Scott Peck affect your thoughts on conflict?

**A:** This will be a personal response. The leader must understand that there are situations where his or her best efforts will be futile. There are some who simply will not allow effective resolution, but will seek to maintain the conflict until they get what they seek.

### Understanding Basic Conflict Issues

#### 3.2.1 — Identify the foundation for conflict contained in the essence of ministry.

It is helpful to understand that the Scriptures give plenty of information about how to deal with conflict. Glenn Penner (2005) writes, “The Bible (especially the New Testament) was written by persecuted believers to persecuted believers. This context cannot be ignored without it having profoundly negative implications for how we read and apply the Bible and how we follow Christ individually and corporately. A cross-centered gospel requires cross-centered messengers.”

One such example is found in 1 Corinthians 4:1 . “Let a man so consider us, as servants of Christ and stewards of the mysteries of God” (NKJV). In context, Paul was speaking of the leaders, apostles, famous speakers, and pastors who served the church in Corinth. People were comparing them against one another and picking their favorite preacher. This is common today and, as it did during that time, can cause confusion in the body of Christ. Paul addresses this issue by saying, we are huperetas , “servants,” a word that typically concerns having the attitude necessary to get the job done. However, when Paul wrote this instruction, the word was also used in the classical Greek culture to describe the punishment that criminals had to endure. Criminals were considered outcasts of society and were frequently sentenced to row in the belly of a ship, and they were often required to live below deck, as well. They were the engines that pushed the ships ahead; huperetas were called under-rowers .

This is the same word that the apostle Paul uses to describe the attitude that is necessary for those called to spiritual leadership. Ministry, servanthood, and spiritual leadership are difficult. However, these roles are also fulfilling because we are to understand the presence of God in our lives and the unique peace that Christ bestows when we faithfully do His will. Conflict is persecution, and persecution is conflict—and it is never easy.

Ministry is not a party; it is about pulling people from hell’s grasp and introducing them to heaven’s hope. Ministry is about taking broken lives, full of pain, and bringing healing and peace. Ministry is about helping immature people of all ages grow into mature saints who are serious about God’s kingdom. You can see how this often sets up the minister for criticism, cynicism, and conflict. If we do not understand what ministry is about, we need to reconsider the kind of work we do. It is not easy, but it is eternally rewarding.

Normal Conflict Principles

Difficult people are in every church.

People come from numerous backgrounds; have different personalities, family histories, and experiences. All people are at different levels of spiritual growth and many react to life’s difficulties out of emotional pain.

People can also change their minds about their Christian commitment and become angry or bitter because of numerous reasons. In Colossians, the apostle Paul mentions Demas along with “Luke the beloved physician” (Colossians 4:14), and in 2 Timothy, Paul again mentions Demas, only this time as someone who “has forsaken me, having loved this present world” (4:10). It is tragic, but true, that every pastor will experience what Paul experienced. People who once followed the Lord and even served as leaders may decide to turn away from their commitment to Christ. Often, they decide to be difficult in the process. Other people can be difficult when they sincerely want help. With this second group, we need to be understanding and patient and seek ways to assist them.

Focus on those who are supportive.

Though you may have two families who are unhappy, you also have thirty families that are happy. I do not know of any church where all the people are happy with everything all the time. When reading the gospels, note whether Jesus had 100 percent of His followers in agreement with Him all of the time. At one point, Peter disagreed with Him about washing his feet, Phillip questioned His desire to feed the masses of hungry people, and often we see that Judas’ heart was not with the Lord’s.

Many times someone will disagree with you; however, try to keep your focus on the supportive majority. As much as possible, try not to become distracted with the few. Our human natures forget the compliments but remember the criticisms. All of us have had Sundays where we felt our message was not the best. On most weeks our people express their gratitude through a handshake or kind comment. At times we hear a criticism or possibly more than one. You have a majority of people who need and appreciate your attention and focus. Do not let a few take your valuable time and energy.

Ask yourself, “What are my critics saying?”

As a leader you will need to discern between a member who is offering valid criticism and one who has become, or is becoming, a destructive force in the church. We should not be afraid of criticism. Constructive criticism is our friend. It helps us improve, fix what is broken, or adjust something that has gotten a little off track. However, a destructive force is different. This opposition is not satisfied when we adjust or fix a particular program, person, or issue. Here, people want to injure and cause damage to others. The aggressors lack sensitivity and understanding of people, and they want to be right more than they want to be holy.

Valid criticism from a sincere person who wants to help is a wonderful gift. Many view all criticism as negative, but it is not. On the other hand, a destructive force needs to be stopped. It is better to stop this kind of behavior sooner, rather than later.

Understanding the difference between an aggressive person and a divisive person is necessary for all leaders. We need to realize that some of the best ideas for the health of our church may come from aggressive leaders. Also, we must understand that disunity often comes from people who are acting autonomously and are not obeying the Scripture.

Pastors need sensitive calluses.

Vance Havner said that a preacher’s “biggest problem is how to toughen his hide without hardening his heart” (Preaching-Qualifications). The criticism and the stress of spiritual leadership can get to any leader. Ministry is hard work, but we must remain tender in our hearts while we deal with the challenges of those we are called to serve. Even sophisticated, educated people can have fragile lives. Being sensitive to their needs is critical because without sensitivity we cannot minister. However, with some issues we need to develop calluses, so we do not let things or people get to us. We cannot change everyone’s mind; we will not have 100 percent cooperation all the time, and thankfully, we are not responsible to change people’s behavior. We do our best with a loving, godly attitude and maintain a tender heart while we “toughen our hide”—so the negative people do not get to us.

Why Can We Not Get Through This Conflict

It is important to understand the details on both sides of the issue. A good attorney understands both sides of the argument. We encounter many times when we need to do our homework and try to find some historical reasons (past hurts and conflicts) that surround a particular challenge. Taking time to study the issues and think about the personalities involved is critical. In doing this, we will develop understanding and wisdom concerning how to become involved.

Second, figure out who the person is with the most influence in the difficult group. If possible, that is the person we need to deal with. Sometimes a conversation works, and sometimes a confrontation will be necessary. However, before we confront a person, we should ask ourselves these four questions:

1. Is this issue worth standing up for? Is it an issue that is so important that we need to address it? Some issues are not that important, and it would be better if we let them go.

2. Do I have a personal problem or personality difference with one of the parties? Many times, pastors and leaders have difficulty with people because they are very different from themselves in personality. Maybe the pastor would not have done the project exactly the way the person has chosen to do it. Again, if one of these is the case, it is prudent to let it go and realize that he or she is different than you and the job is getting done even if it is not exactly the way you would do it.

3. Do I have my own agenda? Is this difference in opinion something between the person and me, or will their behavior affect the entire congregation? If it is only a personal difference and the church is not affected, then often the best way to deal with the situation is to choose not to confront the person. However, if it does affect the church, is an unbiblical behavior, or is a conscience issue, then confrontation is necessary.

Assume the Best about Those Who Have Other Opinions

As pastors and leaders we are to show respect and appreciation to people who have opinions and sincerely want to help. The person who disagrees might not be divisive or a dissenter. Listening to another’s opinion and evaluating his or her idea is a good thing.

4. When we have talked to the person or group and have settled the issue, then we need to forget it. It is common to remember the difficulty and not put it behind us. We should not continue to bring up the issue—let it go.

How can we lead people who do not want to be led? This problem could arise over something as simple as our decision to change the color of the carpet, adjust the times for our worship services, or change the format of the bulletin. There are sentimental items, programs, and memories that people have in any congregation. Being sensitive to these issues is wise. As leaders, sometimes we decide to fix something without thinking about whom it will affect or how long that program or item has been there. We can impulsively decide to do something out of innocence and discover that many people have a different opinion.

We should consider whether we are trying to change many things at the same time. Remember that change equals conflict most of the time. It is better to address one issue (or change) at a time, then move on to the next, all the while being sensitive to the needs of people. Too much change, too quickly, will cause some to wonder if you can be trusted to lead.

Even when we have people who do not want to be led, we need to lead with God’s strength and be full of His grace. At times pastors must act on their best instincts and leave the results to God. We need to remain optimistic in crisis and not run when conflict happens. While we lean toward the side of God’s grace and mercy, we also can be bold in our leadership.

The Predictable Times of Conflict

There are many times, events, or leadership decisions that will cause concern or conflict in any church or ministry. A few are Christmas, Easter, the Fourth of July, and other special holidays. Some have emotional difficulty with holidays; some are very involved in the events of the season and are tired, and some do not like the special emphasis we put on these seasons. Following are examples of other moments that could create conflict.

During stewardship campaigns or special requests for money: Talking about finances (asking for more) causes tension with many.

The addition or departure of pastoral staff: When a pastoral staff person leaves, we need to remember that relationships have been formed, and many in the congregation often feel deep appreciation. When new pastoral staff joins the leadership team, they also bring new ideas that some may find disagreeable.

When we quickly change our leadership style: It is a common mistake for pastors when they go to a conference, read a book, or talk to someone new, that they radically change their leadership style. Remember this analogy: “Turning a large ship takes a wide circle.”

When we leave on vacation: At times people with wrong agendas will choose to voice their opinion and cause a ruckus while we are gone.

When the church is trying to reach all cultures, ages, and socioeconomic groups and trying to find a balance between the traditional and the contemporary: Trying to reach all people and all ages while being sensitive to various preferences will cause conflict with some.

When we complete a building program: A significant percentage of pastors resign or are released after a building program has been completed. Part of the reason is that the pastor chooses to be too involved in building and raising finances and neglects his or her pastoral responsibilities. Often, he or she burns out and chooses to leave.

When there is a loss in membership: A loss of membership can occur for many reasons. People move; the economic culture of the city changes (less jobs), or perhaps the leader is addressing issues that need to be addressed. This loss can cause some to complain and disagree; however, this is normal.

When there is an increase in membership: New people mean new ideas and, hopefully, more finances and more leaders to do the work. This can cause difficulty with some who had control of areas or who have lost their favorite seat during worship services.

Conflict is normal with leadership. Understanding that we can predict conflict with certain events, seasons, or changes in our church will help us appropriately deal with it when it comes.

**Q:** Explain the attitude a minister must possess to serve the church effectively.

**A:** A minister must recognize his or her role as a servant if he or she is to survive long in ministry. Those who seek their own way or desire to control will often experience the highest levels of conflict. Only by pursuing the mind and attitude of Christ can a minister act effectively in situations of conflict.

**Q:** What are some of the reasons the work of ministry can be ripe for conflict?

**A:** The nature of ministry is turning people from the destruction of sin and introducing them to the principles of new life. People come from numerous backgrounds and have different personalities, histories and experiences. They can be expected to act differently in any situation. People can change their minds concerning Christian commitment.

**Q:** What steps can a pastor take to manage conflict effectively?

**A:** Focus on those who are supportive. While those in conflict need a level of attention, dwelling on such situations often cause them to seem larger than they truly are. Ask “What are my critics saying?” Seek the wisdom of such opinions, looking for what may be accurate and can be helpful for your life. Seek “sensitive calluses.” Assume the best about those with differing opinions.

**Q:** Make a list of those occasions where conflict in a church is most likely.

**A:** During special events; Addition or departure of pastoral staff; Changes in leadership style; When a pastor takes vacation; When a church seeks to reach new people groups; Upon completion of a building program or other major initiative; Increase or Loss in membership

**Q:** Why is conflict possible when there is an increase in membership?

**A:** New people bring new ideas and more potential leaders. While this is what the pastor seeks, those in other places of leadership can feel threatened or a sense of losing the level of control they have gained.

#### 3.2.2 — Recognize and be prepared for local church situations that can bring conflict.

It is helpful to understand that the Scriptures give plenty of information about how to deal with conflict. Glenn Penner (2005) writes, “The Bible (especially the New Testament) was written by persecuted believers to persecuted believers. This context cannot be ignored without it having profoundly negative implications for how we read and apply the Bible and how we follow Christ individually and corporately. A cross-centered gospel requires cross-centered messengers.”

One such example is found in 1 Corinthians 4:1 . “Let a man so consider us, as servants of Christ and stewards of the mysteries of God” (NKJV). Ministry is about helping immature people of all ages grow into mature saints who are serious about God’s kingdom. You can see how this often sets up the minister for criticism, cynicism, and conflict. If we do not understand what ministry is about, we need to reconsider the kind of work we do. It is not easy, but it is eternally rewarding.

Normal Conflict Principles

Difficult people are in every church.

People come from numerous backgrounds; have different personalities, family histories, and experiences. All people are at different levels of spiritual growth and many react to life’s difficulties out of emotional pain.

People can also change their minds about their Christian commitment and become angry or bitter because of numerous reasons. In Colossians, the apostle Paul mentions Demas along with “Luke the beloved physician” (Colossians 4:14), and in 2 Timothy, Paul again mentions Demas, only this time as someone who “has forsaken me, having loved this present world” (4:10). It is tragic, but true, that every pastor will experience what Paul experienced. People who once followed the Lord and even served as leaders may decide to turn away from their commitment to Christ. Often, they decide to be difficult in the process. Other people can be difficult when they sincerely want help. Though you may have two families who are unhappy, you also have thirty families that are happy. I do not know of any church where all the people are happy with everything all the time. When reading the gospels, note whether Jesus had 100 percent of His followers in agreement with Him all of the time. At one point, Peter disagreed with Him about washing his feet, Phillip questioned His desire to feed the masses of hungry people, and often we see that Judas’ heart was not with the Lord’s.

Many times someone will disagree with you; however, try to keep your focus on the supportive majority. As much as possible, try not to become distracted with the few. Our human natures forget the compliments but remember the criticisms. All of us have had Sundays where we felt our message was not the best. On most weeks our people express their gratitude through a handshake or kind comment. At times we hear a criticism or possibly more than one. You have a majority of people who need and appreciate your attention and focus. Do not let a few take your valuable time and energy.

Ask yourself, “What are my critics saying?”

As a leader you will need to discern between a member who is offering valid criticism and one who has become, or is becoming, a destructive force in the church. We should not be afraid of criticism. Constructive criticism is our friend. It helps us improve, fix what is broken, or adjust something that has gotten a little off track. However, a destructive force is different. This opposition is not satisfied when we adjust or fix a particular program, person, or issue. Here, people want to injure and cause damage to others. The aggressors lack sensitivity and understanding of people, and they want to be right more than they want to be holy.

Valid criticism from a sincere person who wants to help is a wonderful gift. Many view all criticism as negative, but it is not. On the other hand, a destructive force needs to be stopped. It is better to stop this kind of behavior sooner, rather than later.

Understanding the difference between an aggressive person and a divisive person is necessary for all leaders. We need to realize that some of the best ideas for the health of our church may come from aggressive leaders. Also, we must understand that disunity often comes from people who are acting autonomously and are not obeying the Scripture.

Pastors need sensitive calluses.

Vance Havner said that a preacher’s “biggest problem is how to toughen his hide without hardening his heart” (Preaching-Qualifications). The criticism and the stress of spiritual leadership can get to any leader. Ministry is hard work, but we must remain tender in our hearts while we deal with the challenges of those we are called to serve. Even sophisticated, educated people can have fragile lives. Being sensitive to their needs is critical because without sensitivity we cannot minister. However, with some issues we need to develop calluses, so we do not let things or people get to us. We cannot change everyone’s mind; we will not have 100 percent cooperation all the time, and thankfully, we are not responsible to change people’s behavior. We do our best with a loving, godly attitude and maintain a tender heart while we “toughen our hide”—so the negative people do not get to us.

Why Can We Not Get Through This Conflict

It is important to understand the details on both sides of the issue. A good attorney understands both sides of the argument. We encounter many times when we need to do our homework and try to find some historical reasons (past hurts and conflicts) that surround a particular challenge. Taking time to study the issues and think about the personalities involved is critical. In doing this, we will develop understanding and wisdom concerning how to become involved.

Second, figure out who the person is with the most influence in the difficult group. If possible, that is the person we need to deal with. Sometimes a conversation works, and sometimes a confrontation will be necessary. However, before we confront a person, we should ask ourselves these four questions:

1. Is this issue worth standing up for? Is it an issue that is so important that we need to address it? Some issues are not that important, and it would be better if we let them go.

2. Do I have a personal problem or personality difference with one of the parties? Many times, pastors and leaders have difficulty with people because they are very different from themselves in personality. Maybe the pastor would not have done the project exactly the way the person has chosen to do it. Again, if one of these is the case, it is prudent to let it go and realize that he or she is different than you and the job is getting done even if it is not exactly the way you would do it.

3. Do I have my own agenda? Is this difference in opinion something between the person and me, or will their behavior affect the entire congregation? If it is only a personal difference and the church is not affected, then often the best way to deal with the situation is to choose not to confront the person. However, if it does affect the church, is an unbiblical behavior, or is a conscience issue, then confrontation is necessary.

Assume the Best about Those Who Have Other Opinions

As pastors and leaders we are to show respect and appreciation to people who have opinions and sincerely want to help. The person who disagrees might not be divisive or a dissenter. Listening to another’s opinion and evaluating his or her idea is a good thing.

4. When we have talked to the person or group and have settled the issue, then we need to forget it. It is common to remember the difficulty and not put it behind us. We should not continue to bring up the issue—let it go.

How can we lead people who do not want to be led? This problem could arise over something as simple as our decision to change the color of the carpet, adjust the times for our worship services, or change the format of the bulletin. There are sentimental items, programs, and memories that people have in any congregation. Being sensitive to these issues is wise. As leaders, sometimes we decide to fix something without thinking about whom it will affect or how long that program or item has been there. We can impulsively decide to do something out of innocence and discover that many people have a different opinion.

We should consider whether we are trying to change many things at the same time. Remember that change equals conflict most of the time. It is better to address one issue (or change) at a time, then move on to the next, all the while being sensitive to the needs of people. Too much change, too quickly, will cause some to wonder if you can be trusted to lead.

Even when we have people who do not want to be led, we need to lead with God’s strength and be full of His grace. At times pastors must act on their best instincts and leave the results to God. We need to remain optimistic in crisis and not run when conflict happens. While we lean toward the side of God’s grace and mercy, we also can be bold in our leadership.

The Predictable Times of Conflict

There are many times, events, or leadership decisions that will cause concern or conflict in any church or ministry. A few are Christmas, Easter, the Fourth of July, and other special holidays. Some have emotional difficulty with holidays; some are very involved in the events of the season and are tired, and some do not like the special emphasis we put on these seasons. Following are examples of other moments that could create conflict.

During stewardship campaigns or special requests for money: Talking about finances (asking for more) causes tension with many.

The addition or departure of pastoral staff: When a pastoral staff person leaves, we need to remember that relationships have been formed, and many in the congregation often feel deep appreciation. When new pastoral staff joins the leadership team, they also bring new ideas that some may find disagreeable.

When we quickly change our leadership style: It is a common mistake for pastors when they go to a conference, read a book, or talk to someone new, that they radically change their leadership style. Remember this analogy: “Turning a large ship takes a wide circle.”

When we leave on vacation: At times people with wrong agendas will choose to voice their opinion and cause a ruckus while we are gone.

When the church is trying to reach all cultures, ages, and socioeconomic groups and trying to find a balance between the traditional and the contemporary: Trying to reach all people and all ages while being sensitive to various preferences will cause conflict with some.

When we complete a building program: A significant percentage of pastors resign or are released after a building program has been completed. Part of the reason is that the pastor chooses to be too involved in building and raising finances and neglects his or her pastoral responsibilities. Often, he or she burns out and chooses to leave.

When there is a loss in membership: A loss of membership can occur for many reasons. People move; the economic culture of the city changes (less jobs), or perhaps the leader is addressing issues that need to be addressed. This loss can cause some to complain and disagree; however, this is normal.

When there is an increase in membership: New people mean new ideas and, hopefully, more finances and more leaders to do the work. This can cause difficulty with some who had control of areas or who have lost their favorite seat during worship services.

Conflict is normal with leadership. Understanding that we can predict conflict with certain events, seasons, or changes in our church will help us appropriately deal with it when it comes.

**Q:** Explain the attitude a minister must possess to serve the church effectively.

**A:** A minister must recognize his or her role as a servant if he or she is to survive long in ministry. Those who seek their own way or desire to control will often experience the highest levels of conflict. Only by pursuing the mind and attitude of Christ can a minister act effectively in situations of conflict.

**Q:** What are some of the reasons the work of ministry can be ripe for conflict?

**A:** The nature of ministry is turning people from the destruction of sin and introducing them to the principles of new life. People come from numerous backgrounds and have different personalities, histories and experiences. They can be expected to act differently in any situation. People can change their minds concerning Christian commitment.

**Q:** What steps can a pastor take to manage conflict effectively?

**A:** Focus on those who are supportive. While those in conflict need a level of attention, dwelling on such situations often cause them to seem larger than they truly are. Ask “What are my critics saying?” Seek the wisdom of such opinions, looking for what may be accurate and can be helpful for your life. Seek “sensitive calluses.” Assume the best about those with differing opinions.

**Q:** Make a list of those occasions where conflict in a church is most likely.

**A:** During special events; Addition or departure of pastoral staff; Changes in leadership style; When a pastor takes vacation; When a church seeks to reach new people groups; Upon completion of a building program or other major initiative; Increase or Loss in membership

**Q:** Why is conflict possible when there is an increase in membership?

**A:** New people bring new ideas and more potential leaders. While this is what the pastor seeks, those in other places of leadership can feel threatened or a sense of losing the level of control they have gained.

### Understanding You and What You Do

#### 3.3.1 — Identify the emotional challenges that are a regular part of ministry life and how they join with cultural influences in adding to the potential for conflict.

“For wisdom will enter your heart, and knowledge will be pleasant to your soul. Discretion will protect you, and understanding will guard you. Wisdom will save you from the ways of wicked men whose words are perverse, who leave the straight paths to walk in dark ways” (Proverbs 2:10–13). We live in a complicated world, and our churches have people with complicated lives. Our calling and chosen occupation is not a career with a predictable job description. We could be called to address a variety of crises in someone’s life at any moment. Someone within the congregation could have an affair, and we could be asked to do emergency marriage counseling. We are responsible to comfort the dying and to offer grace to those who have lost their loved one. The budget, finances, building, support staff, lay leaders, and surrounding community all offer potential changes or challenges in the way we do what we do. Then, we have people’s personalities, their motivations, likes and dislikes, opinions of us and the previous pastor, and the way they think we should do our job. When evaluating this and more, many men and women are re-evaluating whether they want to go into the ministry for an occupation. Fear, questions about the future, personal emotional health concerns, and financial issues, often stop quality and qualified people from entering or continuing in the ministry. The writer of Proverbs reminds us of a key to taking the complication out of whatever we do in life. Wisdom, discretion, and understanding are words that stand out in Proverbs 2:10–13. We need these traits as we discover who we are and how we do what we do. We need this as we try to understand the congregation we have been called to serve. Our understanding of the culture in which we live, the congregation in which we have been called, and our realistic knowledge of our personality is all part of evaluating whether we fit into a church situation. Without this, many pastors become disillusioned and discouraged with their ministry and either leave or are fired. Recent research has discovered that clergy are leaving the ministry in record numbers. One-fourth of all pastors have experienced a forced termination in some time of their ministry (Schilling 1997). The Southern Baptist Convention reports that twenty-three of their pastors are terminated each week (Southern Baptist Convention 2001–2006). Many who are terminated decide to discontinue their career in ministry; 10 percent of those forced out do not return (LaRue 1996). The Baptists also reported that during an eighteen-month period ending in 1989, some 2,100 pastors were fired, which at that time was a 31 percent increase since 1984. Most denominations do not easily give this kind of information, for obvious reasons.

The Culture America generally asks, “What can the church do for me?” not “What can I do for the church?” This consumerism that is part of the fabric of our culture is also part of the church in which we serve. The people who sit in the pew are part of culture. They are all at different levels of growth in their lives. Some are not Christians, some are carnal, some are new Christians, and some are believers who are full of wisdom and are praying for and supporting you. The consumer mentality is at times selfish and is generally not loyal or interested in the long-term relationships necessary for a church’s health. Many of our parishioners overlook the fact that most pastors work between sixty and seventy hours a week. Some pastors are bi-vocational, and some have difficulty making ends meet because of their income. Parishioners sometimes forget that pastors have responsibilities as husbands and fathers, or wives and mothers. Pastors have competitive factors that they often face such as the number of people who are looking at the church’s numbers and its progress. They then compare your church to the one down the street and you to the minister on television. At one church that I served, we enjoyed a two-year growth pattern when everyone seemed excited. Truth be told, I did not understand all the reasons the church was growing; however, during that time we were able to pay off bills, add parking, and staff, and begin to feel the financial burden lift. One of my board members was particularly happy about the turn around and frequently complimented me. One day I asked him, “What would you feel about me if our church were not growing?” He said, “Well we would look at it for a year or two, then probably make a pastoral change.” The answer surprised me, until I thought of consumer mentality. That is what he would have done in his business; however, the church (while it disciplines itself to excellent business practices) is not a business. People choose to come to a church or leave a church for hundreds of reasons. Understanding that the surrounding culture has become deeply woven into most of our churches is key to knowing how to do what we do. This is the culture we have been called to serve. Knowing its worldview helps us minister within it and communicate a biblical worldview.

The Congregation Congregations are often guilty of projecting unrealistic responsibilities on their pastor. This is neither biblical nor healthy. George Barna has often written that many congregations expect pastors to be available night and day and to fulfill as many as seventeen to twenty roles with excellence. This is one of the reasons why many pastors go through a burnout at some point in their lives. The Assemblies of God enjoys what we call church autonomy within our sovereign churches. Churches that are not sovereign typically are new church plants or congregations with less than fifty members (and thereby under the direction of their district). Most of our sovereign churches would be considered free churches where decisions whether to hire and fire the pastor rests with the members and not with the denominational officials. At times, pastors are vulnerable to assault by small but committed factions of critics. These assaults are not necessarily because of poor performance or scandalous behavior, but due to conflicts that have surfaced from unhealthy congregational dynamics. These conflicts eventually target the pastor who, although innocent of wrongdoing and perhaps in an inherited situation, is caught off guard. The pastor finds himself or herself in almost a lynch mob setting. Many people feel that since the congregation pays you, they own your time. They feel that their pastor is an employee who is paid to do the work of the ministry. Their point is often expressed by their lack of involvement in the many programs the church offers. They expect the paid staff to do everything and feel their own role is to keep an eye on whether it is getting done or not. With this unbiblical model in place, pastors often feel that the job is too much for them (which is true) and they feel like they are in it alone (which they often are). Having knowledge of the expectations of a particular congregation should be required before you accept the invitation to serve them. Every congregation is different. Some are very healthy; some are in great need; and some need adjustment in a few key areas. Reading through the New Testament and evaluating the writings of Paul reveal that every church has its unique challenges.

Your Personality Preparing ourselves for the challenges of ministry requires more than taking a few courses, attending seminars, or, for many, four to six years of university and seminary. Learning how to adapt to a culture and a congregation adds a level of challenge that one does not prepare for overnight or within a few years. Pastoring is similar to marriage in that although you receive pre-marriage counseling, when you get married you face a lifetime of adjustments, flexibility, compromise, and challenge. People who get married often have unrealistic expectations, and pastors often have unrealistic expectations, too. Every church is different; every city and culture is different, and you are not like the pastor you followed. Pastoring requires adjustment, openness, and a willingness to be understanding and to be understood. When a pastor has numerous inflexible goals that become unattainable, it is likely that he or she will become discouraged and, at times, depressed. He or she will lose confidence and energy, and the people the pastor once loved will become a burden. We hear more often than we would like, the story of another minister who has fallen. All of us are vulnerable, and when sin is allowed into our lives in the form of lies, infidelity, addictions, pornography, or illegal behavior, our ministry will lose its supernatural anointing. Some are forced to terminate their position, or they choose to resign. This is typically viewed as failure, and most will not re-enter the ministry or they will be denied future ministry. When considering a pastorate, one should consider the culture and the community of that particular city, the history of the church, and who served there previously. Did the previous pastor leave healthy, or was there a failure of some sort? Was he or she fired, and, if so, why? How long was the previous pastor there, and why did he or she leave? Also, ask yourself, “Do I fit in this type of church and in this community?” Is my personality the kind that will be enjoyed by most of the people in this church? If not, am I willing to change, adjust, and be flexible as I endeavor to serve the people of this church?

Why can conflict come when there is an increase in membership? a)	New people bring new ideas and can be threatening to the existing congregation. b)	Potential for conflict does not increase when membership increases. c)	Most new members cannot really be trusted, especially if they have attended other area churches. d)	The membership process creates a lot of extra work for the pastor. 8.	According to lesson two, why does the completion of a building program elevate the potential for conflict? a)	Typically, too much money is collected for major projects and people argue about how the remainder will be spent. b)	Most people will believe that the project was not done correctly. c)	Workers will begin to brag that the project could not have been completed without them. d)	The pastor may be burnt out after depleting his or her energy and neglect other responsibilities. 9.	Which of the following represents cultural impact on the potential for conflict? a)	People have become very consumer-driven, even at church. b)	People sometimes forget that a pastor has others to care for including his or her own family. c)	A consumer mentality is often very self-centered.

d)	All of the above 10.	Which of the following did the text say a leader should consider before accepting a new ministry position? a)	The culture and whether his or her personality fits within the congregation b)	The proximity of a good bookstore and library, and the church’s access to the latest technology c)	Those opportunities that represent an increase in salary d)	Those settings where the former pastor is no longer living
