Why can conflict come when there is an increase in membership? a)	New people bring new ideas and can be threatening to the existing congregation. b)	Potential for conflict does not increase when membership increases. c)	Most new members cannot really be trusted, especially if they have attended other area churches. d)	The membership process creates a lot of extra work for the pastor. 8.	According to lesson two, why does the completion of a building program elevate the potential for conflict? a)	Typically, too much money is collected for major projects and people argue about how the remainder will be spent. b)	Most people will believe that the project was not done correctly. c)	Workers will begin to brag that the project could not have been completed without them. d)	The pastor may be burnt out after depleting his or her energy and neglect other responsibilities. 9.	Which of the following represents cultural impact on the potential for conflict? a)	People have become very consumer-driven, even at church. b)	People sometimes forget that a pastor has others to care for including his or her own family. c)	A consumer mentality is often very self-centered.

d)	All of the above 10.	Which of the following did the text say a leader should consider before accepting a new ministry position? a)	The culture and whether his or her personality fits within the congregation b)	The proximity of a good bookstore and library, and the church’s access to the latest technology c)	Those opportunities that represent an increase in salary d)	Those settings where the former pastor is no longer living

## 4 - The Different Types of Conflict

### Keeping Conflict Under Control

#### 4.1.1 — Define and demonstrate the biblical concept of confrontation.

World leaders deal with conflict between nations, businesses deal with conflict between personnel, and churches deal with a variety of conflict issues. Recognize that conflict is normal and should be an expected part of life and leadership. However, it does not have to be destructive or permanent.

There are always two opinions with every conflict. We need to understand the issues at hand. When two people are involved in conflict, each needs to carefully listen to the other and attempt to clear up any misunderstandings. Get a clear perspective on the alternatives you face, the primary issues that are causing the tension, or the reasons for the struggles. At times a third party—pastor, Christian leader, or counselor—can help if there seems to be an impasse.

Understand the nature of the conflict situation and the objectives, motives, and feelings of those involved. After both parties understand the issues, the reasons for tension, and the misunderstandings or misinformation, then a decision toward resolution needs to be made. Often resolution can be reached when both parties remain calm. “A gentle answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger” (Proverbs 15:1).

In most situations where there has been tension, gossip, accusation, or worse, a more active confrontation is needed. This includes the willingness to challenge and be respectfully challenged. Resolution at this level typically does not come overnight; it takes time and sometimes will not come at all. As much as we want to have peace with everyone and bring people into agreement, some differences are irreconcilable.

Confrontation does not mean “hitting them with both a left and a right.” It means “exhort.” Paul had great concern for the Corinthian church as he saw a split in the making. This church was both dysfunctional and Pentecostal. However, Paul had invested a great deal of time and energy in that congregation. He used his writing to exhort this body of believers.

The word exhort comes from a combination of two words in the original language: the word to call and the word alongside . It conveys the idea of calling one alongside for the purpose of helping him or her pursue a course of conduct that is right. This is what a parent does with a disobedient child. It is what a professor does with a student who is not making the grade. It is what employers do with an employee who is not doing the job. The purpose of exhortation is to come alongside someone for the goal of helping him or her rethink and consider what he or she is doing so that it can be done right rather than wrong.

When one decides to win at all costs and damage another person’s reputation, church, or organization, then direct—and sometimes—public confrontation is necessary. This person is beyond wanting resolution or peace; he or she wants to win and injure. Titus 3:10 gives this instruction: “Warn a divisive person once, and then warn him a second time. After that have nothing to do with him.”

At the forefront in our minds should be the goal of everyone winning. A win-win solution is always best. In most instances, this is possible. Instead of one side winning while the other loses, both sides should attempt to help each other win. For parents, a good example would be when two children are dividing the last piece of pizza. If one cuts it and the other chooses his or her piece first, they both get a fair choice—and both win. If the older parishioner feels the portable chairs are too hard to sit on, offer to pick up a chair pad or reserve some softer chairs for older people. This kind of problem solving is always the best way to slow down or stop conflict from developing.

If a decision cannot be made about which is best, when both choices are acceptable, or both opinions are right, it is helpful to list the alternatives in two columns on paper. Write down the good and bad points of each. This can clarify the issues and help you decide what to do. If one person has an inner conflict that involves the emotions that seem to pull him or her in two directions or they have two opposing desires, then you or another leader needs to help this person.

“He who doubts is like a wave of the sea, blown and tossed by the wind. That man should not think he will receive anything from the Lord; he is a double-minded man, unstable in all he does” (James 1:6–8).

Some people cannot make up their minds. They have too many choices, options, or opinions. They get stuck and cannot focus on doing one thing. As a result, they are not solid and do a half-hearted job because they cannot decide what they really want to do. A lack of focus, vision, and concentration is often the result of being double-minded and unstable.

Paul loved Timothy as a son (2 Timothy 1:2) and a faithful co-worker (Romans 16:21). Paul named Timothy as a co-sender of six of his letters. Timothy was the pastor at the church in Ephesus. This was a dynamic, large church that was facing difficult days. Nero was attempting to stop the spread of the Christian faith and was severely persecuting believers.

In Paul’s first letter to Timothy, the Ephesian church seemed to be growing and developing into a strong, healthy Christian community. However, because of tension that developed as a result of persecution, the exodus of people who did not want to be recognized, and the escalation of normal church conflict, Paul wrote the second letter to Timothy.

Do not have anything to do with foolish and stupid arguments, because you know they produce quarrels. And the Lord’s servant must not quarrel; instead, he must be kind to everyone, able to teach, not resentful. Those who oppose him he must gently instruct, in the hope that God will grant them repentance leading them to a knowledge of the truth, and that they will come to their senses and escape the trap of the devil, who has taken them captive to do his will. (2 Timothy 2:23–26)

Paul’s wisdom to this young pastor is wisdom for us today as we pastor in the twenty-first century. The topic was foolish arguments that caused conflicts within the church in Ephesus. The direction that Paul gave Timothy was God’s wisdom for us as we deal with tension and arguing among some of the people who attend our churches. Along with Timothy, we are to understand the things we discuss in the following paragraph.

Some arguments are just plain foolish and stupid. The only thing that happens with this kind of heated discussion is more quarreling. Small issues such as whether we put up this kind of wallpaper or that, or purchase a Ford or Chevy bus, or if the pastor should greet people in the foyer each week instead of remaining at the altar praying with people after the service are all foolish arguments. Many arguments are to be avoided, and the pastor needs to keep a good spirit. Refuse to stoop to the level of getting involved in discussions that really do not make any difference and often have no good resolution. The pastor is not to be known for his or her quarreling, but for demonstrating kindness and having a forgiving heart.

I was teaching at a “Stress in the Ministry” conference, and after one of the sessions, a pastor came to me and said, “My district superintendent required me to attend this conference before he would consider renewing my credentials.”

I asked him, “What happened that made him come to this decision?”

The middle-aged pastor said, “Well, one of my deacons started another argument with me in a board meeting and after the meeting I beat him up, and this is not the first time I have done that!” True story. It should be obvious to us that this pastor is not known for being kind to everyone. On the contrary, he is feared because he might hit someone! This is not how God wants His under-shepherds to act.

The tension and stress of ministry can become overwhelming, and we can find ourselves feeling angry and becoming involved in disagreements. This is one area where every leader must exhibit self-discipline while making the decision to be a pastor who is kind and forgiving in his or her spirit.

We do not need to raise our voice or use destructive language to make our point. We are to give instruction with gentleness. As leaders we are to control the way we choose to talk to people. Even when someone is wrong, we are to gently instruct him or her.

Many think that if they raise their voices, then they will convince the other person that they are right. Paul instructed Timothy to “gently instruct” and in his heart, “hope that God will grant them repentance.”

God is the One who uses our words to speak to a person. Our tone of voice, accusations, or labels during the discussion can injure the ability to make the biblical point.

Understand that some have been trapped by the devil and their common sense has been affected. Many pastors do not like to admit that there could be people in their congregations who have been trapped by the enemy and are doing his work.

Paul warned the Ephesian elders: “I know that after I leave, savage wolves will come in among you and will not spare the flock. Even from your own number men will arise and distort the truth in order to draw away disciples after them. So be on your guard!” (Acts 20:29–31)

Spiritual warfare is part of what pastors do. Conflict can be deeply seated in a spiritual battle of God’s kingdom versus the enemy’s kingdom. The wise pastor will seek the Lord and discern if this is the case. However, even with the enemy’s servant, the confrontation that is needed should to be done with a gentle and kind spirit. We hold our ground, gently instruct, pray, and let God speak to those who have been influenced by the enemy.

**Q:** What are some of the key responsibilities of a mediator in aiding a conflict resolution?

**A:** The mediator must understand both sides of the conflict clearly. The mediator must ascertain and measure the emotional state of both parties and must seek to maintain emotional control of the conflict.

**Q:** What is confrontation, and how is it sometimes misunderstood?

**A:** Some think of confrontation as attacking or forcing an individual to face facts. Confrontation’s best biblical parallel is the idea of exhortation. To exhort is to “call alongside” to help an individual reevaluate or rethink his or her actions or position.

**Q:** What are some of the ways a winning solution can be found?

**A:** Win-win solutions are always preferred. When choosing between two acceptable options, a list of pros and cons can help ascertain which option to choose.

**Q:** What does the Bible say about one who is double-minded?

**A:** Such a person is unstable because he or she is unable to stay with a decision. Such a person will find difficulty in discerning God’s direction as he or she will be easily influenced in other directions.

**Q:** Explain Paul’s advice to Timothy (2 Timothy 2:23–26) in your own words.

**A:** This will be a personal response, but should focus on avoiding foolish arguments.

### Congregations that Are Having Conflict

#### 4.2.1 — Identify the underlying elements common to congregational conflict.

Congregations are a lot like families. When a family goes on vacation or a church spends time together, people soon discover different personalities, opinions, and spiritual ideas that can cause tension or even tear them apart.

Following are some actual case histories that illustrate the kind of conflict a congregation can experience:

Church One: A large church hired a new, part-time music director

The congregation had grown to over a thousand people in its forty-year history. The new pastor understood the advantage of having a full-time music and worship pastor. With the board in total agreement, the new pastor found an excellent person. As a result, the church grew, the services had a deeper sensitivity to the moving of the Spirit, and several choirs were developed with various age groups. The conflict? Some people felt the music was too loud; some could not hear (depending on where they sat); some felt the worship needed to be comprised of hymns; and some only wanted new choruses.

Church Two: The youth group needed to grow

An inner-city church had a solid adult population, but the teens were not being reached. The church asked a dynamic youth pastor from another state to become the youth pastor. As a result, the youth group grew quickly, young people were interested in participating in the youth events and services, and teens from numerous racial backgrounds attended and became Christians. The conflict? Some parents were concerned that some of the people who started attending did not know the Lord, and the parents felt those people might cause their own teens to sin. Some adults who were involved in the youth department did not like the new youth pastor because he made decisions that they used to make, and he set new goals. Instead of trying to understand the youth pastor’s heart, they decided they would ask the pastor to send him packing.

Church Three: The church was almost paid off

For almost twenty years the congregation had been paying off a debt that was acquired for the sanctuary. However, after twenty years, the sanctuary did not look new; it needed to be restored and re-configured. The carpet was worn out; wallpaper needed to be replaced; another restroom needed to be added; and the sound system was constantly breaking down. The cost of restoration and improvements was large and would require both raising money from donors and borrowing money. This new debt would take another seven to ten years to pay off. Some vocal members disagreed with any idea of borrowing, stating it was money that could be given to missions, or new pastoral staff. Some hated debt altogether and felt it was never God’s will.

If you have been a pastor for any length of time you will discover conflict issues, personality problems, and different views of theology within the church you lead. How do you address these kinds of conflicts? Following are some suggestions:

Remind the congregation and leadership (board, deacons, elders, and departmental leaders) that you need each other. Far too many congregations divide in opinion or lose parishioners when pastors, elders, or other leadership insist that their opinion is the only way. For a congregation to grow, every department needs to understand that they all need each other. The youth need the wisdom and stability of age. The aged need the energy, courage, and boldness of the youth.

Do not run from conflict. Congregations tend to ignore conflicts and say things like, “Oh, that is only Bill; he is always yelling or is critical of things he does not like. But you need to understand he is always here.” In reality, growing Christians are concerned about Bill (or should be), and want him to change. Most of the time, leadership merely hopes Bill will one day leave the church. Conflict resolution is hard work; however, every pastor is required to do it. Clarify the real problem, and then resolve to work on it together.

Listen to other people’s opinions and criticisms. Allow people (staff, leaders, and members) to express their opinions. Listen when they are talking. Listen to words, names used, and decisions that have been made. Even if it runs the opposite way of your opinion, it is important to hear this information and consider their opinion before decisions are made.

Have knowledge about your audience and culture. Some people who attend your church may have a consumer mentality where they ask, “How can this church help me?” They may wonder, “If this church cannot manage its own conflicts, how can they help me with my problems?” Without understanding and actively developing new ways to minister to the people of our culture and location, we will not reach them. Are you willing to watch new people leave because you will not attempt new ways to reach people?

Understand that any change has conflict as a companion. Consider how your congregation will be affected before you try something new or change something that has been done for years. If, for example, you want to begin new programs to reach young people, evaluate how some will react and try to speak to them about how the church needs to reach the youth of the city. This kind of conversation can help them feel part of the decision.

Be sensitive to your emotions and the emotions of others. You can often sense tension or emotional issues with some people. It is best to receive advice and gather information and wise counsel from people who are somewhat detached from the issue of concern. Emotions can distract people from the real issue.

Keep the various issues separate. When having conversations with people who have concerns, talk about one issue at a time and continue to bring the subject back to the topic when the conversation drifts to other concerns. When people are troubled by one thing, they can confuse it with something else. For example, some can be upset because the youth department did not clean the church bus after the last event, but should this concern override the fact that new kids are coming and they are developing healthy relationships? Issues need to be addressed one at a time, and, in this example, the only thing needed was to remind the youth pastor (and every other department) to clean the bus after each event.

Involve people from both sides of the conflict. If people are in disagreement with each other, they should be part of the resolution process. Understanding both sides of the opinion brings sensitivity to people who want to understand, not just have their way. It might be difficult to hear both sides, but that is necessary before they can take ownership of the problem and find a solution for the good of the body.

Remain objective to both sides. Undermining one side of a dispute and siding with another will not help. Unless the conflict is divisive, sinful, political, or injurious to a person, we seek to understand and stay somewhat neutral. We can offer our professional opinion and give information of what other congregations have done about this kind of challenge; however, we need to manage the discussion with wisdom. We are to remember that this is our family of believers that God has asked us to lead.

When necessary, bring in an outside mediator. With some congregations, the only way out of a conflict is to bring in a neutral party who can offer professional advice. Be cautious that this person is unbiased toward the denomination, the district, and the people in your church, or even toward you. We are to understand that mediation will only work when both parties want and are willing to accept the process.

Case Histories of the Three Aforementioned Churches

Church One

The new music pastor listened to the older people and developed worship that included hymns as well as old choruses and new choruses. He was sensitive to how long people were asked to stand in the services and sensitive to people’s opinions of the musical programs. As a result, the services became more contemporary (as was realistically needed for the new and younger people), but some of the older people chose to leave because they wanted only hymns. The fact is that pastors need to understand that we must use sensitivity and receive other opinions; however, at times people will decide to go to another church, but your church will continue to reach new people.

Church Two

The new youth pastor noticed the parents’ concerns about the safety of their teens and secured and trained supervisors and hall monitors to insure that all youth events were safe all of the time. He talked to the previous volunteer leaders and asked for their assistance as they were going to develop more programs to reach the youth of the community. The pastor kindly informed the few who wanted the youth pastor to be let go that this would not be possible, as, he was doing an excellent job. The pastor asked them for patience as the youth department grew; however, one vocal family left the church and took as many as they could with them. The husband in this particular family had previously had many angry confrontations with leaders and felt he was spiritual because he was a leader (without demonstrating Christian leadership qualities). The youth group is now reaching young people who have never been reached for Christ.

Church Three

The pastor knew that a facility restoration needed to be done. He gave full disclosure to the board about the condition of the church. He involved the board in acquiring bids for any materials and labor. The pastor and board decided they would try to raise cash and pledges and would borrow the remainder of the expense. A low interest bank loan was approved. At that point the pastor brought the restoration program to the business meeting and received approval for the needed project. The restoration goal was presented with pictures, graphics, bids, color selections from an outside committee, and a plan for future expansion. The congregation appreciated the due diligence and the extra restroom. In the end, the sanctuary was restored and there was a general feeling of satisfaction from the membership. A few people were disappointed that money had to be borrowed to finish the project; however, very little was said about this.

**Q:** What must the pastor do when the congregation begins to take sides in an argument?

**A:** The pastor must remind them of their need for one another. He or she must work to maintain mutual respect for one another even in the midst of conflict. The pastor must demonstrate the ability to bring about peaceful and respectful resolution.

**Q:** In what ways do congregations run from persons who create conflict?

**A:** They often dismiss it as “that’s just the way he is.” They determine someone’s right to be negative according to his or her attendance or financial support. They privately hope the person will just go away.

**Q:** Why must a pastor listen carefully to opposing views and criticisms?

**A:** Good decisions are always the product of good information. Failure to consider information provided in criticism can cause mistakes to be deepened or repeated.

**Q:** Why does change bring such a potential for conflict?

**A:** Change causes what is established to be unsettled. Change brings new ideas and threatens those who have held the former ideas. Change often means new people are involved, which can be threatening to those who have been in charge previously.

**Q:** Why is it important to keep various issues separate?

**A:** People tend to pile on when they are debating. Trying to resolve multiple issues can confuse the discussion and limit effectiveness. When the mediator can keep issues separate, they are often more easily resolved.

**Q:** Why should people from both sides of a conflict be included in the resolution?

**A:** Answer If one side is left out, they will be suspicious of any resolution. Involving both sides increases the potential of gaining the support of both sides in the resolution. Involving both parties trains both parties in the importance of hearing and respecting differing views.

#### 4.2.2 — List suggested ways of dealing with congregational conflict.

Congregations are a lot like families. When a family goes on vacation or a church spends time together, people soon discover different personalities, opinions, and spiritual ideas that can cause tension or even tear them apart.

Following are some actual case histories that illustrate the kind of conflict a congregation can experience:

Church One: A large church hired a new, part-time music director

The congregation had grown to over a thousand people in its forty-year history. The new pastor understood the advantage of having a full-time music and worship pastor. With the board in total agreement, the new pastor found an excellent person. As a result, the church grew, the services had a deeper sensitivity to the moving of the Spirit, and several choirs were developed with various age groups. The conflict? Some people felt the music was too loud; some could not hear (depending on where they sat); some felt the worship needed to be comprised of hymns; and some only wanted new choruses.

Church Two: The youth group needed to grow

An inner-city church had a solid adult population, but the teens were not being reached. The church asked a dynamic youth pastor from another state to become the youth pastor. As a result, the youth group grew quickly, young people were interested in participating in the youth events and services, and teens from numerous racial backgrounds attended and became Christians. The conflict? Some parents were concerned that some of the people who started attending did not know the Lord, and the parents felt those people might cause their own teens to sin. Some adults who were involved in the youth department did not like the new youth pastor because he made decisions that they used to make, and he set new goals. Instead of trying to understand the youth pastor’s heart, they decided they would ask the pastor to send him packing.

Church Three: The church was almost paid off

For almost twenty years the congregation had been paying off a debt that was acquired for the sanctuary. However, after twenty years, the sanctuary did not look new; it needed to be restored and re-configured. The carpet was worn out; wallpaper needed to be replaced; another restroom needed to be added; and the sound system was constantly breaking down. The cost of restoration and improvements was large and would require both raising money from donors and borrowing money. This new debt would take another seven to ten years to pay off. Some vocal members disagreed with any idea of borrowing, stating it was money that could be given to missions, or new pastoral staff. Some hated debt altogether and felt it was never God’s will.

If you have been a pastor for any length of time you will discover conflict issues, personality problems, and different views of theology within the church you lead. How do you address these kinds of conflicts? Following are some suggestions:

Remind the congregation and leadership (board, deacons, elders, and departmental leaders) that you need each other. Far too many congregations divide in opinion or lose parishioners when pastors, elders, or other leadership insist that their opinion is the only way. For a congregation to grow, every department needs to understand that they all need each other. The youth need the wisdom and stability of age. The aged need the energy, courage, and boldness of the youth.

Do not run from conflict. Congregations tend to ignore conflicts and say things like, “Oh, that is only Bill; he is always yelling or is critical of things he does not like. But you need to understand he is always here.” In reality, growing Christians are concerned about Bill (or should be), and want him to change. Most of the time, leadership merely hopes Bill will one day leave the church. Conflict resolution is hard work; however, every pastor is required to do it. Clarify the real problem, and then resolve to work on it together.

Listen to other people’s opinions and criticisms. Allow people (staff, leaders, and members) to express their opinions. Listen when they are talking. Listen to words, names used, and decisions that have been made. Even if it runs the opposite way of your opinion, it is important to hear this information and consider their opinion before decisions are made.

Have knowledge about your audience and culture. Some people who attend your church may have a consumer mentality where they ask, “How can this church help me?” They may wonder, “If this church cannot manage its own conflicts, how can they help me with my problems?” Without understanding and actively developing new ways to minister to the people of our culture and location, we will not reach them. Are you willing to watch new people leave because you will not attempt new ways to reach people?

Understand that any change has conflict as a companion. Consider how your congregation will be affected before you try something new or change something that has been done for years. If, for example, you want to begin new programs to reach young people, evaluate how some will react and try to speak to them about how the church needs to reach the youth of the city. This kind of conversation can help them feel part of the decision.

Be sensitive to your emotions and the emotions of others. You can often sense tension or emotional issues with some people. It is best to receive advice and gather information and wise counsel from people who are somewhat detached from the issue of concern. Emotions can distract people from the real issue.

Keep the various issues separate. When having conversations with people who have concerns, talk about one issue at a time and continue to bring the subject back to the topic when the conversation drifts to other concerns. When people are troubled by one thing, they can confuse it with something else. For example, some can be upset because the youth department did not clean the church bus after the last event, but should this concern override the fact that new kids are coming and they are developing healthy relationships? Issues need to be addressed one at a time, and, in this example, the only thing needed was to remind the youth pastor (and every other department) to clean the bus after each event.

Involve people from both sides of the conflict. If people are in disagreement with each other, they should be part of the resolution process. Understanding both sides of the opinion brings sensitivity to people who want to understand, not just have their way. It might be difficult to hear both sides, but that is necessary before they can take ownership of the problem and find a solution for the good of the body.

Remain objective to both sides. Undermining one side of a dispute and siding with another will not help. Unless the conflict is divisive, sinful, political, or injurious to a person, we seek to understand and stay somewhat neutral. We can offer our professional opinion and give information of what other congregations have done about this kind of challenge; however, we need to manage the discussion with wisdom. We are to remember that this is our family of believers that God has asked us to lead.

When necessary, bring in an outside mediator. With some congregations, the only way out of a conflict is to bring in a neutral party who can offer professional advice. Be cautious that this person is unbiased toward the denomination, the district, and the people in your church, or even toward you. We are to understand that mediation will only work when both parties want and are willing to accept the process.

Case Histories of the Three Aforementioned Churches

Church One

The new music pastor listened to the older people and developed worship that included hymns as well as old choruses and new choruses. He was sensitive to how long people were asked to stand in the services and sensitive to people’s opinions of the musical programs. As a result, the services became more contemporary (as was realistically needed for the new and younger people), but some of the older people chose to leave because they wanted only hymns. The fact is that pastors need to understand that we must use sensitivity and receive other opinions; however, at times people will decide to go to another church, but your church will continue to reach new people.

Church Two

The new youth pastor noticed the parents’ concerns about the safety of their teens and secured and trained supervisors and hall monitors to insure that all youth events were safe all of the time. He talked to the previous volunteer leaders and asked for their assistance as they were going to develop more programs to reach the youth of the community. The pastor kindly informed the few who wanted the youth pastor to be let go that this would not be possible, as, he was doing an excellent job. The pastor asked them for patience as the youth department grew; however, one vocal family left the church and took as many as they could with them. The husband in this particular family had previously had many angry confrontations with leaders and felt he was spiritual because he was a leader (without demonstrating Christian leadership qualities). The youth group is now reaching young people who have never been reached for Christ.

Church Three

The pastor knew that a facility restoration needed to be done. He gave full disclosure to the board about the condition of the church. He involved the board in acquiring bids for any materials and labor. The pastor and board decided they would try to raise cash and pledges and would borrow the remainder of the expense. A low interest bank loan was approved. At that point the pastor brought the restoration program to the business meeting and received approval for the needed project. The restoration goal was presented with pictures, graphics, bids, color selections from an outside committee, and a plan for future expansion. The congregation appreciated the due diligence and the extra restroom. In the end, the sanctuary was restored and there was a general feeling of satisfaction from the membership. A few people were disappointed that money had to be borrowed to finish the project; however, very little was said about this.

**Q:** What must the pastor do when the congregation begins to take sides in an argument?

**A:** The pastor must remind them of their need for one another. He or she must work to maintain mutual respect for one another even in the midst of conflict. The pastor must demonstrate the ability to bring about peaceful and respectful resolution.

**Q:** In what ways do congregations run from persons who create conflict?

**A:** They often dismiss it as “that’s just the way he is.” They determine someone’s right to be negative according to his or her attendance or financial support. They privately hope the person will just go away.

**Q:** Why must a pastor listen carefully to opposing views and criticisms?

**A:** Good decisions are always the product of good information. Failure to consider information provided in criticism can cause mistakes to be deepened or repeated.

**Q:** Why does change bring such a potential for conflict?

**A:** Change causes what is established to be unsettled. Change brings new ideas and threatens those who have held the former ideas. Change often means new people are involved, which can be threatening to those who have been in charge previously.

**Q:** Why is it important to keep various issues separate?

**A:** People tend to pile on when they are debating. Trying to resolve multiple issues can confuse the discussion and limit effectiveness. When the mediator can keep issues separate, they are often more easily resolved.

**Q:** Why should people from both sides of a conflict be included in the resolution?

**A:** Answer If one side is left out, they will be suspicious of any resolution. Involving both sides increases the potential of gaining the support of both sides in the resolution. Involving both parties trains both parties in the importance of hearing and respecting differing views.

### Unique Challenges in Different Size Churches

#### 4.3.1 — Identify and explain the differing dynamics found in churches of various sizes.

Among the leading researchers of congregational life are Arlin Rothauge (1983) and Lyle Schaller (1984). Both have developed valuable material to assist pastors in understanding how the size and history of a church affect its life and work. Rothauge categorizes four sizes of congregations. The following churches differ not only in the number of people who attend weekly, but also in the way the church is managed or led.

Churches that have 0–50 in attendance = The Family Church

Churches that have 50–150 in attendance = The Pastoral Church

Churches that have 150–300 in attendance = The Program Church

Churches that have over 300 in attendance = The Corporation Church

The Family Church

These usually contain a single cell where the pastor functions similarly to a chaplain. The pastor may not be the most significant leader in the congregation, but likely defers to the lay leader who sanctions the addition of new members, new programs, changes, financial management (or control), and so forth. The congregation will often look more to the lay leader than the pastor for decisions. Pastors tend to leave these churches after shorter periods of service; however, the membership and leadership stay the same.

Conflict Issues

Conflict between the congregation and pastor does not last long because the pastor quickly understands that he or she does not have the power to resist the group. Single cell churches are monolithic and are rarely open to conflict. They are much like a family with different ages, sizes, and philosophies. Conflict is typically suppressed. When a family church has conflict, there is usually a long-standing history of that issue. This church will look to the lay leader to advise them.

The conflict could be rooted in certain families, and it is not uncommon to have one family against another family. Because of the small size, two or three opposing families create a feeling of tension and confusion. These problems are often unique to the family church:

• Conflict is often suppressed and avoided.

• People with different ideas are not allowed to express them.

• Challenges to lay leadership are considered inappropriate.

• When a problem does surface, it tends to be at a high level and long lasting.

• The pastor of a family church will need to deal with conflict issues over a long-term, while understanding the family dynamics.

The Pastoral Church

This type of church needs more involvement from more leaders. Typically, the pastoral church has two or three cells that have close (or intense) relationships. The cells are like layers or circles revolving around the pastoral leadership. The leadership circle, which includes leaders of each cell, replaces the one lay leader or matriarch. Usually a paid pastor with education and ordination is shepherding this type of church. His or her effectiveness depends a great deal on his or her ability to communicate and delegate authority, assign responsibility, and recognize the accomplishments of the laity. In this size of church, most of the members are loyal to the pastor and follow the advice and direction of the shepherd.

Conflict Issues

In the pastoral church there usually is a history of tensions (or strong conflicts) between the different cells or groups within the congregation. As long as the pastor serves as a bridge between the different groups, the congregation usually moves along with little concern. However, if the pastor takes sides, conflict could grow and the pastor will become the center of the issue. Different groups will either want to remove the pastor or keep him or her, depending on which group the pastor sided with.

The pastoral church can handle more conflict than the family church. A common mistake that pastoral churches make is for some of the members to believe that the pastor is to blame for their difficulties. If the church is hurt too badly by a conflict or fight and the pastor leaves, they may go through a time of not being able to find a pastor. A replacement at the level of competence for their congregation can be difficult to find and may take a longer period of time.

The pastor of this church will need to manage conflict issues by helping members understand that they are responsible to face their difficulties and challenges, make decisions with Christian maturity, and seek ways to accomplish their goals as a group.

The Program Church

With the increase in size, it is no longer possible for the pastor to know everyone or be at every event. Much of the responsibility is delegated, and people in leadership are held accountable for their departments. Team leadership takes the place of one centralized leadership person. Rothauge (1983) observes that the church staff and lay leadership will require more training and pastoral support for their expanded ministry. The senior pastor becomes a pastor to the lay pastors.

Conflict Issues

With this size church there is usually another full-time or part-time staff member who will attract members, and this could cause factious groups to form and perhaps promote conflict. This type of church is more complex because the members are more removed from staff and each other; it is not as tight a group. It typically takes longer for people in leadership to feel the conflict and to respond to it. Staff tension can enter in and territorialism, turf, and personal preferences can quickly develop. In the program church, the conflict can often be worked out within the staff without needing to involve the larger congregation.

The pastor will need to bring the various leaders, groups, or department heads together to work out the issues.

The Corporation Church

This type of church is more complicated and diverse in its function as a congregation. The lay leader returns as a member on the deacon board or a committee as he or she begins to give direction under the leadership of the pastor. This size of congregation has become deeper in relationships, and small groups form around personal relationships.

Conflict Issues

The conflicts tend not to be church-wide, and not everyone is emotionally involved in the final outcome. The level of caring about the issues is uneven, and the pastor needs to be careful not to put an indifferent person into a position of making decisions for others who care greatly. Before a decision is brought to the entire congregation, the pastor will need to take time to inform the church and invite participation from them concerning the decision.

The pastor and board need to be aware of the different groups, cultures, races, ages, and departments as they manage any conflict issues.

When There is Opposition to the Pastor

Most of the significant conflict in churches is pastor-focused. More often than not, the pastor is the issue under discussion. Whether it concerns the pastor as a person, the pastor’s leadership style, or the pastor’s position on issues, the church needs to be cautious to make sure that its dealings with the pastor are fair and appropriate. The pastor is rarely the main reason for difficulties in a church. Others have typically played a major part in creating and continuing the conflicts at hand. Those who would blame the pastor need to be cautious to take into consideration those who have interfered with healing and those who have supported the conflict’s history. In this case, the board will need to receive the following:

• Accurate feedback about the problems

• Clear statements of expectations

• An opportunity for the pastor to change his or her performance in a reasonable time frame

Clear feedback includes specifics, avoids generalities and labels, and is not vague. It is never right to defame a person’s ministry by allowing people to say: “I am not sure who said it, but this person feels that the pastor does not have integrity and is not humble,” or “The pastor is lazy,” or “The pastor does not get it!”

Knowing the names of the people who have the problem is important. People should not be able to say something like this and not give the name of the person. Also, generalizations like, “He is not humble,” or “He is lazy” are not accurate ways to define someone because people define those labels differently.

Every pastor is different; some have a higher level of energy than others, some are morning people, while others are evening people, etc. Some are extroverted and some introverted in their personalities. The pastor should know the clear expectations, and they should be spelled out in advance. If the pastor has not fulfilled the agreed upon expectations, then he or she should have the opportunity to hear the concerns and explain his or her reasons. Perhaps the expectations were not practical or were too generous, or might not have considered the various groups. If the board and congregation expect a pastor to be involved in all the hospital calls, also to speak, study, pray, administrate, participate in denominational events and committees, be involved in the community, manage staff, and ensure that the church properties are always looking wonderful, then that board and congregation need to rethink their expectations and make their requirements more realistic.

If the pastor needs to change or improve his or her performance, then he or she should be given a reasonable amount of time to meet the expectations. It takes time to relearn, retrain, and readjust a schedule with changing a job performance. Patience, encouragement, prayer, and support need to be part of the board’s attitude as they and the pastor work toward their goals. At least six months should be given to a pastor who needs to change, and if the expected, agreed-upon goals are not met in that time period, it might be appropriate for the pastor to consider changing his or her ministry, or the board might need to encourage a change in a timely manner.

If the pastor ends up leaving the church, the board should consider the length of service, the pastor’s opportunity to find other work, insurance benefits, the need for retraining, and develop a reasonable severance package.

Every size congregation, in every community and with every type of church history, will have unique needs for management style. All congregations have challenges; there is no church without problems or potential conflict. As we lead the church God has called us to, He will give us the understanding and strength to be the under-shepherd He desires us to be.

**Q:** What is the pastoral church, and how is conflict often handled?

**A:** The pastoral church is one whose attendance is between 50-150 people. There can be a history of conflict between cells in the church. If the pastor takes a side, he or she can become the center of an issue. The pastoral church can handle more conflict than the family church.

**Q:** What is the program church, and how is conflict often handled?

**A:** The program church is one whose attendance is between 150-300 people. This church is not as tight as the previous types. Often a staff member is involved in the conflict. Typically it will take longer for people to feel the conflict in the program church.

**Q:** How does a pastor’s role change as a congregation grows?

**A:** In the smaller congregation, the pastor is typically involved in every activity of the church. As the church grows, others must take responsibility for the growing ministry, and expectations of the pastor must become more realistic. Potential for conflict arises when people have differing views of what a pastor should be involved in. Potential for conflict increases as people are reluctant to accept a pastor’s more limited role in areas where he or she had previously been more active.

**Q:** What is the corporation church, and how is conflict often handled?

**A:** The corporation church is one whose attendance exceeds 300 people. Conflict in the corporation church tends to not be church-wide. Because there is greater activity, the pastor will give a differing level of energy to each area. The pastor must be careful to be aware of the different groups, cultures, ages, departments, and races of the congregation as he or she makes decisions and manages conflict.

**Q:** How should conflicts involving the pastor be addressed?

**A:** Those involved should be identified and given an appropriate hearing. The pastor’s responsibilities should be clearly spelled out. The pastor should receive clear communication as to the areas where he or she is not meeting expectations and be given a reasonable amount of time to affect changes. If an acceptable solution cannot be reached, the board should give consideration to the pastor’s needs and years of service when developing a severance plan.

### Confrontation

#### 4.4.1 — Describe and apply the characteristics of conflict and confrontation experienced by Nehemiah.

Nehemiah’s understanding of how the enemy would try to stop his efforts was the key to his ability to finish what he believed God asked him to do. As we look again at the book of Nehemiah, we can discover that he not only proceeded with the building of the wall around Jerusalem, but also as needed, brought necessary confrontation to the people. Nehemiah discovered that the enemy tried to stop his work by these things:

• Ridicule, which affects our confidence: “They mocked and ridiculed us” (Nehemiah 2:19).

• Discouragement, which drains our strength (Nehemiah 4:6–7).

• Threats, which cause us to fear : “We will be right there among them and will kill them and put an end to their work” (Nehemiah 4:11).

• The conflict came from within through organized opposition, which makes us feel overwhelmed; and through internal opposition, which causes division (Nehemiah 5:1–13).

These are some of the enemy’s favorite tools to prevent individuals and churches from doing God’s will. We should not be surprised with these attempts by the devil’s kingdom to stop God’s kingdom from advancing. Division is one of the enemy’s favorite tools, and we should be on the alert for it at all times.

Division from within is dangerous in any organization and can destroy the momentum and potential of a church. Jesus instructed His disciples: “Every kingdom divided against itself will be ruined, and every city or household divided against itself will not stand” (Matthew 12:25).

Nehemiah needed to confront the people who were causing the problem. Confrontation is never easy; however, it is necessary for pastors and church leaders. The Scripture is full of examples of how to confront, of the timing involved in confrontation, and what to do after we have confronted a person. In Nehemiah’s situation, there was a legitimate problem that needed to be fixed. People were being taken advantage of in Jerusalem. Not only had the city fallen apart, but also the leadership of the community was not being sensitive to the needs of the people. Nehemiah saw the problem, heard the cries of the people, and got involved in alleviating the problem.

Nehemiah explains, “The men and their wives raised a great outcry against their Jewish brothers” (Nehemiah 5:1). The families were crying out because of several reasons.

They did not have enough food: “In order for us to eat and stay alive, we must get grain” (Nehemiah 5:2).

They owed too much on their homes: “We are mortgaging our fields, our vineyard, and our homes to get grain during the famine” (Nehemiah 5:3).

They were paying too much for taxes: “We have had to borrow money to pay the king’s tax” (Nehemiah 5:4).

They were exploiting each other: “Although we are of the same flesh and blood as our countrymen and though our sons are as good as theirs, yet we have to subject our sons and daughters to slavery” (Nehemiah 5:5).

The conflict that Nehemiah was experiencing is in the same family of conflict issues that we deal with today. It was driven by selfishness from one group toward another and one person toward another. Most of the time, church conflict can be identified with one group or with a person who wants his or her way and is not sensitive to others in the church. James instructs us that arguing, fights, and much of the divisive behavior we see in peoples’ lives stems from the desires that battle within a person (James 4:1).

Selfishness is the driving force behind much of the church division that pastors face. When we experience this type of behavior, we have a decision to make. Do we try to address it? Do we ignore it? Or do we ask someone else to take care of it? I believe that all three questions are necessary and that every situation and person is a little different. At times, someone else on our leadership team, or another pastor, or a board member, or person in the church can talk to the person, and the situation will be managed well. At other times, we need to wait for the right timing and try to sense the appropriate way to handle the confrontation ourselves. However, the fact remains that there are many situations in our churches that we will need to speak to. Following are biblical recommendations about how to confront people who are living in sin or causing division:

Get emotionally involved. Many leaders choose not to become emotionally involved because of the fear of being too emotional. Understanding the balance between not being emotionally involved and being too emotionally involved is critical. Knowing how we choose to manifest our emotions is important and something that all leaders need to manage. In Nehemiah’s case, he explained, “When I heard their outcry and these charges, I was very angry” (Nehemiah 5:6).

Jesus saw how the people were misusing the temple, and He became emotionally involved. He “entered the temple area and drove out all who were buying and selling there. He overturned the tables of the money-changers and the benches of those selling doves, saying, ‘My house will be called a house of prayer’” (Matthew 21:12–13). The Bible tells us that God becomes angry at sin, division, and selfishness.

Feeling angry and becoming emotional are not bad; how we choose to manifest our emotions is where many get into trouble. The Scripture informs us to “Be angry but sin not” (Ephesians 4:26). If we refuse to get emotionally involved in the critical issues of our ministry, then we could be ignoring important areas that God wants us to rectify.

Say something, but be cautious about what you say. Before we confront someone or a group that we know we need to talk to, we need to think about what we are going to say. Nehemiah pondered, then accused the nobles and officials (Nehemiah 5:7).

When we need to talk to an employee, person in the church, family member, or another leader, it is always good to plan out what we are going to say before we say it. James instructs us to “Be quick to listen, slow to speak and slow to become angry” (James 1:19).

If we plan what we are going to say and schedule a good time to say it, our words and the timing are typically accurate.

Confrontation

In planning a confrontation, we determine who needs to be confronted. Determine who the leader is and go to him or her. However, before you confront, answer these questions:

• Is this a personal issue between only him or her and me? If it is just the two of you, then keep the discussion on that level.

• Do I have a private motivation or agenda? Understanding our personal motives is important before we confront someone.

• Is this difficulty going to affect the church or just me? If it is just you, then it might be a good idea to let it go.

Nehemiah was experiencing a crisis where the less fortunate were being greatly affected. “I told them, you are exacting usury from your own countrymen!” (Nehemiah 5:7). He told them exactly what they were doing. Again, he was speaking to confrontation. Jesus explained to His disciples: “If your brother sins against you, go and show him his fault just between the two of you” (Matthew 18:15). Jesus also instructed that, first, we go to the person; second, if this does not work, we bring another mature Christian with us and talk to the person again; third, if that does not work and the person does not stop his or her destructive behavior, then we need to inform the church of his or her behavior. All of this is God’s way to deal with church division, sin issues, and any area that needs to be confronted in the local church. Paul informed Titus that he was to “warn a divisive person once, and then a second time. After that, have nothing to do with him” (Titus 3:10).

Understand that what has been said in private might need to go public. When a pastor needs to bring a situation or a divisive person to public awareness, he or she often fears losing support. However, most of the time, the opposite happens. Nehemiah very likely had that concern as well; however, he chose to make the public aware of the difficult situation. Therefore, he called together a large meeting: “‘What you are doing is not right. Should you not walk in the fear of our God to avoid the reproach of our Gentile enemies?’” Then, he confronted the opposition by saying, “‘Let the exacting of usury stop!’” (Nehemiah 5:7; vv. 9–10).

The Bible tells us that there was silence after his confrontation and then the response was what he had hoped and prayed for: they repented. “‘We will give it back,’ they said. ‘And we will not demand anything more from them. We will do as you say’” (v. 12).

The usury was 1 percent a month or 12 percent a year. It was against the Jewish law to charge usury, and Nehemiah needed to address this issue before he could continue building the wall.

At times we will need to put a hold on church programs or new evangelistic outreaches while we deal with a divisive issue. Nehemiah also held them to their commitment to do the right thing. He said, “I also shook out the folds of my robe and said, ‘In this way may God shake out of his house and possessions every man who does not keep this promise. So may such a man be shaken out and emptied.’ The whole assembly said, ‘Amen,’ and praised the Lord” (Nehemiah 5:13). It is important to follow through on whether or not the person is correcting the behavior.

Lead Your People by Example

Nehemiah gained favor with his decision to confront the issue and address the right people, while being sensitive to the proper timing. He could have taken advantage of the people (like the previous leaders had); however, he led by example. He could have received many “perks” for the job, but he did not accept them. He said, “I was appointed to be their governor in the land of Judah . . . neither I nor my brothers ate the food allotted to the governor. But the earlier governors—those preceding me—placed a heavy burden on the people and took forty shekels of silver from them in addition to food and wine” (Nehemiah 5:14–15).

He did not take advantage of the excessive food allowances, benefits, or unusual pay advances. Instead he thought of the people. His first concern was that they would be cared for and their protection and provisional would be met.

Leaders must set an example of excellence, humility, compassion, commitment, and appropriate confrontation. I believe this is what Paul meant when he said, “Follow my example, as I follow the example of Christ” (1 Corinthians 11:1). Leaders only ask people to go where they have gone.

**Q:** What did Nehemiah experience from those who tried to hinder his efforts?

**A:** Ridicule, Discouragement, Threats, Organized opposition, and Internal opposition

**Q:** What did Nehemiah see as the problems confronting the people?

**A:** They lacked food. They were unable to pay for their homes. Taxes were too burdensome. They were exploiting each other.

**Q:** How did this knowledge prepare Nehemiah for the necessary confrontation?

**A:** He was able to target specific issues. He was able to identify those who were at the source of the problem. He was able to communicate an agenda that those he served could appreciate. His success was measurable by the specific targets he had chosen.

**Q:** What issues should be settled in planning a confrontation?

**A:** We must determine who needs to be confronted. We must evaluate the scope of the confrontation. We must determine the nature of our true agenda. We must determine who will be affected by the confrontation.

**Q:** When should a private confrontation be made public?

**A:** Such a step is necessary when an individual is consistently unwilling to act biblically. Such a step may be required when an individual’s behavior is negatively influencing others. Such a step should always be avoided if the goal is simply to justify the pastor’s position. 5 CHAPTER

#### 4.4.2 — Identify the biblical recommendations for confrontation.

As we look again at the book of Nehemiah, we can discover that he not only proceeded with the building of the wall around Jerusalem, but also as needed, brought necessary confrontation to the people. Nehemiah discovered that the enemy tried to stop his work by these things:

• Ridicule, which affects our confidence: “They mocked and ridiculed us” (Nehemiah 2:19).

• Discouragement, which drains our strength (Nehemiah 4:6–7).

• Threats, which cause us to fear : “We will be right there among them and will kill them and put an end to their work” (Nehemiah 4:11).

• The conflict came from within through organized opposition, which makes us feel overwhelmed; and through internal opposition, which causes division (Nehemiah 5:1–13).

## 5 - The Health of Conflict

### Keeping the Goal in Mind

#### 5.1.1 — Identify and define the goals to achieve on the path of reconciliation.

Conflict resolving is a theologically sound and godly thing to do. Jesus said, “Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called the sons of God” (Matthew 5:9). There is no question that addressing and possibly eliminating deep-level conflict within the church you pastor is the will of God. Conflict resolution is reconciliation . As sinful people, we need to be reconciled to God, and, as believers, we often need to be reconciled to another person in the body of Christ. We can try only to do and say the right things, but we need the help of God to supernaturally reconcile people to Him or to another believer. We cannot force reconciliation; people have to make a decision to want it and work for it. Most of the time it does not happen overnight. Conflict resolution―the reconciliation between two or more people―takes time.

There are emotional responses and opinions we need to be aware of when striving for reconciliation. As we evaluate the person, group, or church, the following five goals will be helpful.

1. Reduce fear in the group, or the person, and in yourself. Fear is a tremendous motivator. Reducing people’s fear is often the first goal because when people are afraid they react as if they are being threatened. When people feel they are being threatened, or that they will lose something important to them, most of the time they will react. People who are afraid often raise their voices, fight, act irrationally, ask other people to help them fight, try to justify their case so they will win, avoid the problem, hide from what they are afraid of, and hide emotionally.

Also, people who are experiencing fear will not typically follow through on the agreement that they make, begin acting rationally, commit themselves to the goal, or care about the church, program, or you.

As pastors, we need to deal with our own fear of rejection, people’s opinions, and losing our job. Additionally we must grow emotionally in these areas of our lives. We should recognize that fear is one of the most common emotions, and typically people’s reactions and actions are somewhat predictable. We should try to do all we can to eliminate fear because when they or we are struggling with this, our thoughts will not be clear. We could be frightened and have a fight-or-flight attitude.

A way to reduce people’s fear is to increase their tolerance for others’ opinions. Every church has numerous opinions about how to do most anything, including how their pastor should preach. When there is growth in people’s tolerance for different styles, or appreciation for different opinions, then they have less fear.

2. Make a decision. Possibly the loudest verbal comments you will hear in conflicts are the two or more opinions . This common characteristic of conflict is a stance of disagreement or indecision between two or more people or two or more groups. Indecision does not need to be conflict because sometimes it is good to give people needed time to think things through before making a decision. However, in conflict there is an absence of agreement.

At times, one person or group has made a decision that has upset another person or group. For instance, it could be that you have been using the sanctuary screen for the words of the songs that the church sings instead of the hymnbook. This small decision by someone could trouble others.

To manage the conflict we need to try to bring an end to the indecision. Three goals we need to aim for in order to do this are the following:

1. The decision needs to be made.

2. The decision should be an excellent one that will bring good.

3. Leadership must be committed to the decision.

Commitment to the decision is typically strong when everyone likes it and feels good about it. To reach commitment, there needs to be a time where information is given and discussion at some level has happened. People need to feel that their opinion was taken into account as the decision was made.

3. Involve the different groups for different opinions. Conflict resolution goes to a deeper level when the two people or groups feel they have been heard and their opinion has been seriously considered. One side has the opportunity to present their case and then the other. The other might not change their mind; however, the intention is that their side will be heard.

4. Build skills in conflict resolution. The goal of a well-managed conflict process is to help the organization and the individuals within it become stronger rather than weaker. Conflict can be an opportunity to grow and develop; it does not have to reveal a weakness or be a negative experience.

Conflict is common in everyone’s life. Learning how to use it as a growth opportunity and producer of maturity is good. We do not need to create conflict so we can grow; life brings enough of it along the way, and certainly church work is full of different opinions.

If your church is experiencing conflict, possibly the first thing you should do is set some ground rules for appropriate behavior before you go on. Then proceed with the goal of reconciliation.

5. Give as much information as possible. When people are involved in conflict, they are hungry for more information that will enable them to support their case. They want to convince others that they are right. As a pastor, it is good to give new information that will bring knowledge to both sides. However, if the conflict is at a deeper level and a person does not really care about anyone else’s opinion, then more information typically does not help; it just adds fuel to their fire. That person is going to be intolerant and stick to his or her opinion (and try to influence others to join him or her). If the conflict is still new, the people often look at the new knowledge as an opportunity to grow in understanding, acquire better information, and open a new perspective. This positive is only possible when there is a low-level conflict.

The goals to reconcile two Christians, two groups, or the pastor with a group in the church are not only in our hands, but also require the grace of God. Conflict resolution pleases God. Reconciliation needs the help of our Creator. As we keep the goals in mind, we need to continue to fast and pray and ask God to give us His wisdom and to move supernaturally on our behalf.

Here are helpful steps to resolution:

1. Prepare yourself before you meet with the person or groups.

2. Select the location for the discussion yourself.

3. Receive commitments to meet from the person or the two groups.

4. Write out an agenda with a time limit.

5. Mentally prepare yourself to be in a problem-solving mode.

6. Listen carefully to all sides (with no one allowed to yell).

7. Listen to more than words; watch body language.

8. Determine an action plan.

9. Review what you have agreed on.

10. Plan what the future could look like.

Note that the conflict might be between the pastor and a parishioner. In this case, the pastor must:

• Be aware of his or her emotions and fears.

• Be willing to express his or her needs and interests.

• Have the attitude that the conflict is a problem to solve, not a war to win.

• Respect the other person.

• Avoid positioning, polarizing or being stubborn.

• Verbalize the goal of the church and his or her desire to be a peacemaker.

• Arrange for all disputing parties to be at the table.

• Understand and accept that conflict is normal.

• Establish these ground rules for conflict resolution:

• No profanity

• No demeaning sarcasm or ridicule

• No physical abuse

• Use “I” statements.

• All parties must agree to the process.

• All parties must speak directly to the pastor (mediator).

• Only one party will speak at a time.

• Once a decision has been made, participants must not discuss the conflict again.

• Agree to move forward.

• Pray for healing of emotions, understanding that they are often deep.

**Q:** What are the five goals to be set for pursuing reconciliation as mentioned in the text?

**A:** Reduce fear. Make a decision. Involve the different groups for different opinions. Build conflict resolution skills. Give as much information as possible.

**Q:** How can you reduce fear?

**A:** Establish guidelines that eliminate those things that increase fear, such as raised voices, irrational statements, and involving others. Increase tolerance for the other’s opinion.

**Q:** Why must the leader commit to bringing as much information as possible to both sides?

**A:** Good decisions are a function of good information. Often parties in conflict withhold information from one another. New information can help alter perceptions based on inadequate information.

**Q:** What are the ten steps a leader must take in preparing to aid restoration?

**A:** Prepare yourself before meeting with the persons or groups. Select the location for the meeting yourself. Receive commitments to meet from the persons or groups. Write out an agenda with a time limit. Mentally prepare yourself to be in a “problem-solving” posture. Listen carefully to all sides. Listen to more than words; watch body language. Determine an action plan. Review what has been agreed upon. Plan what the future could look like.

**Q:** In what ways is conflict a normal and healthy part of life?

**A:** Conflict is a part of every relationship and evidences the effort to join together two different individuals or groups.

**Q:** Why should a conflict be addressed sooner rather than later?

**A:** Avoidance does not bring resolution. Tensions can escalate, allowing the conflict to grow out of control. Delay can often give room to adding unnecessary people to the conflict.

#### 5.1.2 — List and demonstrate the ten steps to be taken in preparing for reconciliation.
