We should try to do all we can to eliminate fear because when they or we are struggling with this, our thoughts will not be clear. We could be frightened and have a fight-or-flight attitude.

A way to reduce people’s fear is to increase their tolerance for others’ opinions. Every church has numerous opinions about how to do most anything, including how their pastor should preach. When there is growth in people’s tolerance for different styles, or appreciation for different opinions, then they have less fear.

2. Make a decision. Possibly the loudest verbal comments you will hear in conflicts are the two or more opinions . This common characteristic of conflict is a stance of disagreement or indecision between two or more people or two or more groups. Indecision does not need to be conflict because sometimes it is good to give people needed time to think things through before making a decision. However, in conflict there is an absence of agreement.

At times, one person or group has made a decision that has upset another person or group. For instance, it could be that you have been using the sanctuary screen for the words of the songs that the church sings instead of the hymnbook. This small decision by someone could trouble others.

To manage the conflict we need to try to bring an end to the indecision. Three goals we need to aim for in order to do this are the following:

1. The decision needs to be made.

2. The decision should be an excellent one that will bring good.

3. Leadership must be committed to the decision.

Commitment to the decision is typically strong when everyone likes it and feels good about it. To reach commitment, there needs to be a time where information is given and discussion at some level has happened. People need to feel that their opinion was taken into account as the decision was made.

3. Involve the different groups for different opinions. Conflict resolution goes to a deeper level when the two people or groups feel they have been heard and their opinion has been seriously considered. One side has the opportunity to present their case and then the other. The other might not change their mind; however, the intention is that their side will be heard.

4. Build skills in conflict resolution. The goal of a well-managed conflict process is to help the organization and the individuals within it become stronger rather than weaker. Conflict can be an opportunity to grow and develop; it does not have to reveal a weakness or be a negative experience.

Conflict is common in everyone’s life. Learning how to use it as a growth opportunity and producer of maturity is good. We do not need to create conflict so we can grow; life brings enough of it along the way, and certainly church work is full of different opinions.

If your church is experiencing conflict, possibly the first thing you should do is set some ground rules for appropriate behavior before you go on. Then proceed with the goal of reconciliation.

5. Give as much information as possible. When people are involved in conflict, they are hungry for more information that will enable them to support their case. They want to convince others that they are right. As a pastor, it is good to give new information that will bring knowledge to both sides. However, if the conflict is at a deeper level and a person does not really care about anyone else’s opinion, then more information typically does not help; it just adds fuel to their fire. That person is going to be intolerant and stick to his or her opinion (and try to influence others to join him or her). If the conflict is still new, the people often look at the new knowledge as an opportunity to grow in understanding, acquire better information, and open a new perspective. This positive is only possible when there is a low-level conflict.

The goals to reconcile two Christians, two groups, or the pastor with a group in the church are not only in our hands, but also require the grace of God. Conflict resolution pleases God. Reconciliation needs the help of our Creator. As we keep the goals in mind, we need to continue to fast and pray and ask God to give us His wisdom and to move supernaturally on our behalf.

Here are helpful steps to resolution:

1. Prepare yourself before you meet with the person or groups.

2. Select the location for the discussion yourself.

3. Receive commitments to meet from the person or the two groups.

4. Write out an agenda with a time limit.

5. Mentally prepare yourself to be in a problem-solving mode.

6. Listen carefully to all sides (with no one allowed to yell).

7. Listen to more than words; watch body language.

8. Determine an action plan.

9. Review what you have agreed on.

10. Plan what the future could look like.

Note that the conflict might be between the pastor and a parishioner. In this case, the pastor must:

• Be aware of his or her emotions and fears.

• Be willing to express his or her needs and interests.

• Have the attitude that the conflict is a problem to solve, not a war to win.

• Respect the other person.

• Avoid positioning, polarizing or being stubborn.

• Verbalize the goal of the church and his or her desire to be a peacemaker.

• Arrange for all disputing parties to be at the table.

• Understand and accept that conflict is normal.

• Establish these ground rules for conflict resolution:

• No profanity

• No demeaning sarcasm or ridicule

• No physical abuse

• Use “I” statements.

• All parties must agree to the process.

• All parties must speak directly to the pastor (mediator).

• Only one party will speak at a time.

• Once a decision has been made, participants must not discuss the conflict again.

• Agree to move forward.

• Pray for healing of emotions, understanding that they are often deep.

**Q:** What are the five goals to be set for pursuing reconciliation as mentioned in the text?

**A:** Reduce fear. Make a decision. Involve the different groups for different opinions. Build conflict resolution skills. Give as much information as possible.

**Q:** How can you reduce fear?

**A:** Establish guidelines that eliminate those things that increase fear, such as raised voices, irrational statements, and involving others. Increase tolerance for the other’s opinion.

**Q:** Why must the leader commit to bringing as much information as possible to both sides?

**A:** Good decisions are a function of good information. Often parties in conflict withhold information from one another. New information can help alter perceptions based on inadequate information.

**Q:** What are the ten steps a leader must take in preparing to aid restoration?

**A:** Prepare yourself before meeting with the persons or groups. Select the location for the meeting yourself. Receive commitments to meet from the persons or groups. Write out an agenda with a time limit. Mentally prepare yourself to be in a “problem-solving” posture. Listen carefully to all sides. Listen to more than words; watch body language. Determine an action plan. Review what has been agreed upon. Plan what the future could look like.

**Q:** In what ways is conflict a normal and healthy part of life?

**A:** Conflict is a part of every relationship and evidences the effort to join together two different individuals or groups.

**Q:** Why should a conflict be addressed sooner rather than later?

**A:** Avoidance does not bring resolution. Tensions can escalate, allowing the conflict to grow out of control. Delay can often give room to adding unnecessary people to the conflict.

### Conflict Can Be Helpful

#### 5.2.1 — Understand and explain a healthy approach to dealing with conflict.

Constructive conflict is part of every healthy relationship. In marriage, when we have differences with our spouse, it is of great value to be able to discuss the differences and come to a win-win decision. Growing children disagree with parents; however, if both sides can openly discuss and come to a reasonable solution, it will build stability in their home. Employers and employees have differing opinions about how to do their work or improve the company. Wise employers will listen to their employees, and committed employees will respect their employer.

Church conflict can also have a good side that demonstrates health. Following are some constructive uses of conflict:

It is better to address conflict sooner rather than later

While we do not want to be reactionary or quick to come to a conclusion, we also do not want to avoid conflict and pretend it will just go away. Tension is always uncomfortable, and it takes courage to find a way to talk to the people involved in the conflict. If, as a leader, you cannot do this because of time constraints, then you will need to have another pastor or deacon address things as soon as possible. Waiting for conflict to go away or hoping the people will just work it out is usually futile, and the longer you wait to address the situation, the deeper the hurt and the more damage will be done. When we are passive or accommodating (letting it continue), our inaction will only serve as fuel for the fire of anger and wrong behavior.

The goal of conflict should be to reach resolution

Resolution usually does not prove one side to be totally wrong and the other side to be totally right. Both have a perspective, opinion, or problem that needs to be communicated and evaluated. This is a team approach as we work together in the body of Christ for a common, healthy goal for the good of the church. The two sides are not adversaries—they are problem solvers with the high goal of honoring God’s work.

As leaders we do not confront from a base of power. Our first instinct is to react defensively or aggressively. It is always better to confront power with God’s truth or by correcting the misinformation or untruth in the situation.

Keep the main thing the main thing; focus on the issue

Discussions can get out of control and emotions can turn into raised voices, hardness of hearts, labeling (name calling), and using the Bible as a weapon rather than an instruction manual. The safest way to avoid this is to keep one issue on the table at a time. What is the main thing? What is the issue? Who is the person? What decision was made that caused difficulty? As a pastor, you cannot allow the discussion to regress to personal and emotional attacks. Cooperation and understanding are the goals even though both sides are in opposition to one another. Thus, you must evaluate the conflict’s progress and readjust as often as needed. Here are some rules of discussion:

• People are allowed to give their position with energy and specific examples.

• All who are involved are protected from disrespect, hurtful words, labels, or false information.

• All people can disagree without feeling guilty.

• Even a small agreement or open discussion is viewed as progress and will be considered worthwhile.

“A gentle answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger. The tongue of the wise commends knowledge” (Proverbs 15:1–2).

Help the parties with considering other options. Do as much research and thinking as possible, and provide accurate information with successful examples of how this concern has been dealt with in other churches. Continue to think about how to bring viable alternatives for each person / side to consider.

Looking into other possibilities can take time. We should remind ourselves not to resolve the conflict too quickly. If it is under control, meaning that people are only talking to themselves and not others (gossiping) and they are willing to talk about options, then we need to let the process go on as long as reasonable. It is a mistake to jump to quick conclusions and offer surface (simple) solutions, when issues are typically much more complicated than that. Often we need to focus on the deeper history or source of the conflict.

Determine a time for each party to come to an adjusted, independent, and knowledgeable decision. Both sides need to feel they are free to make a decision after evaluating the facts. At times one side might compromise more than the other; however, this is not giving up or losing ground. This is adjusting an opinion based on knowledge.

People can subtly manipulate without realizing it. As pastor and mediator, we must guard against this, as the other person will feel used. Give both sides the respect needed to resolve the conflict.

Remind and encourage the parties that conflict is not a battle but a difference of opinion or a problem that needs to be resolved. Leaders understand that mediation does not provide all the answers or solutions; it is just a facilitation of healthy communication, openness, and growth.

We can continue to offer hope for the probability of coming to a solution—at some point. Christians can discuss, listen, and communicate in healthy ways and find the answer to the disagreement. At times you might need to remind them of the rules of discussion, or calm one or both parties down, gently rebuking them if one appears to be trying to just win, as in “I win; you lose.” That is not the goal. Unity in the body of Christ and being healthy Christians is the goal.

Remember that constructive criticism given with the right attitude and received with openness can be a tool for growth. However, gossip and destructive words will always retard growth.

As the conflict is resolved, discuss how it will look in the future. Conflict is never over until a plan or goal as to how the people will function in the future is agreed on. For example, consider the following questions. “How do you plan to respond when this comes up next week?” “What will you say to people when they say, ‘I hear you are not getting along with ______?’” Or, “Is it true that you do not like the direction the church is going?” The conflict is over, the plan has been determined, and the response to these kinds of questions should be decided upon in an honest, calm, and healing way.

Some conflicts have no answers, and the parties need to agree to disagree . People live in wonderful, healthy relationships all the time and do not agree on everything. In fact, I doubt that there are any two people who agree on everything . However, there are lots of people who are flexible and adjust to other people’s opinions for the sake of unity in the body of Christ. Unless it is a sinful situation, biblically wrong, or injurious of people, we can find a middle ground. That sometimes is expressed this way: “I do not see it like you do; however, we are Christians and we will work this out for the good of the church and the newer believers.”

If the issue does not call for a moral compromise or a legal action, consideration needs to be observed, as the other person simply has another opinion. The problem comes when people with different opinions express their views by attacking, producing guilt, or misinforming. Healthy disagreements can strengthen relationships and be a positive force.

When conflict arises because of a difference of opinion between individuals in the congregation and the pastor, the same format can be used. However, pastors need to involve elected deacons in the conflict if it has elevated to the point where an individual or a group is talking negatively.

It is necessary that the pastor talk to the people involved and give them as much correct information as possible. For example, “The reason we are changing the hymnbooks is because there are many new songs and choruses that we would like to use, and the old book does not have them.” Or, “The committee comprised of (names) has evaluated the condition of the carpet and has sought expert opinions on the new color and style as they made their decision. The deacons received three bids, and they feel this is the best decision for the church.” However, if the parties are in a hysterical and / or angry mode, perhaps someone should help calm them down before a meeting with the pastor. This person(s) should also explain the rules of Christian engagement (love, gentleness, understanding, and restoration of fellowship). He or she may need to serve as a moderator during the meeting with the pastor.

Knowledge and communication often settle the concern. However, we have another level of concern when there is a general feeling of dislike from some toward the pastor, or when he or she is labeled as not one of us , or when he or she is defined as not being as spiritual as some. Often this kind of conflict requires a unanimous deacon board that will wholeheartedly stand with the pastor and gently disagree with the opposing person or group. A united front is critical.

When church conflict is about a ministry program or direction, ask these questions:

What are the positive things about our church? (love, finances, people, and so forth)

What areas do we feel need to be worked on?

What are we doing right? What are we doing wrong?

What kind of church would you like us to become over the next five years?

What would you like our community to say about our church?

What needs are we overlooking in our congregation?

What do you feel we need to do to become healthier as a Christian body?

We continually need to remind ourselves that God is the One who changes hearts. We cannot force people to make correct spiritual decisions or to change their behavior. Daily, we bring the people and the situation to God and trust Him for wisdom. He will work behind the scenes with the people as He helps us know Him better and grow as spiritual leaders. Pastors are to hunger for a deeper relationship with God and learn to trust Him with all we are required to do. The Lord Jesus Christ will provide enough grace to deal effectively with people and any challenge that comes your way.

**Q:** How can additional alternatives aid a conflict resolution?

**A:** Often the parties have not considered a different option. Such options can possibly be win-win. A different option allows the parties to save face by not choosing the other people’s option.

**Q:** Is it truly possible to agree to disagree ?

**A:** People can live in healthy relationships without coming to agreement on every issue. Finding middle ground is often a function of disagreeing on the preferred approach. When the issue is not sin or moral compromise or necessitating legal action, resolution can focus on accepting differences and moving forward.

**Q:** What questions should be asked when the church is in a major conflict over a minor issue?

**A:** What are the positive things about our church? What areas do we feel need to be worked on? What are we doing right and what are we doing wrong? What kind of church would we like to be in five years? What would we like our community to say about our church? What needs are we overlooking in our congregation? What do we feel we need to do to become healthier as a Christian body?

**Q:** How does a healthy church manage conflict?

**A:** They address it rather than avoid it. They address it while maintaining mutual respect for one another. They seek the good of the group rather than personal preferences.

**Q:** How can gossip be addressed in a healthy manner?

**A:** The pastor must recognize that sometimes people need to talk about what concerns them. People need to be able to bring their concerns to leaders who can provide biblical guidance. The church can set a goal to provide a means to address every issue of potential conflict.

#### 5.2.2 — Define occasions where conflict cannot be fully resolved, and explain how to approach such circumstances.

Constructive conflict is part of every healthy relationship. In marriage, when we have differences with our spouse, it is of great value to be able to discuss the differences and come to a win-win decision. ### Conflict Can Grow a Healthy Church

#### 5.3.1 — Identify and explain how a church’s conflict management style indicates its overall spiritual and emotional health.

If a church body can learn to control and deal with conflict, it will be able to grow to another level. Effectively addressing conflict and being assertive in conflict management is a sign of health for a pastor and a church. However, not dealing with conflict in a biblical manner is harmful to any church. It is like exercise. When we do it correctly, we gain strength and endurance. It will be less painful to exercise after your body gets into condition. When churches begin to address areas that need conflict management, it is painful for a while. However, after the leadership is trained and the congregation understands that these kinds of issues are dealt with, it becomes easier for the church body. In physical training, when we lift weights incorrectly, we tear muscles and are sore for weeks. In conflict management, when we handle people and their concerns incorrectly, we often injure the body for months or years.

The overall body will be stronger when the pastor, leadership, department heads, and congregation all understand the necessity of effectively dealing with conflict.

Gossip can also be dealt with in a healthy way. We need to remind ourselves that at times people need to talk about things that concern them. When leadership is willing to talk with people about their issues in a timely manner, concerns are often brought to the surface sooner. Pastors and leadership can be a safe place where church members can work through their different issues. Leadership needs to know the reasons why the pastor has decided to do some things that might cause some parishioners to be concerned. Changing service times, choosing a new paint color for the sanctuary, putting an addition on the sanctuary, and so forth are just some examples where a prudent pastor will get the information to the leadership before he or she brings it to the body. Then, when people talk to leadership in any area, the leaders will be able to give the history, reason, and possible costs to the church. Remember, people will talk to somebody. Better to have the availability of knowledgeable people who are committed to you.

It should be the goal of every church to provide an environment where conflict is handled through honest communication and respect for all involved parties. If people do not feel safe and if they do not feel they have the ability to discuss issues of concern, then worries, fears, and different opinions can easily become gossip.

Many pastors have a difficult time confronting people. However, if they cannot confront in a gentle and firm (when necessary) way, there will be areas where people will gossip, or even be divisive. Asking yourself the following questions will help you think about why, or if, you struggle with confrontation.

• Am I afraid that I will appear to be the bad guy ?

• Am I avoiding an uncomfortable but necessary discussion?

• Do I feel guilty about something I have done, causing me to feel like I cannot talk to certain people?

• Do I fear that I will be misunderstood?

• Am I afraid that I will hurt the person or will push him or her over the edge?

• Do I fear the consequences—what the opposition will do?

• Am I not interested in solving things in a biblical, healing way?

All of these fears and concerns are normal; however, we need to ignore these natural fears when issues are to be addressed in the church. If we have pastored for very long, we understand that we will be misunderstood; some will view us as the bad guy ; we will always feel somewhat uncomfortable, and some will react in an irresponsible way.

We will never get everyone to agree on everything, but we can help people and the overall church to grow through this challenging issue. When people feel free to talk to you or other leaders, they feel appreciated and are glad their opinion is heard. Often these other opinions bring a new option to the surface or an idea that we did not think of. Creative ideas come from people who feel their opinion will be considered.

We can use different opinions to bring about constructive exchange. For years I have worked with church boards and have observed the principle of unanimity. When dealing with church purchases that were not in that year’s budget, adding new programs or staff, and addressing issues of concern, I have talked to the board, and we have used the following format for making a decision:

• Introduce the concern, need, or recommendation.

• Provide knowledgeable information, facts, history, bids, and so forth.

• Discuss the problem, item, and issue until all feel they understand.

• Make the decision.

• Agree to be ready to be responsible for the decision when questioned by people. For instance, when someone questions a board member about who made a particular decision, he or she will say that the board evaluated the situation, did the research, and together came to the conclusion.

In much the same way, individuals with opposing opinions can turn a conflict situation into healthy dialogue, by asking:

• What do we agree on?

• What do you think the underlying motive is for the different opinions?

• Why do I feel the way I do?

• What are the advantages and disadvantages of doing this my way?

• What will my choice cost, or how will my opinion help the church?

• Will my choice help the overall body (age groups, cultures, racial mix)?

• Do I have all the facts, or do I only know people’s opinions?

When people can realistically answer these kinds of questions, they will often come to their own conclusions and understand why change is happening, or why something needed to be addressed. Information, knowledge, and communication are important action points to remember as pastors.

We can create a healthy church where growth comes from normal conflict. Throughout the Bible, conflict issues are addressed. In Paul’s thirteen letters, he deals with concerns, sinful behavior, divisive people, and rebellious people—both non-Christian and Christian. Much of his instruction to Pastor Timothy was about how to deal with people and make decisions (some might be considered controversial). He did not run from it, but tactfully and assertively dealt with conflict as needed. As the Holy Spirit led him, he endeavored to be sensitive to timing and format in dealing with conflict. Following are some responses we can choose that will encourage an environment of growth from conflict.

Model humility in your attitude by being transparent about the fact that you have made mistakes, failed, and have at times been corrected and have grown through the process. The Scripture instructs:

Do nothing out of selfish ambition or vain conceit, but in humility consider others better than yourselves. Each of you should look not only to your own interests, but also to the interests of others. Your attitude should be the same as that of Christ Jesus: Who, being in very nature God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped, but made himself nothing, taking the very nature of a servant, being made in human likeness. And being found in appearance as a man, he humbled himself and became obedient to death—even death on a cross! Therefore God exalted him to the highest place and gave him the name that is above every name. (Philippians 2:3–9)

We live in a fallen world, and self-centered humankind does not appreciate humility. Our example of being humble will encourage the people we serve to have that attitude as well. To be humble means to be conscious of our weaknesses and quick to give credit to God and others for what we have accomplished (John 3:27; 5:19; 14:10; James 4:6).

We are to be humble because we are dependent on God for our worth and fruitfulness and really cannot accomplish anything of lasting good without God’s help and the help of others (Psalm 8:4–5; John 15:1–16).

God is close to those who walk humbly (Isaiah 57:15; Micah 6:8) and gives more grace to the humble. He opposes the proud (James 4:6; 1 Peter 5:5). His children are to serve “the Lord with great humility” (Acts 20:19).

As believers and leaders in God’s kingdom, we are to live in humility toward people, considering them more important than ourselves (Romans 12:3).

The antithesis of humility is pride, which is a magnification of self-importance and self-esteem. People who believe in their own merit, superiority, and accomplishment versus giving the glory to God and to the assistance they received from others are walking in pride, not humility. The normal tendency of human nature is toward pride (1 John 2:16; Isaiah 14:13–14; Ezekiel 28:17; 1 Timothy 6:17).

Encourage and thank people who bring out new creative ideas or think of new ways to get things done.

Be assertive and talk about attitudes that might fuel conflict before the decision is made to do something or change something.

Decide who might have other agendas, power struggles, or cultural issues.

Express esteem for all members of the church, not only for the people who are like you or who might agree with you. Everyone is entitled to his or her opinion. Everyone is not entitled to be divisive, stubborn, self-righteous, or demonstrate un-Christlike behavior.

Teach the importance of every member’s unique perspective and unique spiritual gifts.

Encourage the application of the priesthood of all believers, in that everyone has a valuable role in the body of Christ.

Be a model in the way you communicate. “Do not let any unwholesome talk come out of your mouths, but only what is helpful for building others up according to their needs, that it may benefit those who listen” (Ephesians 4:29).

Schedule action steps, timing, and who will get involved in the follow-through of any kind of conflict resolution.

Conflict can be your friend if you manage it effectively and teach leadership that assists you in the importance of having the right attitude and response toward it. Churches will become healthier and grow because of their effective creativity in establishing an environment that encourages growth from conflict.

**Q:** What are some ways a pastor can contribute to the congregation’s ability to manage conflict?

**A:** Model humility. Encourage and thank people who bring out new ideas. Be assertive and talk about attitudes that might fuel conflict. Express esteem for all members of the church. Teach about the importance of every member’s unique perspective. Be a model in your own communication.

**Q:** Why is humility such an important part in conflict?

**A:** We often are afraid of humility, but fearing it will cause us to lose the issue. We are unworthy of the esteem we often carry for our own opinions. The Bible tells us not to think of ourselves too highly. Pride becomes a road block to restoration.

#### 5.3.2 — List and explain the steps a leader can take in increasing the health of his or her group in managing conflict.

If a church body can learn to control and deal with conflict, it will be able to grow to another level. Effectively addressing conflict and being assertive in conflict management is a sign of health for a pastor and a church. However, not dealing with conflict in a biblical manner is harmful to any church. It is like exercise. When we do it correctly, we gain strength and endurance. It will be less painful to exercise after your body gets into condition. When churches begin to address areas that need conflict management, it is painful for a while. However, after the leadership is trained and the congregation understands that these kinds of issues are dealt with, it becomes easier for the church body. In physical training, when we lift weights incorrectly, we tear muscles and are sore for weeks. In conflict management, when we handle people and their concerns incorrectly, we often injure the body for months or years.

The overall body will be stronger when the pastor, leadership, department heads, and congregation all understand the necessity of effectively dealing with conflict.

Gossip can also be dealt with in a healthy way. We need to remind ourselves that at times people need to talk about things that concern them. When leadership is willing to talk with people about their issues in a timely manner, concerns are often brought to the surface sooner. Pastors and leadership can be a safe place where church members can work through their different issues. Leadership needs to know the reasons why the pastor has decided to do some things that might cause some parishioners to be concerned. Changing service times, choosing a new paint color for the sanctuary, putting an addition on the sanctuary, and so forth are just some examples where a prudent pastor will get the information to the leadership before he or she brings it to the body. Then, when people talk to leadership in any area, the leaders will be able to give the history, reason, and possible costs to the church. Remember, people will talk to somebody. Better to have the availability of knowledgeable people who are committed to you.

It should be the goal of every church to provide an environment where conflict is handled through honest communication and respect for all involved parties. If people do not feel safe and if they do not feel they have the ability to discuss issues of concern, then worries, fears, and different opinions can easily become gossip.

Many pastors have a difficult time confronting people. However, if they cannot confront in a gentle and firm (when necessary) way, there will be areas where people will gossip, or even be divisive. Asking yourself the following questions will help you think about why, or if, you struggle with confrontation.

• Am I afraid that I will appear to be the bad guy ?

• Am I avoiding an uncomfortable but necessary discussion?

• Do I feel guilty about something I have done, causing me to feel like I cannot talk to certain people?

• Do I fear that I will be misunderstood?

• Am I afraid that I will hurt the person or will push him or her over the edge?

• Do I fear the consequences—what the opposition will do?

• Am I not interested in solving things in a biblical, healing way?

All of these fears and concerns are normal; however, we need to ignore these natural fears when issues are to be addressed in the church. If we have pastored for very long, we understand that we will be misunderstood; some will view us as the bad guy ; we will always feel somewhat uncomfortable, and some will react in an irresponsible way.

We will never get everyone to agree on everything, but we can help people and the overall church to grow through this challenging issue. When people feel free to talk to you or other leaders, they feel appreciated and are glad their opinion is heard. Often these other opinions bring a new option to the surface or an idea that we did not think of. Creative ideas come from people who feel their opinion will be considered.

We can use different opinions to bring about constructive exchange. For years I have worked with church boards and have observed the principle of unanimity. When dealing with church purchases that were not in that year’s budget, adding new programs or staff, and addressing issues of concern, I have talked to the board, and we have used the following format for making a decision:

• Introduce the concern, need, or recommendation.

• Provide knowledgeable information, facts, history, bids, and so forth.

• Discuss the problem, item, and issue until all feel they understand.

• Make the decision.

• Agree to be ready to be responsible for the decision when questioned by people. For instance, when someone questions a board member about who made a particular decision, he or she will say that the board evaluated the situation, did the research, and together came to the conclusion.

In much the same way, individuals with opposing opinions can turn a conflict situation into healthy dialogue, by asking:

• What do we agree on?

• What do you think the underlying motive is for the different opinions?

• Why do I feel the way I do?

• What are the advantages and disadvantages of doing this my way?

• What will my choice cost, or how will my opinion help the church?

• Will my choice help the overall body (age groups, cultures, racial mix)?

• Do I have all the facts, or do I only know people’s opinions?

When people can realistically answer these kinds of questions, they will often come to their own conclusions and understand why change is happening, or why something needed to be addressed. Information, knowledge, and communication are important action points to remember as pastors.

We can create a healthy church where growth comes from normal conflict. Throughout the Bible, conflict issues are addressed. In Paul’s thirteen letters, he deals with concerns, sinful behavior, divisive people, and rebellious people—both non-Christian and Christian. Much of his instruction to Pastor Timothy was about how to deal with people and make decisions (some might be considered controversial). He did not run from it, but tactfully and assertively dealt with conflict as needed. As the Holy Spirit led him, he endeavored to be sensitive to timing and format in dealing with conflict. Following are some responses we can choose that will encourage an environment of growth from conflict.

Model humility in your attitude by being transparent about the fact that you have made mistakes, failed, and have at times been corrected and have grown through the process. The Scripture instructs:

Do nothing out of selfish ambition or vain conceit, but in humility consider others better than yourselves. Each of you should look not only to your own interests, but also to the interests of others. Your attitude should be the same as that of Christ Jesus: Who, being in very nature God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped, but made himself nothing, taking the very nature of a servant, being made in human likeness. And being found in appearance as a man, he humbled himself and became obedient to death—even death on a cross! Therefore God exalted him to the highest place and gave him the name that is above every name. (Philippians 2:3–9)

To be humble means to be conscious of our weaknesses and quick to give credit to God and others for what we have accomplished (John 3:27; 5:19; 14:10; James 4:6).

We are to be humble because we are dependent on God for our worth and fruitfulness and really cannot accomplish anything of lasting good without God’s help and the help of others (Psalm 8:4–5; John 15:1–16).

The normal tendency of human nature is toward pride (1 John 2:16; Isaiah 14:13–14; Ezekiel 28:17; 1 Timothy 6:17).

Encourage and thank people who bring out new creative ideas or think of new ways to get things done.

### Preventing Staff Infection

#### 5.4.1 — Understand and explain how ministry transition can add to the potential for conflict among the pastoral staff.

One of the most important things we do as leaders is the decision of whom we choose to hire or to be on the leadership team. Remember that once we make this decision, we have these individuals on our team. Making these calls is a big deal, and when we do this impulsively, we walk on dangerous ground. We understand that the larger the congregation, the more important it is to build a staff that complements and supports the biblical priorities of the senior pastor.

When a new senior pastor arrives, there is often hidden and direct conflict among the inherited staff. Often this comes from the void of leadership during the search process and competitiveness among the staff members. Possibly, one of the associate ministers wanted the job of senior pastor. The fact that the new senior pastor did not have the opportunity to interview the current staff before they were hired and that he or she probably would not have selected the inherited person(s) will cause some stress. Also, the associates might not particularly like the new pastor, so they may not immediately support him or her, thus creating loyalty issues. This will cause immediate (three to twelve months) tension and perhaps even cause undermining. I remember overhearing a conversation from two pastors I inherited. It went something like this: “He will catch on to how we do things around here; we should just pretend to agree with him and give him time.” This difficult reality is part of the process. Congregations and pastoral staff need to understand this delicate time that all churches experience.

It is common that a significant turnover happens within the first two to four years, following the selection of a new senior pastor. A pastoral change also means that job descriptions change among the staff pastors and leaders as the ministry positions are being replaced.

It is important that someone (pastor, consultant, coach) explain to the leadership of the church that this is normal. It is challenging because senior and associate pastors have built relationships, and there are loyalties among these people. Pastors who leave a pastorate need to understand that they should handle this time with extreme care and impeccable ethics. They can leave right (and help the church’s future), or they can leave wrong (and hurt the church’s future). When inherited staff leaves, this does not represent a weakness in the senior pastor’s ability to get along with people. Lay leadership and key congregational people can understand that.

It is normal and predictable institutional behavior when staff members change positions or find other employment when a new leader is employed. This is a product of the unique characteristics of larger congregations. It is a result of dominance of relational values over functional concerns. Most senior pastors feel more comfortable if they have seniority over the other pastors. It is the responsibility and privilege of the senior pastor to build a staff team that he or she feels comfortable with regarding what he or she believes is favorable for the health of the church.

Current research has shown that the number one reason why pastors quit and denominations cannot get more people to enter this occupation is conflict. There are conflict issues among the congregational members and conflict issues among the staff. More pastors quit because of this issue. They become discouraged and depressed with their lives and ministries and choose secular work over the ministry (Chang 2004).

What can senior pastors do to scientifically lower this dangerous trend? Do homework. I am often surprised when I hear of a pastor hiring a staff member because he or she has a decent resume. Little research is done, the references are not called, and the interview is short. With this behavior, it is no wonder that many staff relationships do not work out. In about fourteen years of senior pastor ministry, I have lost only one associate whom I have hired. I have lost several that I inherited (I did not interview them) but only one that I have brought on board. I think the reason is that my wife and I sincerely try to do our homework and pray about the match. We spend at least a weekend with them, usually in our home, during the interview process. This gives all of us a clear focus on whether or not the relationship will work.

Also, I always ask this question: “Is there anything that you have done, or are doing that would injure the witness of this church?” This question immediately brings to mind pornography issues, moral problems, marital difficulties, and hopefully ethical concerns. Take care of this before you hire anyone.

It is important that the pastor and staff member feel good about each other. Though there will be difficult times, there must be a confidence that is built on trust and team commitment. Both the pastor and staff pastor(s) work at getting along by mutual cooperation and building trust.

In the happiest two-pastor multiple staff teams, the senior pastor does perhaps 80 percent of the getting along with the associate, and the associate minister does about 80 percent of the getting along with the senior pastor. The result is a surplus of getting along. In the unhappy arrangements, each contributes 10 to 20 percent to that process, and the resulting deficit produces unhappiness (Schaller 1996, 102).

Communicate, communicate, and communicate. Talk about personal issues: “How is your family?” “What are your dreams” “Are you feeling fulfilled and happy with your job?” “How has your personality developed?” “Is there anything that I can do to help you do what you do?” “What are your expectations?” “How can I help you reach the goals we have set for your department?” “Is there anything you see as a barrier in reaching your goals?” These things are all part of communication: staff meetings, luncheons, stopping by their offices, and welcoming them in your office.

It takes time to build trust, but trust between staff members is critical. If the team does not trust the leader or vice-versa, a lack of trust will eventually damage the team.

The staff needs to be loyal to the pastor; however, the pastor must be loyal to the associates too. Remember that the associate works for the pastor, not the board or anyone else in the church. People may want to get rid of an associate pastor for any reason, but the senior pastor’s response should demonstrate confidence in his or her team. There can be complications, however.

If there are difficulties, we always tell the truth. People grow with the truth. When the associate feels that the pastor tells the truth and will try to help him or her with what he or she needs to do, then the pastor’s correction is often accepted. If the associate does not get along with someone else on staff, then the pastor needs to explain to both parties that the high road is to find a way to work it out, or the team will be injured.

The major issues that pastors can focus on in order to cure staff conflict are listed here.

Coherence (harmony): Personal and professional compatibility among the members of the staff team

Endurance : The importance of long-term relationships and long pastorates—nearly all of today’s rapidly growing congregations, which sustain that growth, have long-term pastorates and associates who stay longer than a few months or years.

Capability : When I ask someone to join the team, I hire spirit before I hire talent. A teachable spirit is critical. I have met too many associates who come straight out of college or seminary thinking they are going to fix the church or the pastor. I look for specific characteristics when hiring associates. They should be humble (trust God for their ability), hard working (possess a good work ethic), and smart (identify with the position, recognizing its personal and professional priorities of ministry).

Trust : In each other and from the members of the congregation. Trust is not automatic; it grows over time and by making one right decision after another.

Agreement and Adhesion : Consistency among the staff members as they display a logically connected set of professional relationships. Programs or ministry reinforces agreed-upon priorities in fulfilling the role God has called that congregation to accept. In other words, the team sticks together and agrees with the goals of the pastor.

Team : The staff members complement one another and make a fully rounded team, covering a wide variety of perspectives and needs. They are not to become carbon copies of the senior pastor, but they are to understand their unique roles and gifts while working together for a common goal of church health.

Understanding : Is there a high degree of agreement on the vision, goals, strategies, and purposes, or is the staff competing with each other?

What if there is a staff conflict going on? Whenever a pastor adds a staff pastor or multiple staff pastors, there will be disagreement and conflict. Everyone comes from different backgrounds, perspectives, and birth orders, and everyone has opinions. We want their opinions, but we must have their cooperation and desire to get along with the other staff members. Normal disagreements happen. You will recall the strong disagreement with Paul and Barnabas: “They had such a sharp disagreement that they parted company. Barnabas took Mark and sailed for Cyprus” (Acts 15:39). However, we see the maturity of the leaders in that they were “commended by the brothers to the grace of the Lord” and Paul “went through Syria and Cilicia, strengthening the churches” (vv. 40–41). Disagreements and being disagreeable are different things. As the leader, we must understand and talk this through with the members involved.

Once you hire a leader, the associate is not just a yes man or woman, but he or she is a leader with ideas. Permitting the staff to discuss their various ideas, but finding a way to come to a balanced, healthy opinion is a skill that we can grow in. The church will enjoy a variety of strong ministries when the staff is permitted to lead while being loyal.

The staff may disagree, debate, and discuss, but they should not divide. I remember hearing of a large staff who would yell at each other in staff meetings, and on one occasion, an associate became so angry that he actually threw water from his glass onto the senior pastor! This is not healthy disagreement; this is reason for dismissal. I have heard divisive comments from associates—competing with each other over ministry or personal goals. This is not healthy and will not help the church. It is the senior pastor’s responsibility to permit healthy debate and let staff disagree, but never divide. The meeting should end with everyone still on the team.

Find out where the conflict is coming from, and identify the primary leader of the conflict. The best way is to sit down with each person individually (the two sides) and sort it out. Doing this brings understanding to the pastor, and he or she can determine the best direction to go.

Discover if this conflict is a personal issue or a professional opinion. Personal issues are different from opinions about how to do church . Personal opinions are handled the way God wants us to (especially by leaders): “as far as it depends on you, live at peace with everyone” (Romans 12:18). “If a brother sins against you, go and show him his fault, just between the two of you” (Matthew 18:15). “Lord, how many times shall I forgive my brother? . . . not seven times, but seventy-seven times” (Matthew 18:21–22).

The people involved need to understand that the congregation hears a lot and sees a lot. As ministers of the gospel, we are responsible to work out the differences.

The miracle of dialogue: Getting the two people together and letting them “air it out” is sometimes a good thing to do. If you as a pastor can serve as a coach and remove your personal bias, you can assist these valuable leaders in finding a middle ground. Or, it could be that you help them agree to disagree, but for the sake of the church and the team, pray for each other and demonstrate loyalty to each other.

Seeing the big picture: Sometimes staff members forget the big picture. What is this all about? We are part of an incredible Kingdom, and the church of Jesus Christ is the most powerful organization on earth. We are honored to be leaders in God’s church, and our hearts and opinions need to fit into that perspective. If we do not work this out (the issue at hand), we will not help God’s kingdom—we will hurt it.

Acting like Christians: Before we are leaders, we are Christians. Recall the story of the disciples arguing with each other about who was the greatest (Mark 9:33–37). These men were competing with each other and acted like we do. Jesus said, “If anyone wants to be first, he must be the very last, and the servant of all” (Mark 9:35). Helping the associate team understand kingdom rules for leadership is part of what you do as a pastor.

The eyes of the world are on you: The congregation, community, Christians, and non-Christians know whether the team is getting along or if it is rough going. We are in that city to win the city for Christ. We cannot do it alone, but we can develop the team to pray together, dream together, and strategize how to reach them. It has always amazed me when I have received a phone call from the newspaper, television station, or a letter from a neighbor informing me that “we heard that this is going on.” How did they know? People are always watching us. We do live in a fishbowl.

I get involved if I need to: I let team members discuss, debate, disagree for quite a while, but when I feel they could injure themselves, the team, or anyone in the congregation, I get involved. I often watch the coach of our Wake Forest basketball team. This group of players is often rated in the top ten in the country. Hopefully, they have one goal: to win. For them to win, the coach has the delicate job of calling the strategy for how to win the game. However, he must let the players be their best while working together as a team. If one member wants to do his own thing, the coach has a problem. He could lose the game because this person is arrogant and does not see the big picture. The coach must talk to this player and also appreciate his zeal. Talking, following through, and being sensitive to the challenges of having multiple staff is part of what we do.

Always remind yourself that conflict among the staff is normal. As leaders we are managers of conflict and peacemakers within the tension. Submit to the Holy Spirit to receive His guidance in managing conflict, and He will answer your prayers for emotional healing for the parties involved.

**Q:** Why is there great potential for conflict among the church staff when a new pastor arrives?

**A:** There can be conflict among the staff due to competitiveness in establishing their places with the new pastor. One or more of the staff may have desired the position of senior pastor. The new pastor did not get to interview the staff and may have inherited someone he or she would not have chosen to hire. The staff may not like the new pastor or his or her different way of doing things.

**Q:** How can a pastor help a church in this time of transition?

**A:** The pastor or another leader must explain to the church that this is a normal experience and will take a period of months to overcome. People must expect some additional transition in the first few years. The pastor must ultimately be allowed to build a team with which he is comfortable.

**Q:** What should a congregation know about staff relationships when they are facing a transition?

**A:** It is normal and predictable for staff to change positions when a new leader is employed. A pastor will feel most comfortable with a staff he or she has seniority over. A staff member’s decision to leave does not indicate that the new pastor lacks relational skills.

**Q:** What must a pastor do to limit unhealthy conflict among his staff?

**A:** Do your homework in the hiring process. Pay attention to the feelings you have around one another. Communicate with one another. Target building trust as the greatest goal. Extend loyalty to one another. Always tell the truth.

**Q:** What are the major issues for a pastor to focus on in hiring staff members?

**A:** Cohesion—the compatibility of the team. Endurance—the importance of long-term relationships. Capability—prioritize teachability ahead of high performance. Trust—does it exist in all their relationships? Agreement—do they agree on the priorities and goals of the ministry they will lead? Team—Do they complement one another or are they a carbon copy of each other?

**Q:** What principles should a pastor keep in focus as he or she addresses conflicts among the staff?

**A:** Normal disagreements happen. You have hired leaders with ideas and the ability to get them done. They can disagree and discuss, but never divide. In conflict, you must determine the source individual. Determine if conflict is personal or professional in nature. Create the opportunities for dialogue. UNIT 3
