They become discouraged and depressed with their lives and ministries and choose secular work over the ministry (Chang 2004).

What can senior pastors do to scientifically lower this dangerous trend? Do homework. I am often surprised when I hear of a pastor hiring a staff member because he or she has a decent resume. Little research is done, the references are not called, and the interview is short. With this behavior, it is no wonder that many staff relationships do not work out. In about fourteen years of senior pastor ministry, I have lost only one associate whom I have hired. I have lost several that I inherited (I did not interview them) but only one that I have brought on board. I think the reason is that my wife and I sincerely try to do our homework and pray about the match. We spend at least a weekend with them, usually in our home, during the interview process. This gives all of us a clear focus on whether or not the relationship will work.

Also, I always ask this question: “Is there anything that you have done, or are doing that would injure the witness of this church?” This question immediately brings to mind pornography issues, moral problems, marital difficulties, and hopefully ethical concerns. Take care of this before you hire anyone.

It is important that the pastor and staff member feel good about each other. Though there will be difficult times, there must be a confidence that is built on trust and team commitment. Both the pastor and staff pastor(s) work at getting along by mutual cooperation and building trust.

In the happiest two-pastor multiple staff teams, the senior pastor does perhaps 80 percent of the getting along with the associate, and the associate minister does about 80 percent of the getting along with the senior pastor. The result is a surplus of getting along. In the unhappy arrangements, each contributes 10 to 20 percent to that process, and the resulting deficit produces unhappiness (Schaller 1996, 102).

Communicate, communicate, and communicate. Talk about personal issues: “How is your family?” “What are your dreams” “Are you feeling fulfilled and happy with your job?” “How has your personality developed?” “Is there anything that I can do to help you do what you do?” “What are your expectations?” “How can I help you reach the goals we have set for your department?” “Is there anything you see as a barrier in reaching your goals?” These things are all part of communication: staff meetings, luncheons, stopping by their offices, and welcoming them in your office.

It takes time to build trust, but trust between staff members is critical. If the team does not trust the leader or vice-versa, a lack of trust will eventually damage the team.

The staff needs to be loyal to the pastor; however, the pastor must be loyal to the associates too. Remember that the associate works for the pastor, not the board or anyone else in the church. People may want to get rid of an associate pastor for any reason, but the senior pastor’s response should demonstrate confidence in his or her team. There can be complications, however.

If there are difficulties, we always tell the truth. People grow with the truth. When the associate feels that the pastor tells the truth and will try to help him or her with what he or she needs to do, then the pastor’s correction is often accepted. If the associate does not get along with someone else on staff, then the pastor needs to explain to both parties that the high road is to find a way to work it out, or the team will be injured.

The major issues that pastors can focus on in order to cure staff conflict are listed here.

Coherence (harmony): Personal and professional compatibility among the members of the staff team

Endurance : The importance of long-term relationships and long pastorates—nearly all of today’s rapidly growing congregations, which sustain that growth, have long-term pastorates and associates who stay longer than a few months or years.

Capability : When I ask someone to join the team, I hire spirit before I hire talent. A teachable spirit is critical. I have met too many associates who come straight out of college or seminary thinking they are going to fix the church or the pastor. I look for specific characteristics when hiring associates. They should be humble (trust God for their ability), hard working (possess a good work ethic), and smart (identify with the position, recognizing its personal and professional priorities of ministry).

Trust : In each other and from the members of the congregation. Trust is not automatic; it grows over time and by making one right decision after another.

Agreement and Adhesion : Consistency among the staff members as they display a logically connected set of professional relationships. Programs or ministry reinforces agreed-upon priorities in fulfilling the role God has called that congregation to accept. In other words, the team sticks together and agrees with the goals of the pastor.

Team : The staff members complement one another and make a fully rounded team, covering a wide variety of perspectives and needs. They are not to become carbon copies of the senior pastor, but they are to understand their unique roles and gifts while working together for a common goal of church health.

Understanding : Is there a high degree of agreement on the vision, goals, strategies, and purposes, or is the staff competing with each other?

What if there is a staff conflict going on? Whenever a pastor adds a staff pastor or multiple staff pastors, there will be disagreement and conflict. Everyone comes from different backgrounds, perspectives, and birth orders, and everyone has opinions. We want their opinions, but we must have their cooperation and desire to get along with the other staff members. Normal disagreements happen. You will recall the strong disagreement with Paul and Barnabas: “They had such a sharp disagreement that they parted company. Barnabas took Mark and sailed for Cyprus” (Acts 15:39). However, we see the maturity of the leaders in that they were “commended by the brothers to the grace of the Lord” and Paul “went through Syria and Cilicia, strengthening the churches” (vv. 40–41). Disagreements and being disagreeable are different things. As the leader, we must understand and talk this through with the members involved.

Once you hire a leader, the associate is not just a yes man or woman, but he or she is a leader with ideas. Permitting the staff to discuss their various ideas, but finding a way to come to a balanced, healthy opinion is a skill that we can grow in. The church will enjoy a variety of strong ministries when the staff is permitted to lead while being loyal.

The staff may disagree, debate, and discuss, but they should not divide. I remember hearing of a large staff who would yell at each other in staff meetings, and on one occasion, an associate became so angry that he actually threw water from his glass onto the senior pastor! This is not healthy disagreement; this is reason for dismissal. I have heard divisive comments from associates—competing with each other over ministry or personal goals. This is not healthy and will not help the church. It is the senior pastor’s responsibility to permit healthy debate and let staff disagree, but never divide. The meeting should end with everyone still on the team.

Find out where the conflict is coming from, and identify the primary leader of the conflict. The best way is to sit down with each person individually (the two sides) and sort it out. Doing this brings understanding to the pastor, and he or she can determine the best direction to go.

Discover if this conflict is a personal issue or a professional opinion. Personal issues are different from opinions about how to do church . Personal opinions are handled the way God wants us to (especially by leaders): “as far as it depends on you, live at peace with everyone” (Romans 12:18). “If a brother sins against you, go and show him his fault, just between the two of you” (Matthew 18:15). “Lord, how many times shall I forgive my brother? . . . not seven times, but seventy-seven times” (Matthew 18:21–22).

The people involved need to understand that the congregation hears a lot and sees a lot. As ministers of the gospel, we are responsible to work out the differences.

The miracle of dialogue: Getting the two people together and letting them “air it out” is sometimes a good thing to do. If you as a pastor can serve as a coach and remove your personal bias, you can assist these valuable leaders in finding a middle ground. Or, it could be that you help them agree to disagree, but for the sake of the church and the team, pray for each other and demonstrate loyalty to each other.

Seeing the big picture: Sometimes staff members forget the big picture. What is this all about? We are part of an incredible Kingdom, and the church of Jesus Christ is the most powerful organization on earth. We are honored to be leaders in God’s church, and our hearts and opinions need to fit into that perspective. If we do not work this out (the issue at hand), we will not help God’s kingdom—we will hurt it.

Acting like Christians: Before we are leaders, we are Christians. Recall the story of the disciples arguing with each other about who was the greatest (Mark 9:33–37). These men were competing with each other and acted like we do. Jesus said, “If anyone wants to be first, he must be the very last, and the servant of all” (Mark 9:35). Helping the associate team understand kingdom rules for leadership is part of what you do as a pastor.

The eyes of the world are on you: The congregation, community, Christians, and non-Christians know whether the team is getting along or if it is rough going. We are in that city to win the city for Christ. We cannot do it alone, but we can develop the team to pray together, dream together, and strategize how to reach them. It has always amazed me when I have received a phone call from the newspaper, television station, or a letter from a neighbor informing me that “we heard that this is going on.” How did they know? People are always watching us. We do live in a fishbowl.

I get involved if I need to: I let team members discuss, debate, disagree for quite a while, but when I feel they could injure themselves, the team, or anyone in the congregation, I get involved. I often watch the coach of our Wake Forest basketball team. This group of players is often rated in the top ten in the country. Hopefully, they have one goal: to win. For them to win, the coach has the delicate job of calling the strategy for how to win the game. However, he must let the players be their best while working together as a team. If one member wants to do his own thing, the coach has a problem. He could lose the game because this person is arrogant and does not see the big picture. The coach must talk to this player and also appreciate his zeal. Talking, following through, and being sensitive to the challenges of having multiple staff is part of what we do.

Always remind yourself that conflict among the staff is normal. As leaders we are managers of conflict and peacemakers within the tension. Submit to the Holy Spirit to receive His guidance in managing conflict, and He will answer your prayers for emotional healing for the parties involved.

**Q:** Why is there great potential for conflict among the church staff when a new pastor arrives?

**A:** There can be conflict among the staff due to competitiveness in establishing their places with the new pastor. One or more of the staff may have desired the position of senior pastor. The new pastor did not get to interview the staff and may have inherited someone he or she would not have chosen to hire. The staff may not like the new pastor or his or her different way of doing things.

**Q:** How can a pastor help a church in this time of transition?

**A:** The pastor or another leader must explain to the church that this is a normal experience and will take a period of months to overcome. People must expect some additional transition in the first few years. The pastor must ultimately be allowed to build a team with which he is comfortable.

**Q:** What should a congregation know about staff relationships when they are facing a transition?

**A:** It is normal and predictable for staff to change positions when a new leader is employed. A pastor will feel most comfortable with a staff he or she has seniority over. A staff member’s decision to leave does not indicate that the new pastor lacks relational skills.

**Q:** What must a pastor do to limit unhealthy conflict among his staff?

**A:** Do your homework in the hiring process. Pay attention to the feelings you have around one another. Communicate with one another. Target building trust as the greatest goal. Extend loyalty to one another. Always tell the truth.

**Q:** What are the major issues for a pastor to focus on in hiring staff members?

**A:** Cohesion—the compatibility of the team. Endurance—the importance of long-term relationships. Capability—prioritize teachability ahead of high performance. Trust—does it exist in all their relationships? Agreement—do they agree on the priorities and goals of the ministry they will lead? Team—Do they complement one another or are they a carbon copy of each other?

**Q:** What principles should a pastor keep in focus as he or she addresses conflicts among the staff?

**A:** Normal disagreements happen. You have hired leaders with ideas and the ability to get them done. They can disagree and discuss, but never divide. In conflict, you must determine the source individual. Determine if conflict is personal or professional in nature. Create the opportunities for dialogue. UNIT 3

## 6 - The Addressing of Conflict

### Getting a Grip on Resolving Conflict

#### 6.1.1 — Explain the impact of prolonged conflict and some of the reasons it can exist.

It has been said that “the only pastors who do not experience regular, character-building periods of conflict are either bullies who walk all over everyone or cowards afraid to stand up for what God wants to accomplish” (Rowell 1997, 9). However, as we have examined, many pastors quit or become paralyzed with their vision because of their inability to resolve conflicts within their ministry. Continual conflict will take a toll on everyone who is involved. When pastors experience conflict monthly, weekly, or sometimes daily, it will wear on them; emotional exhaustion is common.

In his study on pastoral tenure, John LaRue found that the typical pastor of forty-six (the mean age for his study) has a tenure of four and nine-tenths years. In ministry for approximately eighteen years, he has changed churches three times and has only neared the mid part of his career. LaRue claims that since the mid-1970s, tenure has declined from an average of seven years per church to less than five. LaRue offers four reasons why pastors leave:

1. The pastor acquired a new vision or sensed a call to a new place: 37 percent of the respondents.

2. The pastor achieved a sense of resolution: the ministry was complete or could go no farther under his leadership: 26 percent

3. The pastor was given an opportunity to do something greater elsewhere (in other words, occupational advancement or promotion): 23 percent

4. Unresolved or ongoing tension or trouble within a church or ministry: 18 percent (LaRue 1995, 56).

Prolonged conflict will hurt the leader. Those who have experienced the tension of prolonged conflict in a church understand how it can be wearing not only on pastor professionally, but on his or her health, emotions, marriage, and family as well.

The deep sense of happiness of being a pastor is greatly diminished when a pastor is constantly reminded of problems and must deal with conflict, complaints, and criticisms from parishioners, employees, and lay leaders.

Prolonged conflict will hurt the church’s potential. The energy and sense of spiritual passion should be focused on God’s dream for that church and that community. Finding time to meditate, pray, and think about how to help the church become healthier and focusing on reaching the community are vital parts of a pastor’s life. Without this time or vision, the church’s potential is gravely damaged.

Prolonged conflict will hurt the people. Conflict can always be found in organizations or churches; however, when it is prolonged with no end in sight, people will get frustrated and intensify (not relieve) the conflict.

Sometimes there is a conflict within the leadership team. Some of the reasons for this are easy to understand. Here is a listing of those reasons:

• Both introverted and extroverted personalities can be involved. Some depend on facts and logic, and others lean on intuition and feelings. Some are spontaneous in their various ministry styles, and others are planners and detail people.

• People will have differences in values.

• People will have differences in theological opinions.

• People will have differences in musical preferences

• People will have different marital experiences. (Some are divorced and remarried).

• People will have different perspectives or perceptions. (A person’s perception is his or her reality.)

When we lead, there are always people with personality differences at the decision table. This is the way people are wired, and we are wise to take the time to understand the people who are part of the team we lead. Following are some quotes you will commonly hear. (You will have a mixture of these perspectives in almost any church.)

“I wanted to talk to the pastor and convince him (or her) that contemporary choruses are the only way to go, but he (or she) is so traditional and wants to use only hymns.”

“I wanted to talk to the pastor and convince him (or her) that traditional hymns of the church are what the people really want, but he does not appreciate the old timers.”

“The pastor is only interested in young people and he (or she) has forgotten the older money people.”

“The pastor is only interested in the older crowd. In fact, he is old and does not care about the youth of our city.”

Cultural differences can contribute to conflict. All cultures have distinct rules and ways of managing conflict. One rule might be in how direct and face-to-face a person needs to be in confrontation. Some might consider this rude and uncultured. Some Asian cultures tend to approach conflict indirectly and obliquely. There is a Chinese proverb that humorously says, “Do not remove a fly from your neighbor’s face with a hatchet.” In Asia, saving face is important. Asians want to maintain harmony and minimize potential conflict. Americans are known for being direct. They will typically hit the issue head on and just deal with it, sometimes having the thought, “Let the cards fall where they will.” Pastoring different cultures, ages, perspectives, and opinions takes a deeper level of sensitivity.

When There Does Not Seem to Be a Point of Closure

If a pastor is leading a church that cannot seem to get through trouble or tension, then the following suggestions will be helpful.

Find people who will pray for you and who believe that God has called you to be the pastor of that church. Aaron and Hur held up the arms of Moses when he was in battle against the Amalekites (Exodus 17:8–13). Pastors need people around them (sometimes it is only two) who will continually do this as a ministry. We need people who will pray for us, counsel us, and allow us to cry and to express our pain and frustration periodically.

Keep a Clear Perspective

When Timothy was going through an exodus of people from his church because of persecution and fear (under the hand of Nero), he became concerned and afraid. Paul wrote and said, “But you, keep your head in all situations” (2 Timothy 4:5). As a pastor, one of your roles is to solve problems; however, if you let the pressure get to you and you lose your perspective, there will be no clear focus. This is not always easy in the ministry. It is important to be emotionally healthy, maintain a strong marriage, stay in physical condition, eat right, and get enough sleep, as all of this will help you keep your head .

Remember that many of your people will be very happy with you as a pastor. We tend to let the few get to us, and most of the congregation has no idea what these few are talking about. We must keep “the big picture” in mind as we are called to serve this church.

We need to remember that we have been called by God to do this as an occupation. Conflict is part of pastoring, and people in spiritual leadership have always had difficult people. Conflict is not a sign that we are out of the will of God. In fact, it might be a sign that we are doing exactly what the Lord has asked us to do. The difficulty could be as simple as just having two different opinions—both might be right. Perspective—a twenty-twenty vision of what is really going on—is important.

Remember What You Have Prayed About and How God Has Answered Your Prayers

Some write these things down; some consistently pray about a person or situation and remember how God delivered or answered in that situation. There have been numerous occasions where I have just turned a person over to the Lord. At times I have chosen to not respond but to remain calm and quiet, knowing that the Lord will handle this person. I can remember many people, occasions, and situations where I have prayed to the Shepherd of the Church because I understand that I am an under-shepherd in one of His locations.

Remember “A Leader Is a Reader; a Reader Is a Leader” and We Must Be Lifelong Learners

We need to continually read, study, and talk to people who are experts with experience. This desire to learn and grow should never end. We are always in need of more information, better advice, and counsel from those who have been there, done that . I try to read continually on different subjects. I listen to CDs, tapes, watch DVDs, and attend seminars that will help me improve in my vocation.

Take Your Day Off and Your Vacation and Get Enough Sleep

When you are tired, you will begin to have a different perspective and become more discouraged. Your energy and ability to handle conflict will be diminished. Getting rest, taking your day off and your vacation, and getting enough sleep and exercise are all energy producers and times when God refreshes your spirit and body. Violating these principles will be detrimental to you.

Be Careful about What Obsesses Your Thinking

Continual stress can promote unhealthy thought patterns. When you are relaxed, your mind will be able to process information more clearly, and you will be able to prioritize better. Prolonged stress can also make you feel overanxious and somewhat confused. Getting a grip on what you are thinking, and controlling this negative pattern will assist you in focusing on the most accurate strategy for what you are facing.

As you have discovered, conflict is part of what we do. But we are not alone. Every leader has to deal with conflict. Understanding the right time to confront, how to manage conflict, and how to understand ways to appropriately handle it are all parts of the sometimes-complicated puzzle. However, God chose pastors to lead churches. Wherever we have people, we will have challenges. With that, we can assume there will be difficult days and troubling people. We are the church of Jesus Christ and the most powerful institution on earth.

**Q:** How can unresolved conflict hurt the leader?

**A:** Such conflict diminishes his or her happiness. Such conflict brings difficulties and complaints to the leader’s thoughts more readily.

**Q:** How were Aaron and Hur important to Moses?

**A:** They provided a level of support that demonstrated their understanding of his task. They aided him in areas of his weakness.

**Q:** Explain what is meant by keeping a clear perspective.

**A:** In conflict, one’s view can become imbalanced. The leader should remember that there are individuals that are pleased with his or her leadership. The leader must remember that he or she is called by God for the work he or she pursues. Conflict should not be viewed as an indicator that one is no longer in the parameters of God’s will.

**Q:** How can a pastor’s reading habits impact his or her conflict skills?

**A:** The pastor should nourish himself or herself from the experiences and insights of others. The pastor can seek counsel from those who have written of similar experiences. When a pastor continues to learn and grow, he or she gains strength to handle individual points of stress.

**Q:** How does physical rest aid in conflict management?

**A:** Those who are tired tend to make issues of conflict larger than they truly are. Physical strength translates into emotional and spiritual strength.

**Q:** Why is it generally better to deal with conflict sooner as opposed to later?

**A:** Conflict has a tendency to grow, particularly if it is deeply rooted. When conflict grows, it quickly leaves controllable boundaries and becomes nearly impossible to solve. Unless a conflict will fade on its own it will ultimately require increasing amounts of time, energy, and resources to solve.

**Q:** Why is it generally better to deal with conflict sooner as opposed to later?

**A:** Conflict has a tendency to grow, particularly if it is deeply rooted. When conflict grows, it quickly leaves controllable boundaries and becomes nearly impossible to solve. Unless a conflict will fade on its own it will ultimately require increasing amounts of time, energy, and resources to solve.

### Nipping Conflict in the Bud

#### 6.2.1 — Describe and justify the need to handle conflict quickly.

Deal with conflict sooner—rather than later. The longer a conflict is allowed to go on, the more time, expense, and energy it will take to fix.

Suppose that we have two leaders in our Royal Rangers program who feel responsible for determining the schedule and training for a particular quarter. You have selected both of them to become leaders, have given them encouragement and direction, and have seen both grow in knowledge and expertise. Now the program has grown to a point where many boys are involved and both leaders, independently of each other, have determined the schedule and training. Both of them are right; however, a decision has to be made because only one person should be responsible for the timeline and training.

A molehill of a conflict is beginning to form in this department of the church. This decision is important to both individuals (as it should be) and should not be a divisive conflict issue.

As the pastor, you could decide to ignore the issue, hoping it will work itself out. However, after several days, numerous e-mails, phone calls, and conversations from both people to their friends, sides are beginning to form. Some believe their friend should make the decisions because he or she has been in the church longer, others believe their friend should make the decisions because he or she is in upper management in his or her job. The larger group begins talking behind the smaller group’s back and forming opinions about who is right and who is wrong. Now, the molehill is becoming a small mountain. The others who have become involved are emotional and feel you are missing the obvious. The Royal Rangers department is divided; the kids have no idea what is going on—they just want to have their outings and events—and the energy that it will take to fix the problem requires ten times more effort now than it would have from the inception.

What I am describing is the 1– 10 – 100 rule, where early in the conflict, involvement by you would have been at a 1 level; now it is at a 10 level.

Now you receive calls from parents of the Royal Rangers. They want to understand why the two leaders no longer talk to each other and the department seems to be stuck. A son feels the tension in the room and does not want to come any longer. Neither of the leaders will return phone calls, so the parents decide to call you. You are told that other parents are also concerned and are thinking about going to another church that seems more organized in their children’s activities. The original molehill has just become a huge mountain. This family is lost from your church, the other Royal Ranger leaders are at odds with each other and have taken sides, and the church board wants to know what is going on. The energy cost of resolving the conflict now is one hundred times (1–10– 100 ) what it would have been had you become involved in the beginning. Major damage may never be undone.

Most of the church conflict issues we face begin with poor communication and not taking the time to understand or to be understood. There is no valid reason for a pastor to fall into that trap with his or her congregation. Proverbs tells us, “Blessed is the man who finds wisdom, the man who gains understanding” (3:13). If a conflict arises within any of your leadership team, find out what is going on. Talk to all parties involved; describe the negative impact of their involving others and forming sides; explain your opinion on how a decision can be made to help them work through the issue.

When you have church growth, you will have differences in opinion, and potential conflict will most likely come with each viewpoint. As long as people are involved with more programs, opinions, agendas, ideas, and scheduling issues, there will be an eventual collision of who is right and who is wrong. If you leave it alone to work itself out, the conflict can be devastating to the church’s effectiveness.

Pastors must keep their eyes and ears open for potential conflict between leaders, addressing suspected issues as quickly as possible. I often take the role of roving manager . I occasionally walk around the various departments and talk to parents, leaders, and kids, as well as look at the rooms and get a sense of what is going on. Now, there are pastors that do just the opposite; they ignore conflicts because they hate conflict. That is understandable. However, ignoring a conflict only creates a larger conflict in most instances. When that happens, they expend a lot of their energy and emotion solving problems, much more than they would have if they had acted earlier.

The first deacons of the church were chosen because of a conflict of opinions. Acts 6 tells us this:

In those days when the number of disciples was increasing, the Grecian Jews among them complained against the Hebraic Jews because their widows were being overlooked in the daily distribution of food. So the Twelve gathered all the disciples together and said, “It would not be right for us to neglect the ministry of the Word of God in order to wait on tables. Brothers, choose seven men from among you who are known to be full of the Spirit and wisdom. We will turn this responsibility over to them and will give our attention to prayer and the ministry of the word.” (Acts 6:1–4)

From that passage, we learn the following:

• The church was growing.

• There were complaints from one group against another because they felt their group was being treated unfairly.

• The pastors (twelve) talked and prayed and came up with a way to deal with the conflict. (They took time to listen to the sides and come to an understanding about how to manage the need).

• The pastors decided that the conflict was too time-consuming and would take the time they needed to pray and study, so they found people who had solid spiritual understanding and wisdom to follow through with the challenge.

They chose seven leaders to handle the responsibility and keep them informed. As a result:

• The people were satisfied.

• The conflict went away.

• The church kept growing.

I have not met a pastor who enjoys dealing with conflict within his or her leadership team or congregation. Conflicts are represented by two different opinions that are often contradictory. Both of the opinions may, on the surface, seem equally correct and valid. When a situation has no obvious right or wrong position, the energy and time it takes to address problems can be exhausting, but it must be done.

As Robert Collier said, “One might as well try to ride two horses moving in different directions, as to try to maintain, in equal force, two opposing or contradictory sets of desires (Cottrel and Harvey 2004, 63).

In the Duke University Pulpit and Pew think tank that I was involved in, we studied the reasons ministers quit or were fired from their ministry. The number one reason was conflict issues. However, within this top reason was conflict with the staff. In other words, pastors quit or were fired because they could not get their staff to get along with each other and / or with them. Conflicts cannot be ignored or neglected on the premise that they will work themselves out. They will not, and we need to understand that this is part of what we do. People have always had issues, opinions, personalities, experiences, and different educational levels. Pastoring is about people, and we are naive to think that people will just get along.

Ignoring a molehill will not make it go away; it will continue to get larger and larger. If we ignore it long enough, eventually it will become a mountain that will stop the vision.

The conflict resolution technique of the 1–10–100 rule or molehill-to-mountains technique tells us to become involved sooner rather than later. The longer a conflict exists without being identified and addressed, the more energy, time, and people it will involve and the more difficult it will be to resolve or fix. If you cannot become quickly involved, then a church leader, associate pastor, or designated person needs to be given the assignment. Delegate the responsibility to someone and ask him or her to keep you informed.

John Madden was a commentator for the National Football League on CBS, FOX and ABC. Before that he was a coach of the Oakland Raiders football team. He observed, “Coaches have to watch for what they do not want to see and listen for what they do not want to hear” (Cottrel and Harvey 2004, 62). As a pastor, you can apply the 1–10–100 rule in many situations. It could be interpersonal conflict between two associate pastors, lay leaders, secretaries, board members, choir members, or so forth. Remember that the probable reason for any conflict is lack of communication. If we choose to walk away or ignore the conflicts that we face in our church, there are two common results: (1) The conflict will negatively impact the church, the momentum, and the leadership team ministry performance; and (2) rather than fixing itself, it will get larger and eventually cause division in the church.

**Q:** What is the “1–10–100” rule?

**A:** This rule illustrates the exponential growth of conflict that is unresolved. Initially a conflict may be at the “1” level, but it begins to involve others and moves rapidly to the “10” level. If left unresolved, it can explode to the “100” level, where it will require 100 times the energy and time to resolve than would have been necessary were it handled at a “1” level.

**Q:** What was the issue of conflict in Acts 6?

**A:** There were widows and others being neglected in the distribution of food by the church in Jerusalem. There was a cultural and racial issue brewing. The apostles were being called upon to deal with issues different from their giftings and assignment.

**Q:** What evidence is offered in the story that indicates the issue was resolved successfully?

**A:** The people were satisfied. The apostles were able to focus on their assignment. Others were involved in meaningful ways. The conflict ended. The church continued its growth.

**Q:** What are the two common results of walking away from conflict?

**A:** The conflict will negatively impact the church and compromise any established momentum. The conflict will grow and eventually cause division within the church.
