### Understanding Escalation in Conflict

#### 6.3.1 — Identify and explain the key reasons why normal conflict can move to aggressive and destructive conflict.

The bedroom community in the resort town enjoyed seasonal growth as the summers came and the winter snow fell. Lakes, summer cabins, and winter ski resorts were the draw during these two seasons. People from larger cities visited the area because of the entertainment and recreation; however, the people who lived in the town year around were the real decision makers and the ones who held the businesses together. Along with the growth in the businesses came growth in the small churches in the area. A visitor typically attended the church of the denomination that he or she was familiar with. The seasons brought new people and more income, so churches could add some improvements to their facilities and purchase the new equipment.

Ministry style is different in this type of church compared to somewhere like the inner city of Chicago. There are cultures to understand wherever we serve, in both the community and the church. In the lake community the elderly pastor had retired. His ministry style was to visit people in their homes often, to daily stop by the hospital and, if necessary, once a week or more drive for about an hour to a larger city hospital. He was available, steady, calm, and personable, not especially good at preaching, but very approachable. When he retired, a younger, single missionary was selected to be the pastor. He wanted to return to the states because his mother was not well, and this particular church was near her retirement home. His style was evangelistic, aggressive, and (though he worked hard and was well-educated) he was not as personable as the pastor he followed. The pulpit committee thought they needed new families, couples, energy, and so they selected this European missionary. You can already anticipate the difficulty for some members.

Within a year, a considerable percentage of the people wanted him to leave. With a congregation of about one hundred that included twenty or so who wanted a pastoral change within a few months, the conflict issues grew rapidly, from one-on-one conversations, to meetings with a few well-intended parishioners. Then board meetings were called where the board tried to reason with the single pastor. He did not want to change. He felt they knew what they were getting when they asked him to come. False accusations were stirred by phone calls, electronic mails, and secret meetings among members of the congregation. Attendance dropped, the budget became a concern, and denominational officials were brought in.

The conflict had escalated from misinformation to aggressive, unfair, and untrue statements about the pastor. As a result, he assessed the situation, felt his reputation was being ruined, and came to the conclusion that he would resign—and sadly left the denomination. The congregation chose an interim pastor to serve them for two years. His pastoral style is similar to that of the one who retired. The people who left have not returned, and the church that was once peaceful is divided, and a feeling of tension prevails. The conflict moved from normal disagreements to destructive power plays. This story is true, and all too familiar.

What Moves Normal Conflict to Aggressive Destructive Conflict

Parishioners can bring personal baggage and agendas to church.

People can project personal needs onto the church or displace private problems onto the pastor. They make excessive demands, offer petty criticisms, and give pharisaical devotion to the trivial details of congregational culture and tradition. They try to obstruct any sense of the larger church mission beyond the meeting of their own needs and those of their small circle of friends.

Among this group, the most challenging are the ones who are publicly seen as pillars or saints (leaders) of the church. Rather than being held accountable to New Testament standards for Christian behavior, they are granted tacit immunity because of their visible energy, perhaps popularity, and devotion to the church. People assume that these cannot be the problem, for they have been around the ministry for years and have worked hard. Thus, in their minds, the new leader (the pastor) must be the problem; he (or she) is the outsider and does not understand how things are done. Though there might be some truth to their point, the deep-seated issue is that the person wants his or her own needs met.

These critics have a particular set of needs and expectations of their pastor—and no pastor can meet all of them. As a result, it is only a matter of time until disillusionment and a general sense of betrayal sets in and the pastor inevitably proves to be a disappointment.

When elected, the pastor looked almost perfect. His or her resume was great; the references were all complimentary; and the first few months or years were exciting. However, the member with a personal agenda changes his or her opinion of the pastor from savior to destroyer, and criticism becomes personal as the member feels he or she must find a way to get the pastor to resign or be removed. The member is antagonistic.

A common tactic used by these people who feel they are the core of the church is to have secret meetings, at times called prayer meetings; however, little prayer actually happens. These meetings are used to discuss concerns and disagreements concerning the pastor, and typically an agenda or strategy is developed during these times. Email and websites provide a vehicle for them to communicate accusations, concerns, and outright attacks very quickly to a lot of people.

Another level of deep-seated conflict comes when the now-organized complaints are presented to the board of deacons or elders. At times the deacons can say, “The charges that are raised are to remain confidential, in order to protect the pastor and the accusers.” When the pastor is kept out of the loop and does not know the concerns, the people involved, or the details of the problem, then the situation can easily escalate to the point where the deacons are presenting the concerns or charge to the pastor in a meeting, taking him or her off-guard. The pastor is not given the opportunity to think, do research about the concern(s), or determine a way to negotiate with the people. The deacons have already reached a conclusion. The crowd’s opinion of the pastor has turned from appreciation to concern, and the pastor is outnumbered. As a result, the momentum is spiraling downward. Since the board has decided to team up against the pastor, all that is left for them to do is to offer a severance package or worse if the pastor does not leave.

Many pastors decide to hold their ground and fix the problem, deal with the divisive people who want control, and determine that this is what they must do for the good of the church and their own reputation. Though this might not be the right thing to do, the pastor understands that someone needs to wisely deal with these factions in the church. The factions will then likely feel that they have been there longer, understand the community and church better, and so must get this pastor out of the way at any cost. At this point vicious rumor campaigns, false accusations, attacks against the pastor’s character or the integrity of his or her family members, and petty issues become part of the control group’s agenda. They now see the pastor as a destroyer of their church, and many try to further their agendas by spiritualizing the situation to say, “The pastor is not God’s will,” or “We must do what God wants for our church; we need another leader.”

This kind of scenario is all too common. Pastors from all denominations and all sizes of churches have gone through or are going through a similar experience.

A caution needs to be given to pastors, as we need to acknowledge that none of us is perfect; we all make mistakes. We all need to seek to understand the history and culture of both our church and city. We need to endeavor to sincerely and properly communicate and be sensitive to changing things too fast. More common than the problem I have just described is the problem of pastors thinking they have all the answers and do not need to be sensitive to the needs of the church. Pastoring is a difficult but extremely rewarding occupation; conflict is part of what we experience, and properly dealing with difficult people will always be part of our calling.

Understanding that we are not perfect because we make occasional mistakes is not the real issue with what I am describing. Does the normal, hard-working pastor deserve to have his or her reputation smeared, his or her career threatened, and his or her spouse and children forced to leave town under traumatic circumstances? I do not believe this is the answer, and there is no biblical precedent for this other than to “shake the dust off your feet” (Matthew 10:14). People often get the church they deserve, and if they want to maintain control instead of allow God total control, so be it for them.

Church Conflict Can be Related to Economic and Cultural Change

When a major business in town goes under or moves to another community, our people become affected because many of them might work for that business. They might need to change careers, move, or retire early. New businesses can come to the community as well. At that time new people, more business opportunities, more jobs, and new ideas come to the area. When the neighborhood changes culturally, or the socio-economic status improves or goes down, this can produce a sense of grief, anger, or betrayal as an old order passes and a seemingly insecure new world emerges. Since the Christian church generally reflects the surrounding culture far more than it resists or transforms it, it is common for a church to feel distressed and overwhelmed with the situation in the outside community. The answer to this issue is for the congregation to understand that they are to offer hope to that community, not be “conformed to the world.” They are a lighthouse to the city and must discover new ways to serve people.

Congregations Can Resemble Clubs Rather than Churches

M. Scott Peck (1987) explains that many congregations have become clubs that are sociological fellowships rather than theological communities. The club is closed, exclusive, and inward looking. It can be friendly, have the best functions, potlucks, outings, and programs, but the congregation as a club is essentially concerned with the past and present relationship circles.

A church that functions as a theological community is designed or focused on being inclusive, inviting, evangelistic, challenging, and concerned about the transformation of people as growing Christians. Its goal is to fulfill the Great Commission while demonstrating the Great Commandment. They feel that the newest visitor is as important as the oldest member.

In club-like congregations, many view the Great Commission strategy that includes change and growth not as an opportunity but as a threat. Their primary concern is with fellowship, relationships, and making people feel good. Fellowship, relationships, and assisting people in feeling accepted is what all churches need to do; however, with club churches, different people, programs, and new leaders threaten the club’s primary reason for being.

Strategy

Whatever the causes of conflict, we need to determine a strategy to deal with our situation.

1. It is important to understand your situation.

Are you dealing with a dysfunctional congregation with a hidden history of conflict? Have prior pastors been wounded or forced out? (Usually this is communicated in skillfully concealed verbiage as an explanation to a pastor before he or she begins.) Have the denominational or church leaders been forthright with you concerning past issues in this particular church? Many times potential pastors or new pastors are simply not given much information, largely because the leadership is afraid that they will not come or will not stay. Sometimes the reasons why the previous pastor left are that he or she was a problem but no real details are given.

2. If tactics are being used against you that violate church bylaws and are deceitful, then you are in another level of conflict.

Secret meetings, slander, and confidential charges (the accuser’s charges are secret) are not the Bible’s way of addressing problems with leadership. At this stage, you might feel that the people are for you one week, but some suddenly dislike or even hate you the next. Before you come to the conclusion that you are in a church like this, you must carefully examine your behaviors and motives. Are you subconsciously making this deeper than it is? Are you experiencing burnout or depression? Do you just want a change? We must understand that no one is immune to sin or self-delusion, including ourselves. However, in many cases, there is more evil present in local churches than that church or denomination would want to admit.

M. Scott Peck (1987) analyzes club churches as being, by their very nature, particularly susceptible to a sudden metamorphosis into evil. The pastor of that church needs to understand that he or she is engaged in a spiritual struggle. The pastor must grow in emotional, physical, and spiritual strength, as he or she will be involved in a battle that will demand all of his or her resources. In Paul’s thirteen books, he addresses issues and people that will teach you a strategy to deal with these kinds of people. The New Testament gives you great insight into the spiritual warfare that is part of being a leader in God’s kingdom. The principles that worked when the Scripture was written will work today in our complicated world.

3. Determine if you need an outside consultant.

Typically, it is better to hire a conflict manager from outside the network of denominational structures, such as someone with experience in mediating in business situations. The church conflict management industry has grown in recent years, but consultants who are hired by the district or church board are tempted to serve the interests of (and report to) the organization that hired them. In that case, it might make better sense to both the denomination and the consultant to get rid of the pastor, rather than displease the faction of vocal critics.

4. Determine if you need legal advice.

We always need to be cautious about determining whether we need to hire an attorney. However, even in churches there can be unfair labor practices, and we need to be protected from wrongful dismissal, have clear and functional job descriptions, the right to face our accusers, and the right to due process when accused of poor performance. The church should adhere to standards of evidence, truth, and fair play when the pastor’s career is on the line. More and more clergy are turning to the courts for protection.

5. Understand that personal growth often comes through our difficulty.

We can choose to grow spiritually and emotionally in times of difficulty. Our problem can be our gift to grow deeper in the Lord and to become more dynamic leaders for His kingdom. Eternal lessons can be learned about spiritual warfare, loving our enemies, serving people, and loving them with the love of Jesus Christ. Though this growth challenge is thrown on us in the form of a conflict or crisis, we can choose to be people of prayer, seek the wisdom that God offers, and look at people through the eyes of Christ.

For a church to become healthy, the escalating conflict will need to be stopped. It is a myth to say that pastors serve at the pleasure of a majority of the membership. Unless the abusive factions are biblically addressed, restrained, or removed from the body, a committed and vociferous minority has the power in any congregation to end a pastor’s tenure. If they experience success once, they will be all the more powerful the next time and again and again. The pain that a pastoral family can go through is often what causes him or her to decide to end his or her career and calling and enter secular work.

**Q:** How does a church member’s personal baggage add to the destructive nature of some conflicts?

**A:** People can transfer their unresolved emotions from previous or private issues to the conflict at hand. Some people can begin to view themselves as more essential than others or deserving of differing levels of respect and authority. Some individuals in a conflict are in similar conflicts in other relationships.

**Q:** How might conflict be related to economic or cultural change?

**A:** When the circumstances surrounding a church change, but the people do not adapt, conflict will ensue. A church whose neighborhood changes must also change or lose its effectiveness. Often a church will lose vision for its community if that culture has changed, while the people of the church have not changed.

**Q:** When does a congregation begin to resemble a club more than a church?

**A:** A club is a closed, exclusive and inward focused group. The Great Commission becomes threatening to those who are of more of a club mentality. A church that becomes more of a club begins to lose track of its reason for being.

**Q:** Identify and explain the first step to be taken when addressing destructive conflict.

**A:** One must understand the situation. This requires more than just accepting one’s personal view. The leader must identify with the group and with the problem to be addressed. The leader must seek to understand the history and the nature of the conflict.

**Q:** When is an outside consultant needed?

**A:** When the conflict has moved to a personal nature and the sides are unwilling to pursue any level of compromise, a consultant is needed. When the parties cannot agree on the issue, but share a mutual relationship with a trusted third party, that individual can aid as a consultant.

**Q:** Is spiritual growth possible in times of such deep conflict? If so, how?

**A:** Growth is a choice. One can choose to be selfish or genuinely seek to gain God’s purposes even in difficult circumstances. Lessons concerning spiritual warfare, love for enemies, and the nature of servanthood are available in times of conflict. 7 CHAPTER

#### 6.3.2 — List the steps of strategy a leader can take to address aggressive and destructive conflict.

The bedroom community in the resort town enjoyed seasonal growth as the summers came and the winter snow fell. Lakes, summer cabins, and winter ski resorts were the draw during these two seasons. People from larger cities visited the area because of the entertainment and recreation; however, the people who lived in the town year around were the real decision makers and the ones who held the businesses together. Along with the growth in the businesses came growth in the small churches in the area. A visitor typically attended the church of the denomination that he or she was familiar with. The seasons brought new people and more income, so churches could add some improvements to their facilities and purchase the new equipment.

Ministry style is different in this type of church compared to somewhere like the inner city of Chicago. There are cultures to understand wherever we serve, in both the community and the church. In the lake community the elderly pastor had retired. His ministry style was to visit people in their homes often, to daily stop by the hospital and, if necessary, once a week or more drive for about an hour to a larger city hospital. He was available, steady, calm, and personable, not especially good at preaching, but very approachable. When he retired, a younger, single missionary was selected to be the pastor. He wanted to return to the states because his mother was not well, and this particular church was near her retirement home. His style was evangelistic, aggressive, and (though he worked hard and was well-educated) he was not as personable as the pastor he followed. The pulpit committee thought they needed new families, couples, energy, and so they selected this European missionary. You can already anticipate the difficulty for some members.

Within a year, a considerable percentage of the people wanted him to leave. With a congregation of about one hundred that included twenty or so who wanted a pastoral change within a few months, the conflict issues grew rapidly, from one-on-one conversations, to meetings with a few well-intended parishioners. Then board meetings were called where the board tried to reason with the single pastor. He did not want to change. He felt they knew what they were getting when they asked him to come. False accusations were stirred by phone calls, electronic mails, and secret meetings among members of the congregation. Attendance dropped, the budget became a concern, and denominational officials were brought in.

The conflict had escalated from misinformation to aggressive, unfair, and untrue statements about the pastor. As a result, he assessed the situation, felt his reputation was being ruined, and came to the conclusion that he would resign—and sadly left the denomination. The congregation chose an interim pastor to serve them for two years. His pastoral style is similar to that of the one who retired. The people who left have not returned, and the church that was once peaceful is divided, and a feeling of tension prevails. The conflict moved from normal disagreements to destructive power plays. This story is true, and all too familiar.

What Moves Normal Conflict to Aggressive Destructive Conflict

Parishioners can bring personal baggage and agendas to church.

People can project personal needs onto the church or displace private problems onto the pastor. They make excessive demands, offer petty criticisms, and give pharisaical devotion to the trivial details of congregational culture and tradition. They try to obstruct any sense of the larger church mission beyond the meeting of their own needs and those of their small circle of friends.

Among this group, the most challenging are the ones who are publicly seen as pillars or saints (leaders) of the church. Rather than being held accountable to New Testament standards for Christian behavior, they are granted tacit immunity because of their visible energy, perhaps popularity, and devotion to the church. People assume that these cannot be the problem, for they have been around the ministry for years and have worked hard. Thus, in their minds, the new leader (the pastor) must be the problem; he (or she) is the outsider and does not understand how things are done. Though there might be some truth to their point, the deep-seated issue is that the person wants his or her own needs met.

These critics have a particular set of needs and expectations of their pastor—and no pastor can meet all of them. As a result, it is only a matter of time until disillusionment and a general sense of betrayal sets in and the pastor inevitably proves to be a disappointment.

When elected, the pastor looked almost perfect. His or her resume was great; the references were all complimentary; and the first few months or years were exciting. However, the member with a personal agenda changes his or her opinion of the pastor from savior to destroyer, and criticism becomes personal as the member feels he or she must find a way to get the pastor to resign or be removed. The member is antagonistic.

A common tactic used by these people who feel they are the core of the church is to have secret meetings, at times called prayer meetings; however, little prayer actually happens. These meetings are used to discuss concerns and disagreements concerning the pastor, and typically an agenda or strategy is developed during these times. Email and websites provide a vehicle for them to communicate accusations, concerns, and outright attacks very quickly to a lot of people.

Another level of deep-seated conflict comes when the now-organized complaints are presented to the board of deacons or elders. At times the deacons can say, “The charges that are raised are to remain confidential, in order to protect the pastor and the accusers.” When the pastor is kept out of the loop and does not know the concerns, the people involved, or the details of the problem, then the situation can easily escalate to the point where the deacons are presenting the concerns or charge to the pastor in a meeting, taking him or her off-guard. The pastor is not given the opportunity to think, do research about the concern(s), or determine a way to negotiate with the people. The deacons have already reached a conclusion. The crowd’s opinion of the pastor has turned from appreciation to concern, and the pastor is outnumbered. As a result, the momentum is spiraling downward. Since the board has decided to team up against the pastor, all that is left for them to do is to offer a severance package or worse if the pastor does not leave.

Many pastors decide to hold their ground and fix the problem, deal with the divisive people who want control, and determine that this is what they must do for the good of the church and their own reputation. Though this might not be the right thing to do, the pastor understands that someone needs to wisely deal with these factions in the church. The factions will then likely feel that they have been there longer, understand the community and church better, and so must get this pastor out of the way at any cost. At this point vicious rumor campaigns, false accusations, attacks against the pastor’s character or the integrity of his or her family members, and petty issues become part of the control group’s agenda. They now see the pastor as a destroyer of their church, and many try to further their agendas by spiritualizing the situation to say, “The pastor is not God’s will,” or “We must do what God wants for our church; we need another leader.”

This kind of scenario is all too common. Pastors from all denominations and all sizes of churches have gone through or are going through a similar experience.

A caution needs to be given to pastors, as we need to acknowledge that none of us is perfect; we all make mistakes. We all need to seek to understand the history and culture of both our church and city. We need to endeavor to sincerely and properly communicate and be sensitive to changing things too fast. More common than the problem I have just described is the problem of pastors thinking they have all the answers and do not need to be sensitive to the needs of the church. Pastoring is a difficult but extremely rewarding occupation; conflict is part of what we experience, and properly dealing with difficult people will always be part of our calling.

Understanding that we are not perfect because we make occasional mistakes is not the real issue with what I am describing. Does the normal, hard-working pastor deserve to have his or her reputation smeared, his or her career threatened, and his or her spouse and children forced to leave town under traumatic circumstances? I do not believe this is the answer, and there is no biblical precedent for this other than to “shake the dust off your feet” (Matthew 10:14). People often get the church they deserve, and if they want to maintain control instead of allow God total control, so be it for them.

Church Conflict Can be Related to Economic and Cultural Change

When a major business in town goes under or moves to another community, our people become affected because many of them might work for that business. They might need to change careers, move, or retire early. New businesses can come to the community as well. At that time new people, more business opportunities, more jobs, and new ideas come to the area. When the neighborhood changes culturally, or the socio-economic status improves or goes down, this can produce a sense of grief, anger, or betrayal as an old order passes and a seemingly insecure new world emerges. Since the Christian church generally reflects the surrounding culture far more than it resists or transforms it, it is common for a church to feel distressed and overwhelmed with the situation in the outside community. The answer to this issue is for the congregation to understand that they are to offer hope to that community, not be “conformed to the world.” They are a lighthouse to the city and must discover new ways to serve people.

Congregations Can Resemble Clubs Rather than Churches

M. Scott Peck (1987) explains that many congregations have become clubs that are sociological fellowships rather than theological communities. The club is closed, exclusive, and inward looking. It can be friendly, have the best functions, potlucks, outings, and programs, but the congregation as a club is essentially concerned with the past and present relationship circles.

A church that functions as a theological community is designed or focused on being inclusive, inviting, evangelistic, challenging, and concerned about the transformation of people as growing Christians. Its goal is to fulfill the Great Commission while demonstrating the Great Commandment. They feel that the newest visitor is as important as the oldest member.

In club-like congregations, many view the Great Commission strategy that includes change and growth not as an opportunity but as a threat. Their primary concern is with fellowship, relationships, and making people feel good. Fellowship, relationships, and assisting people in feeling accepted is what all churches need to do; however, with club churches, different people, programs, and new leaders threaten the club’s primary reason for being.

Strategy

Whatever the causes of conflict, we need to determine a strategy to deal with our situation.

1. It is important to understand your situation.

Are you dealing with a dysfunctional congregation with a hidden history of conflict? Have prior pastors been wounded or forced out? (Usually this is communicated in skillfully concealed verbiage as an explanation to a pastor before he or she begins.) Have the denominational or church leaders been forthright with you concerning past issues in this particular church? Many times potential pastors or new pastors are simply not given much information, largely because the leadership is afraid that they will not come or will not stay. Sometimes the reasons why the previous pastor left are that he or she was a problem but no real details are given.

2. If tactics are being used against you that violate church bylaws and are deceitful, then you are in another level of conflict.

Secret meetings, slander, and confidential charges (the accuser’s charges are secret) are not the Bible’s way of addressing problems with leadership. At this stage, you might feel that the people are for you one week, but some suddenly dislike or even hate you the next. Before you come to the conclusion that you are in a church like this, you must carefully examine your behaviors and motives. Are you subconsciously making this deeper than it is? Are you experiencing burnout or depression? Do you just want a change? We must understand that no one is immune to sin or self-delusion, including ourselves. However, in many cases, there is more evil present in local churches than that church or denomination would want to admit.

M. Scott Peck (1987) analyzes club churches as being, by their very nature, particularly susceptible to a sudden metamorphosis into evil. The pastor of that church needs to understand that he or she is engaged in a spiritual struggle. The pastor must grow in emotional, physical, and spiritual strength, as he or she will be involved in a battle that will demand all of his or her resources. In Paul’s thirteen books, he addresses issues and people that will teach you a strategy to deal with these kinds of people. The New Testament gives you great insight into the spiritual warfare that is part of being a leader in God’s kingdom. The principles that worked when the Scripture was written will work today in our complicated world.

3. Determine if you need an outside consultant.

Typically, it is better to hire a conflict manager from outside the network of denominational structures, such as someone with experience in mediating in business situations. The church conflict management industry has grown in recent years, but consultants who are hired by the district or church board are tempted to serve the interests of (and report to) the organization that hired them. In that case, it might make better sense to both the denomination and the consultant to get rid of the pastor, rather than displease the faction of vocal critics.

4. Determine if you need legal advice.

We always need to be cautious about determining whether we need to hire an attorney. However, even in churches there can be unfair labor practices, and we need to be protected from wrongful dismissal, have clear and functional job descriptions, the right to face our accusers, and the right to due process when accused of poor performance. The church should adhere to standards of evidence, truth, and fair play when the pastor’s career is on the line. More and more clergy are turning to the courts for protection.

5. Understand that personal growth often comes through our difficulty.

We can choose to grow spiritually and emotionally in times of difficulty. Our problem can be our gift to grow deeper in the Lord and to become more dynamic leaders for His kingdom. Eternal lessons can be learned about spiritual warfare, loving our enemies, serving people, and loving them with the love of Jesus Christ. Though this growth challenge is thrown on us in the form of a conflict or crisis, we can choose to be people of prayer, seek the wisdom that God offers, and look at people through the eyes of Christ.

For a church to become healthy, the escalating conflict will need to be stopped. It is a myth to say that pastors serve at the pleasure of a majority of the membership. Unless the abusive factions are biblically addressed, restrained, or removed from the body, a committed and vociferous minority has the power in any congregation to end a pastor’s tenure. If they experience success once, they will be all the more powerful the next time and again and again. The pain that a pastoral family can go through is often what causes him or her to decide to end his or her career and calling and enter secular work.

**Q:** How does a church member’s personal baggage add to the destructive nature of some conflicts?

**A:** People can transfer their unresolved emotions from previous or private issues to the conflict at hand. Some people can begin to view themselves as more essential than others or deserving of differing levels of respect and authority. Some individuals in a conflict are in similar conflicts in other relationships.

**Q:** How might conflict be related to economic or cultural change?

**A:** When the circumstances surrounding a church change, but the people do not adapt, conflict will ensue. A church whose neighborhood changes must also change or lose its effectiveness. Often a church will lose vision for its community if that culture has changed, while the people of the church have not changed.

**Q:** When does a congregation begin to resemble a club more than a church?

**A:** A club is a closed, exclusive and inward focused group. The Great Commission becomes threatening to those who are of more of a club mentality. A church that becomes more of a club begins to lose track of its reason for being.

**Q:** Identify and explain the first step to be taken when addressing destructive conflict.

**A:** One must understand the situation. This requires more than just accepting one’s personal view. The leader must identify with the group and with the problem to be addressed. The leader must seek to understand the history and the nature of the conflict.

**Q:** When is an outside consultant needed?

**A:** When the conflict has moved to a personal nature and the sides are unwilling to pursue any level of compromise, a consultant is needed. When the parties cannot agree on the issue, but share a mutual relationship with a trusted third party, that individual can aid as a consultant.

**Q:** Is spiritual growth possible in times of such deep conflict? If so, how?

**A:** Growth is a choice. One can choose to be selfish or genuinely seek to gain God’s purposes even in difficult circumstances. Lessons concerning spiritual warfare, love for enemies, and the nature of servanthood are available in times of conflict. 7 CHAPTER

## 7 - The Destruction that Conflict Can Bring

### Knowing the Difference between Constructive and Destructive Conflict

#### 7.1.1 — List and explain the key differences between destructive and constructive conflict.

“Listen to advice and accept instruction, and in the end you will be wise” (Proverbs 19:20).

As leaders, we must remember that growing people desperately want relationships, recognition, and some responsibility. We desire relationships because we were created to have fellowship, and we are part of a body . Everyone hungers for deep, healthy relationships. We want recognition, because each one of us is important and has a unique part in the Christian community. We would like to be given responsibility because everyone has gifts, both natural and spiritual (see 1 Corinthians 12:1–4), and we want to develop and use them. However, many pastors have fears of receiving opinions or constructive criticism, or of being transparent with their congregations. As a result, they try to control, run a one-person show, and assume an image of not having any faults. This is unhealthy and will only produce unhealthy leaders within the congregation.

“The way of a fool seems right to him, but a wise man listens to advice” (Proverbs 12:15).

Modeling godly leadership involves finding our own security in God. We trust the Lord for strength and rest in the fact that He has called us to lead our churches (see Acts 20:28). The knowledge and understanding of His love for us and His call on our lives will enable us to listen to others’ opinions, respond tactfully, and know the difference between destructive and constructive conflict.

Conflict can be:

Destructive : People do not understand that it is normal to have conflict when there are two different opinions, or any kind of change.

Constructive : People understand the need to hear both opinions and allow necessary change to occur.

Destructive : People only want their own way. They feel that any other variation of the decision is wrong.

Constructive : People want to discover a win-win-win solution; God wins and both sides win. There is a team attitude, and the goal is to find an agreeable solution in accordance with God’s will.

Destructive : People are defensive and aggressive and label other people or categorize them as part of the other group.

Constructive : People are open and feel that others are entitled to their opinions. The assumption is that people will have different opinions, and a reasonable compromise can be reached.

Destructive : People will not budge from their viewpoint and are unwilling to consider the value of a new idea or a different perspective.

Constructive : People believe that with honest, healthy communication a solid agreement will eventually come to the group. The ultimate opinion of everyone will be better than any one opinion.

Destructive : People decide to personally attack others who have different opinions instead of focusing on the issue at hand.

Constructive : Any disagreements are a result of varying opinions on a particular issue, not on anyone’s personality.

Destructive : People put value in their ideas over healthy relationships within the body.

Constructive : Healthy relationships are valued more than the need to win or be right.

Pastors are often required to come to the delicate conclusion that someone or some group within the church is either being destructive, constructive, or somewhere in between. The pastor’s role is to try to get close enough to people so they trust him or her with their opinions, fears, and even motives. If this happens, the pastor can try to understand the various personalities, gifts, and history of the people involved.

The never-ending challenge of the pastor is in getting behind the conflict and seeing the people. What are their real motives? Why are they reacting this way? Why is their opinion so inflexible? Why do they threaten or use the kind of words they use? All of these questions are important as we make a decision to lead in a particular situation.

Pastors must be able to separate themselves from the conflict and not see themselves as fighters, but as mediators, as advocates for everyone—even those who have chosen to label the pastor as part of the other side. The pastor’s goal is to understand both sides and discover the truth from whatever is being said.

This can only occur when the pastor trusts the Lord and totally surrenders his or her life, occupation, and ministry to Jesus Christ. The pastor will then understand that the Lord wants to defend him or her and nothing will happen that the Lord will not control. God will offer wisdom with every decision and understanding and discernment with every person. The risk of losing the job, reputation, or security is entrusted to God’s hands.

If a pastor is unable to have this kind of attitude and the power that comes from trusting the Lord, then he or she will be constantly on the defensive and will lead the church from fear, not faith.

People often think (or pretend to think) they are seeking truth, when they are only running from their fears. How do we respond to those who are critical of the way we lead? Every pastor will experience conflict in the ministry. This normal part of what we do is sometimes caused by personality differences and by people who have been leaders but no longer are. At times conflict can be our fault because we have not thought through a decision long enough, have moved too fast, or have become closed to other people’s opinions. The conflict could be spiritually based, where the enemy is trying to divide or oppose the work of God.

“Those who oppose him he must gently instruct, in the hope that God will grant them repentance leading them to a knowledge of the truth, and that they will come to their senses and escape from the trap of the devil, who has taken them captive to do his will” (2 Timothy 2:25–26).

If you are the object of criticism or conflict, it is helpful to remind yourself of these six facts:

1. You are called to serve the body of Christ, not to be a fighter, but you cannot encourage conflict by ignoring it or hiding from it.

2. As a leader, your motives will sometimes be misunderstood. Use people’s questions as tools to help you be sensitive to their needs and their pain.

3. Most church problems are not theological; most have to do with people’s opinions. All pastors have this challenge. It is helpful to meet with other pastors and be transparent and open about your situation, as you will find they are going through the same type of difficulty.

4. Do not run from your critics or from the conflict. It is normal to run, hide, get alone or ignore people when we are facing this challenge. Try not to run and try not to fight; find a middle ground. Politely talk to the people about concerns. Listen to, greet them, and see them as people who you need to understand and minister to.

5. Pastors are to have a servant’s heart (see Mark 10:35–45). When afraid, we are tempted to boss and control people. Remember the Lord as He gave us our example of how to be leaders. We are to give, forgive, help, love, and humbly stand for what is biblically right.

6. The person or people who are against you are not as confident as you might think. You can know that beneath all of their anger, criticism, and gossip, there is fear that they will lose power or fail in their attempt to win.

Gossip

Gossip is a tactic that is most often used by those who are reacting wrongly in the conflict situation. It is easier to talk about and label people than to try to understand or sincerely evaluate why they might be feeling the way they do. Gossip has always been a problem in the church. Thus, the Scripture speaks of this behavior often and admonishes the pastor to address it whenever he or she knows it is happening. Gossip is never helpful; it is always full of misinformation, opinions rather than facts, dangerous emotions, and outright lies. Honest, healthy communication is helpful because it is based on truth and transparency. With the technological advancements of email, cell phones, and the web, there is also the ability to communicate ugly gossip to masses of people with great speed and little effort. Not only do we need to listen to what people are saying with their mouths, but what they are sending through emails.

How to Tell the Difference between Honest Criticism and Gossip

Constructive : Wants to improve, encourage, help, and edify

Destructive : Wants to escalate the problem and spread its effects

Constructive : Wants to unite toward a common goal

Destructive : Wants to divide and slow or stop progress

Constructive : Communicated in love, directly to the person who needs to change or understand, while trying to be open and gain understanding yourself

Destructive : Uses end runs or behind-your-back communication tactics with no plans to cooperate or gain understanding

Constructive : Is open and objective

Destructive : Is opinionated, closed, and subjective

Constructive : The motivation is improving the health of the church and growth of the body.

Destructive : The motivation is self-serving and will win at all costs. It can be malicious.

Constructive : Without different opinions, healthy criticism and conflict, a body will become passive, stagnant, and insensitive to the needs of the people or the community.

Destructive : Gossip, slander, and malicious behavior will destroy unity in the body and a healthy church.

“I am afraid that when I come I may not find you as I want you to be, and you may not find me as you want me to be. I fear that there may be quarreling, jealousy, outbursts of anger, factions, slander, gossip, arrogance, and disorder” (2 Corinthians 12:20).

All Christian leaders know that gossip, factions, slander, and pride can greatly injure a church. However, all Christian leaders should know that these behaviors are common in churches. The pastor must find a way to address this kind of behavior while demonstrating the servant leadership of the Lord. It is helpful to understand that certain questions can stop the ongoing behavior of gossip. When talking to the person involved, pastors or leaders can ask:

• How will this information be helpful to me, the church, or the person you are speaking of?

• Who gave you this information or how did you obtain it—specifically?

“A gossip betrays a confidence” (Proverbs 20:19).

• Please give me the names of people whom you have talked to, or who have talked to you.

“A gossip separates close friends” (Proverbs 16:28).

• Have you personally talked to the person you are speaking to me about?

• Have you checked out the information, verifying that it is true?

“Without gossip a quarrel dies down” (Proverbs 26:20).

• Can I quote you when I verify the information you are giving me?

As a pastor, you can teach your congregation to avoid gossip and utilize these tools as they seek to be part of a healthy body of believers.

**Q:** List at least three ways that destructive conflict differs from constructive conflict.

**A:** a) New vision, sense of resolution, unresolved tension, promotion b) New vision, unresolved tension, promotion, sense of resolution c) Unresolved tension, promotion, sense of resolution, new vision d) New vision, sense of resolution, promotion, unresolved tension

**Q:** How can trust in a pastor help alleviate conflict?

**A:** a) Watch your back b) Seek more education c) Keep a clear perspective d) Avoid persecution

**Q:** What is gossip?

**A:** a) Read continually b) Spend extra time on sermon preparation c) Think long and often about the conflict d) None of the above

**Q:** How does honest criticism differ from gossip?

**A:** a) Counting to ten helps us to be patient and gain emotional control, but sometimes counting to one hundred can be necessary. b) Conflict that is not resolved can escalate from a 1- to a 10- to a 100- level. c) The rule identifies the decrease in priority a conflict can become. d) The rule identifies levels of conflict based on the number of people involved.

**Q:** What advice does the book of Proverbs provide concerning gossip?

**A:** a) The church split. b) Peter began preaching to Gentiles. c) Deacons were chosen. d) All of the above

#### 7.1.2 — List and explain the key differences between criticism and gossip.

The pastor will then understand that the Lord wants to defend him or her and nothing will happen that the Lord will not control. God will offer wisdom with every decision and understanding and discernment with every person. The risk of losing the job, reputation, or security is entrusted to God’s hands.

If a pastor is unable to have this kind of attitude and the power that comes from trusting the Lord, then he or she will be constantly on the defensive and will lead the church from fear, not faith.

People often think (or pretend to think) they are seeking truth, when they are only running from their fears. How do we respond to those who are critical of the way we lead? Every pastor will experience conflict in the ministry. This normal part of what we do is sometimes caused by personality differences and by people who have been leaders but no longer are. At times conflict can be our fault because we have not thought through a decision long enough, have moved too fast, or have become closed to other people’s opinions. The conflict could be spiritually based, where the enemy is trying to divide or oppose the work of God.

“Those who oppose him he must gently instruct, in the hope that God will grant them repentance leading them to a knowledge of the truth, and that they will come to their senses and escape from the trap of the devil, who has taken them captive to do his will” (2 Timothy 2:25–26).

If you are the object of criticism or conflict, it is helpful to remind yourself of these six facts:

1. You are called to serve the body of Christ, not to be a fighter, but you cannot encourage conflict by ignoring it or hiding from it.

2. As a leader, your motives will sometimes be misunderstood. Use people’s questions as tools to help you be sensitive to their needs and their pain.

3. Most church problems are not theological; most have to do with people’s opinions. All pastors have this challenge. It is helpful to meet with other pastors and be transparent and open about your situation, as you will find they are going through the same type of difficulty.

4. Do not run from your critics or from the conflict. It is normal to run, hide, get alone or ignore people when we are facing this challenge. Try not to run and try not to fight; find a middle ground. Politely talk to the people about concerns. Listen to, greet them, and see them as people who you need to understand and minister to.

5. Pastors are to have a servant’s heart (see Mark 10:35–45). When afraid, we are tempted to boss and control people. Remember the Lord as He gave us our example of how to be leaders. We are to give, forgive, help, love, and humbly stand for what is biblically right.

6. The person or people who are against you are not as confident as you might think. You can know that beneath all of their anger, criticism, and gossip, there is fear that they will lose power or fail in their attempt to win.

Gossip

Gossip is a tactic that is most often used by those who are reacting wrongly in the conflict situation. It is easier to talk about and label people than to try to understand or sincerely evaluate why they might be feeling the way they do. Gossip has always been a problem in the church. Thus, the Scripture speaks of this behavior often and admonishes the pastor to address it whenever he or she knows it is happening. Gossip is never helpful; it is always full of misinformation, opinions rather than facts, dangerous emotions, and outright lies. Honest, healthy communication is helpful because it is based on truth and transparency. With the technological advancements of email, cell phones, and the web, there is also the ability to communicate ugly gossip to masses of people with great speed and little effort. Not only do we need to listen to what people are saying with their mouths, but what they are sending through emails.

How to Tell the Difference between Honest Criticism and Gossip

Constructive : Wants to improve, encourage, help, and edify

Destructive : Wants to escalate the problem and spread its effects

Constructive : Wants to unite toward a common goal

Destructive : Wants to divide and slow or stop progress

Constructive : Communicated in love, directly to the person who needs to change or understand, while trying to be open and gain understanding yourself

Destructive : Uses end runs or behind-your-back communication tactics with no plans to cooperate or gain understanding

Constructive : Is open and objective

Destructive : Is opinionated, closed, and subjective

Constructive : The motivation is improving the health of the church and growth of the body.

Destructive : The motivation is self-serving and will win at all costs. It can be malicious.

Constructive : Without different opinions, healthy criticism and conflict, a body will become passive, stagnant, and insensitive to the needs of the people or the community.

Destructive : Gossip, slander, and malicious behavior will destroy unity in the body and a healthy church.

“I am afraid that when I come I may not find you as I want you to be, and you may not find me as you want me to be. I fear that there may be quarreling, jealousy, outbursts of anger, factions, slander, gossip, arrogance, and disorder” (2 Corinthians 12:20).

All Christian leaders know that gossip, factions, slander, and pride can greatly injure a church. However, all Christian leaders should know that these behaviors are common in churches. The pastor must find a way to address this kind of behavior while demonstrating the servant leadership of the Lord. It is helpful to understand that certain questions can stop the ongoing behavior of gossip. When talking to the person involved, pastors or leaders can ask:

• How will this information be helpful to me, the church, or the person you are speaking of?

• Who gave you this information or how did you obtain it—specifically?

“A gossip betrays a confidence” (Proverbs 20:19).

• Please give me the names of people whom you have talked to, or who have talked to you.

“A gossip separates close friends” (Proverbs 16:28).

• Have you personally talked to the person you are speaking to me about?

• Have you checked out the information, verifying that it is true?

“Without gossip a quarrel dies down” (Proverbs 26:20).

• Can I quote you when I verify the information you are giving me?

As a pastor, you can teach your congregation to avoid gossip and utilize these tools as they seek to be part of a healthy body of believers.

**Q:** List at least three ways that destructive conflict differs from constructive conflict.

**A:** a) New vision, sense of resolution, unresolved tension, promotion b) New vision, unresolved tension, promotion, sense of resolution c) Unresolved tension, promotion, sense of resolution, new vision d) New vision, sense of resolution, promotion, unresolved tension

**Q:** How can trust in a pastor help alleviate conflict?

**A:** a) Watch your back b) Seek more education c) Keep a clear perspective d) Avoid persecution

**Q:** What is gossip?

**A:** a) Read continually b) Spend extra time on sermon preparation c) Think long and often about the conflict d) None of the above

**Q:** How does honest criticism differ from gossip?

**A:** a) Counting to ten helps us to be patient and gain emotional control, but sometimes counting to one hundred can be necessary. b) Conflict that is not resolved can escalate from a 1- to a 10- to a 100- level. c) The rule identifies the decrease in priority a conflict can become. d) The rule identifies levels of conflict based on the number of people involved.

**Q:** What advice does the book of Proverbs provide concerning gossip?

**A:** a) The church split. b) Peter began preaching to Gentiles. c) Deacons were chosen. d) All of the above

### The Danger of Division

#### 7.2.1 — Describe and illustrate how division is ultimately destructive to a church.

“I appeal to you, brothers, in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that all of you agree with one another so that there may be no divisions among you and that you may be perfectly united in mind and thought” (1 Corinthians 1:10).

Over the years I have observed that God will always accomplish His purposes and fulfill His will—in His time. Christians can choose to behave like Him in loving ways, or they can behave in carnal ways. Carnality is directed by the flesh and manifests itself through strife, contention, and division. As long as there are people, there will be carnality.

If one church cannot be used, God will use another. If one church has been blessed and the congregation permits carnality (the world) to have influence to a point where it grieves the Holy Spirit, then God will bless another church and use it. Again, the answer to this issue is with leadership, the pastor, or, in the Corinthian’s case, the apostle Paul. The leader points out the issue or behavior that is not pleasing to God and does whatever is necessary to stop it, so the Lord will be honored in that congregation. That may carry a great cost, but the cost of doing nothing is too great; it could mean the loss of God’s favor.

Divisions Destroy Churches

Church division is one of the ways the enemy attacks and stops the progress of a church. The devil does not want a church to reach new people for Christ, and he does not want Christians to grow. He does not want a church to have favor in the community and will do everything he can to distract, delay, and destroy a church’s influence. It does not make any difference to him what denomination the church is or who the pastor is. His goal is the same: paralyze any advancement of God’s kingdom. Divisions accomplish much for Satan’s kingdom.

1. Church division will paralyze the fellowship of the church in its witness to the community.

Paul’s concern for the Corinthians was that their behavior was stopping any progress for God’s kingdom in that community. His words, “I appeal to you, brothers” came to them in both a begging and a commanding tone. It appears that he was willing to do whatever it took (beg, plead, and command), throughout the letter. Why? He was willing for the sake of the Christian witness.

Unity in a church is a precious thing. The psalmist wrote; “How good and pleasant it is when brothers live together in unity! . . . For there the Lord bestows his blessing” (Psalm 133:1, 3).

Paul wrote, “Make every effort to keep the unity of the Spirit through the bond of peace” (Ephesians 4:3). Unity is wonderful, and there is little that is more destructive to a congregation than division among the body.

2. Church division will distract from the ministry of the Word of God—the Bible.

When people are concerned or wonder why many in the church are not in general agreement, they are distracted from the solid ministry of the Scripture. Churches are to agree on the

• Ministry of Scripture

• Inerrancy of the Bible

• Deity of Christ

• Trinity

• Holy Spirit’s activity in the baptism in the Holy Spirit

• Leadership structure that God put in place for churches

We must teach, through direct instruction, the principles that are given in Scripture because they are the basics. There are, also, more doctrines that we teach, including the sixteen fundamental truths that our particular Fellowship has observed.

The Corinthian church was arguing because of differences in opinion about how things should be done. It appears that they thought they could have as many messages in tongues as they wished in a service, but Paul gave them instruction that curtailed such liberties (1 Corinthians 14). They argued about which of the teachers, pastors, or apostles was the greatest, and they contrasted the preachers from one another (1 Corinthians 1:12–17). There was a man sleeping with his stepmother, and the church let it go on (1 Corinthians 5:1–5). There was also sexual immorality (1 Corinthians 6:12) and members suing one another (1 Corinthians 6:1–6). The Corinthian church had become dysfunctional because it was full of division. At some point, the leadership discontinued addressing wrong (sinful) behavior and un-Christian communication. Paul had given his heart to the churches he began and trained. He loved the kingdom of God and knew that the enemy wanted to prevent all churches from being effective.

3. Church division disturbs the unity of the Spirit.

“Agree with one another so that there may be no divisions among you and that you may be perfectly united in mind and thought” (1 Corinthians 1:10). Paul was sad to hear that the precious unity had been taken from this church. The word for division is schismata which means “to tear.” To tear apart, destroy that which was one, and pull apart that which was together—are all ways to think about church division. When Paul instructed this church to agree with each other, he was not talking about compromise or not dealing with dangerous behavior or conversations. “Agree with” or be “perfectly joined” is a term used in mending nets (see Matthew 4:21; Mark 1:19). The purpose of mending a fishing net is to fix the net so it can do its job: catch fish! The purpose of agreement in a church body is to be in a spiritual place so that it can catch fish (people) for God’s kingdom. When division is in the body, there is a hole in the net! Only unity will heal the tear in any church.

4. Division will publicize the membership of the church in its witness to the community.

“My brothers, some from Chloe’s household have informed me that there are quarrels among you” (1 Corinthians 1:11). Whether we like it or not, there is no such thing as an isolated church. We often hear from television news about churches, ministers, or priests who are being questioned, criticized, or evaluated. The communities where our churches minister know more about us than we realize and could have opinions that we might not be pleased with. Or, they could have very positive opinions of us and our ministry to them.

When a church is united and actively trying to serve a community and people’s lives are being changed, the word gets out. Also, when a church is arguing, lacking love, or not focusing on serving people, the word gets out too.

Church division will scandalize the membership by

• Perverse activity—activity that is destructive instead of constructive

• Adverse publicity—publicity that the church does not practice what they preach.

The King James Version of the Bible interprets quarrels as contentions: “There are contentions among you” (1 Corinthians 1:11). Contentions are ugly manifestations of the flesh, angry emotions, and quarreling among the members.

Paul gave the Galatian church definitions of how the flesh will operate when it is out of control. He uses the following words:

• Discord: In the Greek, eris —quarreling, antagonism, a struggle for superiority

• Dissensions: In the Greek, dichostasia —divisive teachings not supported by God’s Word

• Factions: In the Greek, hairesis —selfish groups or cliques that destroy the unity of the church

The fruit of the flesh is what Satan wants to encourage in every church. He wants to cause discord, dissensions, and factions. This is the opposite of what the Holy Spirit desires for Christian churches. When a person or group in a particular church behaves like the enemy desires, they become part of Satan’s will. When a person or group in a church behaves like the Holy Spirit desires, they become part of God’s will.

5. Divisions will polarize the leadership and membership in its witness to the community.

“What I mean is this: one of you says, ‘I follow Paul,’ another, ‘I follow Apollos,’ another, ‘I follow Cephas,’ still another, ‘I follow Christ’” (1 Corinthians 1:12). Divisions in a church often mean that the people’s eyes have been turned away from Jesus to a human. Paul gives four options that people were choosing.

• Those who followed Paul: The freedom party

Paul was well-known in that day for preaching Christian freedom and the end of legalism. Probably the majority of those who were for Paul were Gentiles, as his divine call was to the Gentile world.

• Those who followed Apollos: The intellectual party

Luke introduces Apollos in Acts 18:24 as a man who was “mighty in Scripture.” He was an eloquent Jew from the city of Alexandria. He was probably a product of the schools of that city, and he is often viewed as a master of the intellectual approach.

• Those who followed Cephas: Likely the Jewish Christians

Cephas is the Jewish form for Peter’s name. The people who preferred Peter were most likely Jews. Many of them believed that new Christians should observe the Jewish law. They would have believed that Peter superceded the apostle Paul. Galatians 2:11 informs us that Paul confronted Peter over an issue “to his face.” It is likely that the Corinthian church heard of that confrontation.

• Those who said, “I follow Christ:” The exclusive party

These believers formed a self-righteous sect and claimed they were the only true Christians in Corinth. Their error was not in claiming that they belonged to Christ, but their fault was in behaving as if Christ belonged to them.

Choosing your favorite preacher, pastor, Christian writer, or leader in the church is as common today as it was when Paul wrote this letter. People may debate which evangelist is the greatest, and some Christians argue about which Bible translation is the closest to the original. The harm comes when a person puts his or her personal preferences in the dangerous context of being God’s will. No person is perfect, and no two Bible translations are exactly the same. We are to strive for unity in the body and put people and preferences way down on the list of priorities.

The Cure for Division

The cure for the Corinthian church is the same as the cure for us today.

“Is Christ divided? Was Paul crucified for you? Were you baptized into the name of Paul?” (1 Corinthians 1:13). Why did Paul ask these questions? He was demonstrating the absurdity and mindlessness of lowering the person of the Lord Jesus Christ to the level of human leadership.

• Christ is the head of the total church—worldwide.

• Christ is the head of the local church—in communities.

Only when a church recognizes this can human leadership be authorized and organized. Paul said, “I am thankful that I did not baptize any of you except Crispus and Gaius, so no one can say that you were baptized into my name” (1 Corinthians 1:14).

Paul was not in any way belittling baptism; in fact, those who were baptized were special converts. He was not minimizing the ordinance of baptism or criticizing the style of preaching; he was reminding the people of the real focus. The cure for the Corinthian church was to focus on the message, not the messenger. The Christ into whom a person is baptized is more important than the ordinance itself. The message of the gospel of Jesus Christ is more important than the method (style) of delivery.

**Q:** Explain the impact of division on a local church’s community.

**A:** a) New members b) Lay leader c) Staff and leadership d) Family

**Q:** What division is evident in the church at Corinth?

**A:** a) Lack of finances b) Personality issues c) Differing opinions d) Lack of communication

**Q:** How did Paul address the divisions in the church at Corinth?

**A:** MIN281 Conflict Management for Church Leaders, Second Edition UNIT PROGRESS EVALUATION 1 UNIT PROGRESS EVALUATION 2

**Q:** What mindset must the pastor or church leader have when confronting evidence of the enemy’s attack?

**A:** a) The church generally does not reflect the culture around it. b) When a large business moves out of town, the church is rarely affected by it. c) When a new business comes to town, church people readily accept the changes that new people bring. d) The Christian church generally reflects the culture far more than it resists or transforms it.
