### Pastors Understand How the Enemy Attacks

#### 7.3.1 — Identify and explain key ways that the enemy battles against the church and its leaders.

There is some predictability to the ways the devil attacks. Paul said, “He was not unaware of his schemes” (2 Corinthians 2:11). He knew the ways, the methods, and, at times, the people Satan would use to come against the work of the kingdom. We are to understand this as well. It is naïve to think that we can be a pastor in God’s kingdom and that the leader of the other kingdom will sit back and permit us to do whatever we wish. This has never been his way of operating, and it certainly will not start with us.

Here are some basic truths about pastoring:

• Some pastors or churches will not want to work with you, but you must work with pastors and churches anyway.

• Some people will try to oppose you because you are a threat to their power base, but we must work with people anyway.

• Some leaders in the church will think they can do a better job than you, but we must work with leaders anyway.

• Your work will involve stress and hard work, but you must find a way to work smarter and deal with the stress anyway.

• Attacks will come against your family, but you must put your family above the work of the ministry as a good example anyway.

• Attacks will come against your faith, your prayers, and your vision, but you must grow in your trust and spiritual vision anyway.

• The enemy is real, and some people will try to fight the church as we attempt to reach our communities for Christ, but we must fight anyway.

Nehemiah understood that rebuilding the wall around the city he loved would be a tremendous physical task, and he also knew it would be a spiritual and emotional battle. He walked into his plan, knowing that it would have tremendous opposition.

Understanding Opposition

When Nehemiah began his tremendous building project, the enemy began his opposition. Times will come when the enemy will attack or when people will try to discourage a leader from his or her dream. However, the real battle begins when the dream gets to the stage of implementation. There are some common methods that the dissenting party will use. Ridicule is one of them. Business and leadership books on how to handle our competition are readily available. The world ridicules the church. And the condescension, patronization, and outright persecution that Christians receive is evidence that the enemy does not want God’s people to feel good about what they do.

In the case of Nehemiah, opposition came in the form of ridicule (Nehemiah 4:1). Ridicule is effective because it attacks your self-worth. Also, in Nehemiah’s situation, it was anger that motivated the opposition: “He became angry and was greatly incensed” (Nehemiah 4:1).

The person who chooses to bring ridicule or use misinformation, labels, or lies against you is motivated by a root cause. Usually the person is afraid. Afraid that he or she will lose something and that you are going to succeed. He or she wonders what that will mean to him or her. In Nehemiah’s case, those who attacked used the following:

• Name-calling (labels): “Those feeble Jews” (Nehemiah 4:2)

• They overstated (exaggerated) the case: “Will they finish in a day?” (4:2)

• Got other people involved: “Sanballat . . . the associates . . . Tobia” (4:1–3)

As pastors, we must understand that there will always be people who ridicule us if someone else will take the lead. Cowards seldom do things on their own. Small group ridicule is to be expected.

Nehemiah’s opposition used organized-resistance. When plans start coming together, the ones who were verbally ridiculing now become more organized and try to stop the project(s). In the case of Nehemiah, “They all plotted together to come and fight against Jerusalem and stir up trouble against it” (Nehemiah 4:8).

People will try to get someone or a group to agree with them; they will seek out people who will consider the possibility of what they are saying. Then they will organize and strategize a plan of attack. Sometimes their organization and secret meetings will be cloaked with the statement “we are just praying together,” when very little (if any) prayer is going on. If prayer is going on, it is likely the kind that James (4:3) speaks of when the motives are wrong, and there will be no answer from God.

False rumors were spread against Nehemiah. Someone once said to me, “When you move to a new pastorate, be careful of those who help you unpack the moving van.” He meant that those who are closest to us can be the ones to bring harm to us or to the ministry.

False rumors can be circulated by those closest to the enemy. “Then the Jews who lived near them came and told us ten times over, “Wherever you turn, they will attack us” (Nehemiah 4:12).

One of the things I love about pastoring is watching lives changed, marriages healed, and people make the decision to become part of God’s wonderful kingdom. However, I believe we need to be cautious in choosing the people with whom we will spend a lot of time. Negative, suspicious, and power-hungry people often become fearful because they might lose some of the control.

In Nehemiah’s case, the disruptors tried to stir up trouble by spreading false rumors and misinformation (Nehemiah 4:11–12).

The quickest way to feed on false rumor is to feed on people’s fears. When false rumor is repeated—the story grows and gets a life of its own. They “told us ten times over” (v. 12). When a rumor is exaggerated again and again, people often start believing it.

Pastors should be on their guard, not buying into rumors. They can ponder them, but they should never be pulled into them. The potential of people attacking is great if we let false rumor and organized resistance continue. It can stop our dreams, keep the church from growing, prevent us from reaching the community we serve, and even affect our families. We cannot permit the opposition to overwhelm us as we are serving to the best of our ability.

For leaders and people working with leaders, discouragement is the most common emotional response to rumor, opposition, and threats.

“Then the people of Judah began to complain that the workers were becoming tired. There was so much rubble to be moved that we could not get it done by ourselves. Meanwhile, our enemies were saying, ‘Before they know what’s happening, we will swoop down on them and kill them and end their work’” (Nehemiah 4:10–11).

In Nehemiah’s circumstance he had four causes for discouragement. We can suffer these same discouragements. There is normally more than one event or person to discourage us as pastors. Here are four possible causes of discouragement (for Nehemiah and for us):

1. We are tired: “The workers became tired.” Not taking a day of rest, or a vacation, or not disciplining ourselves to live a balanced life causes us to become fatigued. Discouragement and weariness are common companions.

2. We have clutter in our lives: “There was so much rubble.” When we have too much clutter, such as disorganization, being messy, or not having thought through the details of what we are doing, it can cause discouragement. We need to plan in hours, and sometimes days, to become organized and take care of the details.

3. We feel that we have failed: “We could not get it done by ourselves.” When we feel we have failed in a project, or with a person, we can feel discouraged. Even if we are doing the right thing in the right spirit, we will fail at times. However, the Lord will encourage us, and we will reorganize and begin again.

4. We are afraid: “We will swoop down on them and kill them and end their work.” Being fearful about all of the what if’s or the strategies the enemy can use will add fear and worry, and, in turn, bring discouragement.

How Can We Battle the Enemy—and Maintain Our Focus?

Consider the following practical steps.

1. Trust God

“Hear us, O our God, for we are despised. Turn their insults back on their own heads. Give them over as plunder in a land of captivity. Do not cover up their guilt or blot out their sins from your sight, for they have thrown insults in the face of the builders” (Nehemiah 4:4–5).

We need to be cautious that we do not use the same tactics the enemy is using. When we are attacked, we do not take it out on people; we talk it out with God. The greater the opposition the more we will need to pray. Nehemiah was able to say, “Then our God will fight for us” (Nehemiah 4:20). We will be able to say the same thing. Over the years of pastoring, I have often felt that I did not know what to do. I did not feel I had the expertise, knowledge, or understanding of the situation that was needed to deal with the challenge. However, while I did not neglect working hard to find solutions, I learned to seek the Lord, who has always given answers to each unique situation. He is the Shepherd of the church. I am the under-shepherd. He loves our church more than we do and will give us the information we need, so we can do what He wants us to do.

2. Understand that the threat is real

“But we prayed to our God and guarded the city day and night to protect ourselves” (Nehemiah 4:9).

Jesus taught His disciples to watch and pray. We are to do the prayerful thing while doing the practical thing. If we know a burglar is going to break into our house, we pray and lock the doors. Oliver Cromwell said, “Trust God and keep your powder dry.”

The way we get on the offensive is by prayer and preparation. There will be real threats to the work God has called you to do; however, God is our defender.

3. Know where you are weak—and reinforce those areas

“Therefore I stationed some of the people behind the lowest points of the wall” (Nehemiah 4:13).

Good leadership understands where they are weak and builds support in those areas. Leaders anticipate the objections and are ready for them. Leadership also understands the opposition will notice your advancement, promotional campaign, and courage. The enemy will not like what a dynamic church is doing and will endeavor to slow it down or stop its advancement. However, we do not give up because of the opposition. We choose to pray and prepare for the attack. Most of the time leaders choose to respond in one of three ways:

1. Quit —stop doing what they thought was the right thing

2. Fight —become reactionary and overly aggressive

3. Lead and fight —understand that the enemy would like to prevent them from doing God’s work. However, they keep moving forward steadily to advance God’s kingdom using the spiritual weapons of prayer, faith, and God’s Word.

Spiritual leaders are to build and battle at the same time. Leading is never easy. We are in a spiritual battle, and the work we have been chosen to do is not against “flesh and blood” but against “principalities and powers” (Ephesians 6:12). We should keep our eye on the goal but understand that the competition or opposition (the spiritual enemy) will try to prevent us from going forward.

4. Bring assurance, encouragement, and clear understanding to those you lead

Nehemiah told his workers, “Don’t be afraid of them. Remember the Lord, who is great and awesome, and fight for your brothers, your sons and your daughters, your wives and your homes” (Nehemiah 4:14). Encourage the leaders around you to remember the One who will always assist us. “Remember the Lord, who is great and awesome” as He is the one who will help us overcome any attack of the enemy.

5. Do not quit

Many have discontinued, quit, and walked away from what God has called them to do. The opposition wants to hinder or stop what we are doing in our churches. We gain strength through prayer, through doing our work with diligence, and by not stopping the work until we have finished what God has asked us to do.

“So we continued the work with half the men holding spears, from the first light of dawn till the stars came out. At that time I also said to the people, ‘Have every man and his helper stay inside Jerusalem at night, so they can serve us as guards by night and workmen by day.’ Neither I nor my brothers nor my men nor the guards with me took off our clothes, each had his weapon, even when he went for water” (Nehemiah 4:21–23). Nehemiah understood that the leader is the last to give up, so they refused to stop doing the work of God. One of Satan’s favorite weapons against spiritual leaders has always been discouragement. Persistence is the acid test of leadership.

Review Nehemiah’s eternal lessons on spiritual leadership:

1. Always trust God.

2. Understand that the threat is real.

3. Reinforce your weak spots.

4. Bring assurance and encouragement to the leadership team and your family.

5. Do not quit.

**Q:** What methods of the enemy are seen in the story of Nehemiah’s effort to rebuild the wall around Jerusalem?

**A:** a) Gather information about your specific situation, and believe that personal growth can come through difficulty. b) Seek advice from two spiritually mature members, and assign another member to discreetly poll the congregation for their opinions regarding the conflict. c) Determine to solve the conflict without legal help, and counsel the antagonist causing the conflict. d) Hire an attorney immediately, and when you have done all you can to bring peace, do nothing.

**Q:** What are four causes of discouragement?

**A:** a) He initiates most conflict. b) He encourages the spread of conflict. c) He is not at all involved in conflict. d) He is more involved in conflict today than a generation ago.

**Q:** What is the most essential response to attacks from the enemy?

**A:** a) Relationships, recognition, and responsibility b) Relationships, recognition, and reason c) Relationships, reason, and responsibility d) Reason, recognition, and responsibility

**Q:** How do leaders often respond in times of enemy attack?

**A:** a) Disagreements are a result of varying opinions, not personality issues. b) People personally attack others with differing views. c) People think conflict is abnormal. d) People are defensive.

**Q:** What are five lessons from Nehemiah’s leadership?

**A:** a) He will still effectively lead the church. b) He will need to have frequent meetings. c) He will challenge authority. d) He will lead from a position of fear, constantly being on the defensive.

#### 7.3.2 — Summarize a strategy for dealing with these attacks in a manner that preserves the unity of the body of Christ.

There is some predictability to the ways the devil attacks. Paul said, “He was not unaware of his schemes” (2 Corinthians 2:11). He knew the ways, the methods, and, at times, the people Satan would use to come against the work of the kingdom. We are to understand this as well. It is naïve to think that we can be a pastor in God’s kingdom and that the leader of the other kingdom will sit back and permit us to do whatever we wish. This has never been his way of operating, and it certainly will not start with us.

Here are some basic truths about pastoring:

• Some pastors or churches will not want to work with you, but you must work with pastors and churches anyway.

• Some people will try to oppose you because you are a threat to their power base, but we must work with people anyway.

• Some leaders in the church will think they can do a better job than you, but we must work with leaders anyway.

• Your work will involve stress and hard work, but you must find a way to work smarter and deal with the stress anyway.

• Attacks will come against your family, but you must put your family above the work of the ministry as a good example anyway.

• Attacks will come against your faith, your prayers, and your vision, but you must grow in your trust and spiritual vision anyway.

• The enemy is real, and some people will try to fight the church as we attempt to reach our communities for Christ, but we must fight anyway.

Nehemiah understood that rebuilding the wall around the city he loved would be a tremendous physical task, and he also knew it would be a spiritual and emotional battle. He walked into his plan, knowing that it would have tremendous opposition.

Understanding Opposition

When Nehemiah began his tremendous building project, the enemy began his opposition. Times will come when the enemy will attack or when people will try to discourage a leader from his or her dream. However, the real battle begins when the dream gets to the stage of implementation. There are some common methods that the dissenting party will use. Ridicule is one of them. Business and leadership books on how to handle our competition are readily available. The world ridicules the church. And the condescension, patronization, and outright persecution that Christians receive is evidence that the enemy does not want God’s people to feel good about what they do.

In the case of Nehemiah, opposition came in the form of ridicule (Nehemiah 4:1). Ridicule is effective because it attacks your self-worth. Also, in Nehemiah’s situation, it was anger that motivated the opposition: “He became angry and was greatly incensed” (Nehemiah 4:1).

• They overstated (exaggerated) the case: “Will they finish in a day?” (4:2)

• Got other people involved: “Sanballat . . . the associates . . . Tobia” (4:1–3)

Nehemiah’s opposition used organized-resistance. When plans start coming together, the ones who were verbally ridiculing now become more organized and try to stop the project(s). In the case of Nehemiah, “They all plotted together to come and fight against Jerusalem and stir up trouble against it” (Nehemiah 4:8).

Then they will organize and strategize a plan of attack. Sometimes their organization and secret meetings will be cloaked with the statement “we are just praying together,” when very little (if any) prayer is going on. If prayer is going on, it is likely the kind that James (4:3) speaks of when the motives are wrong, and there will be no answer from God.

False rumors were spread against Nehemiah. Someone once said to me, “When you move to a new pastorate, be careful of those who help you unpack the moving van.” He meant that those who are closest to us can be the ones to bring harm to us or to the ministry.

False rumors can be circulated by those closest to the enemy. “Then the Jews who lived near them came and told us ten times over, “Wherever you turn, they will attack us” (Nehemiah 4:12).

One of the things I love about pastoring is watching lives changed, marriages healed, and people make the decision to become part of God’s wonderful kingdom. However, I believe we need to be cautious in choosing the people with whom we will spend a lot of time. Negative, suspicious, and power-hungry people often become fearful because they might lose some of the control.

In Nehemiah’s case, the disruptors tried to stir up trouble by spreading false rumors and misinformation (Nehemiah 4:11–12).

The quickest way to feed on false rumor is to feed on people’s fears. When false rumor is repeated—the story grows and gets a life of its own. They “told us ten times over” (v. 12). When a rumor is exaggerated again and again, people often start believing it.

We cannot permit the opposition to overwhelm us as we are serving to the best of our ability.

“Then the people of Judah began to complain that the workers were becoming tired. There was so much rubble to be moved that we could not get it done by ourselves. Meanwhile, our enemies were saying, ‘Before they know what’s happening, we will swoop down on them and kill them and end their work’” (Nehemiah 4:10–11).

1. We are tired: “The workers became tired.” Not taking a day of rest, or a vacation, or not disciplining ourselves to live a balanced life causes us to become fatigued. Discouragement and weariness are common companions.
